# Archived log: 2026-07-03 through 2026-09-12

Historical entries; current facts live in topic pages.

## 2026-09-12: cross-session asynchronous log routing, Linux TUI reset domain gate alignment, and packaging consistency

Code review findings from external thread `01a095a5-bf0d-7040-82f1-d349a39074e2` identified and resolved three issues:

1. **Cross-Session Asynchronous Log Routing & Race Condition Fix (F-LOG-ASYNC routing):**
   - *Problem:* In `source/main_data_paths.cpp`, when user paths were cleared or resolved (`clear_service_user_data_path_cache`, `resolve_service_user_data_paths`), `StringCchCopyA(g_debugLogPath, ...)` was called without holding `g_debugLogLock`, causing an unsynchronized data race with the background log writer thread. Furthermore, because asynchronous logging decoupled `debug_log()` enqueue from file writes, any pending queued log records formatted under the SYSTEM profile or a previous user session were subsequently drained by the worker thread to whichever path `g_debugLogPath` currently pointed to, potentially leaking privileged or cross-user log lines into a newly logged-in user's profile log.
   - *Fix:* Enhanced `source/debug_log_queue_policy.h` to pack both a 16-bit payload length and a 16-bit route generation into the existing 4-byte record header (`gc_debug_log_queue::pack_header`, `unpack_payload_bytes`, `unpack_route_generation`, `kMaxRouteSlots = 8`). In `source/main_debug_log_writer.cpp`, implemented `debug_log_set_route_path(const char* path)`, which updates an atomic route generation counter and registers the path in a generation table under `g_debugLogLock`. `debug_log_enqueue()` stamps each record with the active route generation at queue time. During dequeuing and draining, the writer thread resolves the destination path per record and maintains destination handles accordingly. Decoupled path updates in `source/main_data_paths.cpp` (`resolve_data_paths`, `clear_service_user_data_path_cache`, `resolve_service_user_data_paths`) to invoke `debug_log_set_route_path()`. Added regression tests 5193–5197 covering header packing, route generation boundaries, and zero-generation backwards compatibility.
2. **Linux TUI Reset Domain Gate Alignment (`source/linux_tui_mutation_actions.cpp`):**
   - *Problem:* `reset_gpu()` in the Linux TUI checked `(available & SERVICE_MUTATION_DOMAIN_ALL) != SERVICE_MUTATION_DOMAIN_ALL`. This required all 10 mutation domains to be supported simultaneously, blocking Reset on laptop/surfaceless GPUs (where power control is unsupported) and non-Blackwell GPUs (where advanced clock domains like XBAR/SYS/VIDEO are absent), even though the daemon backend was already fixed (F-POWER-RESET) to handle optional domains.
   - *Fix:* Replaced the hardcoded `SERVICE_MUTATION_DOMAIN_ALL` check with `linux_reset_preflight_domains_supported(available)` from `source/linux_transaction.h`. Preflight now requires only core domains (baseline, GPU/memory offsets, VF curve, locked clocks, and fans) while cleanly permitting missing power or advanced clock domains. Added regression tests 4561–4564 in `tests/regression_main.cpp`.
3. **Version Bump & Packaging Consistency (`0.25.2`):**
   - *Problem:* `VERSION` was `0.25.1`, but git tag `0.25.1` already existed on remote, which would cause remote release workflows to reject publish. Additionally, Arch packaging templates (`packaging/arch/PKGBUILD`, `PKGBUILD.bin`) and `README.md` examples were pinned to `0.24.0`.
   - *Fix:* Bumped `VERSION` to `0.25.2`. Updated `packaging/arch/PKGBUILD`, `packaging/arch/PKGBUILD.bin`, and `README.md` to `0.25.2`. Added automated gates in `tools/linux_gates.py` (`check_packaging_version_matches_release`) to verify version alignment across all packaging manifests and documentation on every test run. Updated `CHANGELOG.md` with `0.25.2` release notes.

## 2026-09-12: code review improvements across Linux reset, logging, updater, and build parallelization

Audit of the ~last two weeks of commits (`046fcaa..9fa31ac`) identified and implemented 5 durable improvements:

1. **Linux Reset on Surfaceless/Laptop GPUs (F-POWER-RESET):**
   - *Problem:* When F-POWER-SURFACE was introduced (`40ce12c..3df5285`) to support mobile GPUs where power management limits are refused or unavailable, `linux_backend_apply` handled inert power requests, but `linux_backend_reset` in `source/linux_backend_mutation.cpp` still hardcoded `SERVICE_MUTATION_DOMAIN_POWER` in `resetDomains` and required `g->powerLimitDefaultmW > 0`. On any notebook or surfaceless GPU, calling Reset unconditionally failed preflight with `"Reset preflight failed: every mutable domain must support snapshot and restore"`.
   - *Fix:* Extracted pure helpers `linux_reset_required_mutation_domains()`, `linux_reset_preflight_domains_supported()`, and `linux_reset_phases_for_available_domains()` into `source/linux_transaction.h`. Core domains required for reset are baseline, gpu offset, mem offset, vf curve, lock, and fan. Power is optional: when present, `powerLimitDefaultmW > 0` is required and `LINUX_MUTATION_POWER` is scheduled; when absent, preflight succeeds, power reset is safely skipped, diagnostic log `reset: skipping power reset — board exposes no power control surface` is emitted, and all other domains reset cleanly (matching Windows `service_reset_all` behavior). Added regression tests 4553-4560 in `tests/regression_main.cpp`.
2. **Permanent Start-Lockout on Transient Worker Creation Failure:**
   - *Problem:* In `source/main_debug_log_writer.cpp`, `debug_log_writer_ensure_started()` set `g_debugLogWriterStartGuard` via `InterlockedCompareExchange(&g_debugLogWriterStartGuard, 1, 0)`. If `CreateEventW` or `CreateThread` failed (e.g. transient resource pressure at early boot/process spawn), the function returned without resetting the guard, permanently locking out thread creation on subsequent calls and forcing the process into synchronous disk logging forever.
   - *Fix:* Reset `g_debugLogWriterStartGuard` to 0 via `InterlockedExchange` if `!g_debugLogWriterThread`.
3. **Redundant Staging Buffer & Memcpy in Log Dequeue:**
   - *Problem:* `debug_log_dequeue()` allocated a 1.2 KB `staging` buffer on the stack for every single record dequeued, copied from the ring into `staging`, and then called `StringCchCopyA(out, outSize, staging)`.
   - *Fix:* Streamlined to copy directly from the ring into `out` with bounds checking and null termination, eliminating the redundant stack allocation and double memcpy.
4. **Abandoned Pending Settings Restore Capture on Worker Post Failure:**
   - *Problem:* In `source/gui_update_command_worker.cpp`, if completion allocation failed or `PostMessageA(target, APP_WM_UPDATE_COMMAND_COMPLETE, ...)` failed (e.g. main window handle invalid/closing), the pending settings restore capture was never discarded when `work.command == SERVICE_CMD_INSTALL_UPDATE && work.settingsCaptured`. The orphaned restore file remained on disk to be accidentally restored on a future update.
   - *Fix:* Added `gui_update_discard_pending_restore()` calls to both worker failure branches.
5. **Parallel Multi-Architecture Scratch PDB Collision in `build.py`:**
   - *Problem:* With `ACTIVE_WINDOWS_TOOLCHAIN = "clang-cl"` default on native Windows hosts, building `x64` and `arm64` simultaneously in parallel build jobs linked both architectures using `-pdb:greencurve.pdb` and `-pdb:greencurve-service.pdb` in `SCRIPT_DIR`. The linkers wrote to the same files concurrently, and whichever architecture finished first moved the scratch file via `os.replace`, causing the second architecture to fail with `WinError 2 (The system cannot find the file specified)`.
   - *Fix:* Scoped scratch link PDB names by architecture (`f"{stem}-{arch}{ext}"`) in `_prepare_windows_symbol_paths` and passed to `_compile_and_link_windows_msvc`, preserving distinct scratch paths while keeping sanitized CodeView basenames identical in final PE artifacts.

## 2026-09-12: a disk stall made the service unanswerable, and the GUI called it a lost connection (F-LOG-ASYNC + F-READ-MISS)

Report: "gui lost connection to background service for several seconds a few
moments ago". Reproduced from the user-side `greencurve_debug.txt`; two separate
defects, one proximate and one structural, and both were fixed.

1. **What the log actually says.** At 11:25:48.174 a telemetry handler started
   and reported `elapsedMs=19078 budgetMs=1000 lockWaitMs=0`. `lockWaitMs=0` is
   the key number: the runtime lock was uncontended, so nineteen seconds were
   spent INSIDE the handler. The three requests behind it recorded 15375 /
   10391 / 4735 ms of serialized-dispatch wait, the GUI's 4000 ms telemetry and
   5750 ms full-sync deadlines expired, and the GUI tore its whole presentation
   down and rebuilt it (78 lock checkboxes re-subclassed, ~2 s) with the
   applied-profile indicator dropping to "Manual settings".
2. **It was not the GPU. It was the log write.** Lines timestamped 11:25:48.173
   and 11:25:49.816, from two DIFFERENT service threads -- one of them the main
   loop, which touches no GPU -- were appended to the file only after an
   11:26:07 line. `debug_log()` formats the timestamp BEFORE taking the lock, so
   file order versus timestamp order is direct evidence of who was blocked
   inside the write. Both were queued behind a `debug_log()` stuck in its own
   `WriteFile`/`FlushFileBuffers`.
3. **Corroborated outside the process.** Windows System log, 11:25:50, Volsnap
   error 25: shadow copies of volume C: deleted because the shadow copy storage
   could not grow in time, "reduce the I/O load on the system". The volume
   stalled; only the service noticed, because only the service forces a durable
   write per line (`FILE_FLAG_WRITE_THROUGH` + `FlushFileBuffers()` after EVERY
   line). The GUI kept logging normally throughout -- its handle is write-behind.
4. **This is not a deadline-sizing problem.** Read durations across the whole
   session: n=3958, p50 15 ms, p90 16 ms, p99 47 ms, p99.9 328 ms. Exactly one
   overrun in the file. The 2026-09-11 budgets are correct; a 19 s stall is
   outside any budget worth having.
5. **Root cause 1 (F-LOG-ASYNC): observability was on the critical path.**
   `debug_log()` did the whole file write inline, on the caller's thread, under
   `g_debugLogLock` -- and the service calls it from inside
   `service_handle_telemetry_request()`, which holds the runtime lock AND the
   process-wide serialized dispatch lock. The mechanism that exists to diagnose
   the service could therefore wedge the service, without bound.
   Fix: `source/debug_log_queue_policy.h` (pure ring/framing/overflow rules) plus
   `source/main_debug_log_writer.cpp` (the ring, one writer thread, the crash
   drain). Producing a line is now a bounded memcpy into a 512 KiB byte ring and
   a `SetEvent`. Two locks with a one-way order: the ring lock is held for a
   memcpy and never across I/O; the file lock is held across I/O and a producer
   never takes it. The flush is per drain batch instead of per line.
6. **Overflow drops, it never waits.** Making a full ring block the producer
   would reintroduce the exact coupling. The incoming line is dropped, counted,
   and the writer emits a marker naming the count -- the log stays honest about
   its own gap. `head` is published with an interlocked store only after the
   payload memcpy, which is what makes `debug_log_drain_pending_for_crash()`
   safe to walk with NO lock from the VEH/unhandled/fast-fail paths (the
   crashing thread may hold either lock -- the same constraint `debug_log_veh()`
   has always had). Queued lines therefore still reach the crash breadcrumb.
7. **Root cause 2 (F-READ-MISS): the GUI equated "no answer" with "gone".**
   Independent of 5, and it had to be fixed independently: no deadline can be
   long enough for every stall an OS can produce, so raising deadlines only
   changes how OFTEN this fires, never whether it can. The Linux side already
   got this rule on the same day (item 7 of the entry below); Windows never got
   the counterpart. Fix: `ServiceClientReachability` +
   `service_client_read_miss_preserves_presentation()` in
   `service_request_deadline_policy.h`, recorded by the transport and consumed
   by `source/gui_service_stale_read.cpp`. An opened pipe -- or `ERROR_PIPE_BUSY`,
   the Windows analogue of Linux's `EAGAIN` on a saturated backlog -- is positive
   evidence a service exists, and nothing that fails afterwards may be read as
   its absence. A stale read advances no epoch, invalidates no live authority,
   disconnects no model, and keeps the profile identity; the status line says
   "Background service is busy; showing the last live GPU state."
8. **The Win32 error numbers are spelled as values** in the policy header so the
   contract stays unit-testable on the Linux host, with a `static_assert` in
   `main_service_connection.cpp` pinning them to the real `<winerror.h>` macros.
   Same shape as `linux_daemon_connect_reachability()` naming errno values.
9. **Coverage.** Regressions 5154-5166 (reachability and the exact incident
   shape: request submitted, service connected, response deadline expired) and
   5170-5192 (ring framing, the overflow boundary, wrap spans, and the
   length validation the lock-free crash drain depends on). Source gates in the
   new `tools/log_gates.py` (the producer must not call `WriteFile`,
   `FlushFileBuffers`, `OutputDebugStringA` or `GetFileSizeEx`, and must not take
   the file lock) and `check_read_miss_is_not_a_disconnect()` in
   `tools/ui_gates.py` (the completion path must delegate, and a stale read must
   not reach any of the three teardown steps). Both are negatives that fail
   silently when they regress, which is why they are gates.
10. **Empirically verified both directions.** Dropping `ERROR_PIPE_BUSY` from the
    reachability switch fires regression 5154; making `fits()` forget the framing
    bytes fires 5181. Full `python build.py` green on all four targets, `--test`
    green, `--tidy` reports no new findings (38 baselined).
11. **Two pre-existing breakages fixed on the way.** `python build.py --test` did
    not build on `main`: regression 5116 still called the pre-split
    `linux_daemon_serialization_budget_ms()` with no argument, and the Linux
    recovery-deadline gate matched a comment phrase that line-wrapped, so it
    could never pass. The gate now matches the `static_assert`'s own failure
    message -- a gate anchored on prose is satisfied by prose, which is the same
    lesson recorded for the Windows recovery gate.
12. **Deliberately not done.** The MUTATION completion
    (`ui_mutation_completion.cpp`) still routes a failed Apply/Reset straight to
    `gui_service_handle_transport_failure()`. That teardown is defensible there
    for a reason the read path does not have: an unrecovered mutation may have
    written the GPU, so the displayed values are not merely stale but possibly
    wrong, and a resync is the correct answer. What is NOT right is the wording
    it produces -- `backgroundServiceBroken` yields "Background service needs
    repair" for a service that was only busy. Open question: give the mutation
    lane a resync that does not claim the service is broken.

## 2026-09-12: the Linux transport had ONE timeout for four roles (F-DAEMON-DEADLINE)

Follow-up audit after the Windows F-PIPE-DEADLINE work, prompted by "these
commits did not appear to regress Linux, but I found enough of a timeout smell
that I would do a dedicated Linux pass".

1. **The smell was real and the recovery made it worse.**
   `linux_daemon_transport.cpp` used `GC_DAEMON_IO_TIMEOUT_MS 2000` for all four
   I/O roles, including *the client waiting for an answer*. For a mutation that
   is an order of magnitude short: Windows gives the same operation 20000 ms
   (`SERVICE_APPLY_CLIENT_TIMEOUT_MS`) for strictly less work, while the Linux
   APPLY handler additionally does up to four `fsync`-ed record writes inline
   (`store_daemon_record` / `restore_committed_record`) and a second full
   hardware pass (`linux_backend_restore_snapshot`) on the rollback path.
2. **The operation recovery could not cover the case it exists for.**
   `send_simple()` queries the original operation ID on a transport timeout
   instead of writing the GPU again -- but that query carried the SAME 2000 ms,
   and the daemon is single-threaded: `daemon_serve_until_stopped()` runs
   `handle_request()` to completion under `g_lock` before accepting anything
   else. While the mutation is still running the query cannot be accepted
   either, so it expired too and reported "pending or unknown". User-visible
   result: a **committed** Apply presented as "Apply failed"
   (`linux_tui_actions.cpp`) with `state->dirty` still set and nothing stopping
   a second Apply with a fresh operation ID. The recovery only ever worked for
   the rare lost-response case, which barely happens on a Unix socket.
   The wiki claim that "a client timeout queries the original operation instead
   of issuing another hardware write" was therefore true of the code and false
   of the behaviour. Corrected in `linux-scaffold.md`.
3. **Three more findings in the same file, all unbounded-by-design rather than
   broken:**
   - Prefix and body each took a *fresh* relative timeout, so a "2000 ms" budget
     cost up to 4000 ms in both directions, and a peer that stalled after the
     header bought a second full window against a one-at-a-time server.
   - `client_connect()` set `O_NONBLOCK` **after** `connect()`. A blocking
     AF_UNIX `connect()` against a full listen backlog waits for the daemon to
     drain it with no bound at all -- the one client wait no deadline covered.
   - Nothing recorded turnaround, which is why this could only be argued about.
     The Windows incident was diagnosable because its log carried percentiles.
4. **Fix: `source/linux_daemon_deadline_policy.h`**, the Linux counterpart of
   `service_request_deadline_policy.h`. Per-role daemon budgets compose into
   four command classes; `linux_daemon_send()` derives its deadline from
   `request->command` so no call site names a number. The serialization term is
   deliberately worse than Windows: the Linux accept loop is blocked outright,
   not merely serialized after accept. `service_phase_remaining_ms()` was made
   `constexpr` and is reused rather than reimplemented.
5. **The recovery is now a MUTATION-class wait and it loops**, bounded by
   `linux_daemon_recovery_remaining_ms()` so retrying cannot double the worst
   case. `linux_daemon_recovery_may_retry()` permits another attempt ONLY after
   a deadline expiry against a daemon that owns the socket. That predicate is
   the anti-spin rule, and it is not cosmetic: a saturated backlog fails
   *immediately*, so gating on reachability alone would burn the whole budget in
   a busy spin. Regression 5141 covers exactly that; removing the
   `deadlineExpired` term fires it.
6. **An unrecovered mutation is UNKNOWN, not failed.** The response is stamped
   `SERVICE_OPERATION_OUTCOME_UNKNOWN` + the operation ID, and
   `linux_tui_mutation_actions.cpp` says "Apply/Reset outcome unknown" then
   refreshes. Telling the user a possibly-committed write failed is what invited
   the duplicate Apply the old message warned against in prose.
7. **Reachability**: only a failed `connect()` classified `UNREACHABLE` means
   offline. `EAGAIN` from a non-blocking AF_UNIX `connect()` is returned only
   when the pathname IS a listening socket whose backlog is full, so it is
   positive evidence of a live daemon. `tui_refresh_service()` now keeps
   `serviceOnline`, the draft attachment and the last published state when a
   read misses against a reachable daemon -- otherwise raising the read deadline
   would merely have changed *how often* the Linux shape of the Windows incident
   fired, not whether it could.
8. **Two files were split** to stay inside the source-size ratchet, along seams
   they already had rather than at arbitrary line counts:
   `linux_daemon_client_verbs.cpp` (unprivileged client verbs, out of
   `linux_daemon.cpp` at the client/root seam) and
   `linux_tui_mutation_actions.cpp` ("leaves the process" actions, out of
   `linux_tui_actions.cpp`). Two source gates in `tools/linux_gates.py` were
   repointed at the new files.
9. **Coverage:** regressions 5097-5149 (pure, runs on both hosts) plus native
   socket fixtures 50-64 (absolute shared deadline, non-blocking connect, full
   backlog classified as present-but-busy). `check_daemon_deadlines()` in
   `tools/linux_gates.py` guards the non-pure half, including a `forbid_text` on
   `GC_DAEMON_IO_TIMEOUT_MS` so the one-literal shape cannot return.
10. **Empirically verified both directions.** Reverting the recovery lane to the
    read lane fires the `static_assert` in the policy header at compile time
    (two of them); dropping the `deadlineExpired` term from the retry predicate
    fires regression 5141. Full `python build.py` green on all four targets,
    `--test` green, `--tidy` reports no new findings (38 baselined).
11. **Not verified on hardware.** The budgets are derived from what the handlers
    do, not measured on a live daemon, and the native socket fixtures compile on
    Windows but only *execute* on Linux. `note_exchange_duration()` exists to
    supply the real numbers: it logs a per-class high-water mark once an
    exchange passes half its budget. Open question: whether
    `GC_DAEMON_HARDWARE_WRITE_BUDGET_MS` (6000) and
    `GC_DAEMON_STATE_REFRESH_BUDGET_MS` (2500) match a real RTX 5070 apply.
12. **The same defect existed on Windows, one lane further on, and was fixed
    too.** `service_client_query_operation()` sent `GET_OPERATION_RESULT` with a
    hard-coded 5000 ms while the apply it recovers is allowed 20000. Windows
    does accept and read while dispatching -- unlike Linux -- but the query is
    still dispatched under the SAME serialized lock as the mutation it asks
    about, so a still-running apply made it expire and the client reported
    "Operation ... outcome is unknown ... Do not retry it with a new operation
    ID" for a write that had succeeded. `SERVICE_APPLY_HANDLER_BUDGET_MS` and
    its full rationale moved from `main.cpp` into
    `service_request_deadline_policy.h` (which also dropped `main.cpp` back to
    796 lines, so its dead 801-line ratchet entry was removed), and
    `service_operation_recovery_response_timeout_ms()` is derived from it under
    `static_assert`. Regressions 5150-5153. The source gate matches the CALL,
    not the helper name: the first version passed against a reverted literal
    because the rationale comment beside it mentions the helper.
13. **Deliberately not done.** The TUI Apply still blocks its render thread for
    the mutation budget. Unlike the Windows Updates dialog -- whose 700 ms
    *timer poll* froze a message pump -- this is one explicit user action that
    prints "Applying staged settings..." and renders before it blocks, so
    threading it in would add real complexity for no correctness gain.
    `SERVICE_CMD_RESUME_RESTORE` still carries no operation ID and so has no
    outcome recovery; it is settings-free, idempotent, and sent by a systemd
    unit that does not retry, but it is a genuine asymmetry with APPLY/RESET.

## 2026-09-11: Apply stayed pressable during an apply (F-INFLIGHT-APPLY)

1. **Report:** "during the time when values are applied, the gui controls
   aren't locked, and it is e.g. possible to click apply again while settings
   are still being applied."
2. **Not a regression from the F-PIPE-DEADLINE work.**
   `gui_service_actionability_policy.h` was last touched in `e6ac01b`
   ("v0.21 part 2"); neither `8baaa9a` nor `085eff3` went near actionability or
   `applyInFlight`. Pre-existing since F-INFLIGHT shipped.
3. **Root cause:** `GuiServiceActionability` had no in-flight member at all, so
   `GUI_SERVICE_CAP_HARDWARE_MUTATION` was `editor && loaded` and nothing else.
   `g_app.applyInFlight` drove only presentation -- tray theme, banner, status
   line, tooltip -- so every F-INFLIGHT surface greyed out except the button the
   state is actually about.
4. **Second half of the bug:** even with the gate added, the Apply button's
   enable state is only re-asserted where something asks for it
   (`ui_pending_changes.cpp`), and `gui_apply_in_flight_presentation_changed()`
   refreshed the tray and the service controls but not that. The button would
   have kept whatever it had when the write started -- which is "enabled" --
   until the next unrelated editor change. Both halves were needed.
5. **What was NOT wrong:** the write itself.
   `gui_mutation_queue_decide()` turns a second Apply into a PENDING one that
   replaces any earlier pending Apply, so a double click never produced two
   concurrent hardware writes. What it produced was a second full apply the user
   had not asked for, against an editor baseline the first apply had just moved.
6. **Reset deliberately stays enabled.** It is projected from
   `GUI_SERVICE_CAP_EDITOR`, not from the gated capability, because
   `gui_mutation_queue_decide()` treats a Reset queued behind an active write as
   a safety action a later Apply can neither overtake nor discard. Greying it
   during an apply would remove the one escape hatch from an apply that is going
   badly. Profile Load/Save also stay live -- neither writes hardware.
7. **Coverage:** regression 1605 (extended) plus 1633-1639, including the
   same-state-with-the-write-drained case so the gate is provably the in-flight
   flag and nothing else, and the Reset/queue-precedence exemption. Two source
   gates in `tools/ui_gates.py` for the parts that are not pure: the
   `gui_pending_changes_refresh()` call inside the transition, and the policy
   keeping Reset off the gated capability.
8. **Empirically verified both directions:** reverting the policy term fires
   1605; removing the refresh call fires the source gate. (Note for future
   edits: several GUI sources are CRLF -- a scripted revert that reads with
   `newline='
'` silently matches nothing and produces a false "test still
   passes".)
9. **Not verified live** -- compile-, test- and gate-verified only.

## 2026-09-11 (follow-up): the last synchronous IPC on the GUI message thread

Audit of every synchronous `service_send_request` caller after the deadline fix,
because raising a deadline is only safe where nothing is blocking the UI.

1. **The open question from the first entry was aimed at the wrong callers.**
   `service_client_ping()` and the synchronous `service_client_get_ready_state()`
   are NOT on the message thread: every caller is CLI (`entry.cpp:176/313`,
   `main_settings_transfer.cpp`, `gpu_backend.cpp`'s CLI apply) or one pre-window
   GUI startup probe (`entry.cpp:446`). The freeze objection against raising
   their deadlines did not hold, so they now take the contract too
   (`service_health_probe_response_timeout_ms()` = 3000 ms for a ping;
   `service_client_get_ready_state_contracted()` for the CLI snapshot reads).
2. **The real offender was the Updates dialog.** `WM_TIMER` every 700 ms ran a
   blocking `SERVICE_CMD_GET_UPDATE_STATE` with a 5000 ms deadline from the
   window procedure. Update commands share the ONE serialized dispatch lock with
   `GET_SNAPSHOT` (p90 1140 ms, max 1968 ms measured), so a concurrent snapshot
   froze the whole UI -- main window included, since the dialog is modeless on
   that thread. Its poll interval was also shorter than its own deadline.
3. **The poll was deleted rather than moved.** The service stamps
   `ServiceUpdateState` onto every response and `gui_update_note_response()`
   feeds the shared cache from inside the transport, so the coordinator's 1 Hz
   read already keeps it current. The timer is now a pure repaint. This is what
   `gui_update_client.cpp`'s own header comment always described ("no extra
   round trip, and no second code path that could go stale"); the poll was that
   second code path.
4. **Check / Install / SetPolicy now run on a detached worker**
   (`source/gui_update_command_worker.cpp`), with the completion posted to the
   MAIN window so it can never reach a destroyed dialog HWND. See
   `updates.md` for the full contract.
5. **Instrumentation instead of an argument.** `note_gui_message_thread()`
   records the message-pump thread id and `service_send_request_deadlines()` logs
   `service IPC: BLOCKING the GUI message thread ...` if any request ever runs on
   it. Proving "no synchronous IPC on the UI thread" by reading every transitive
   path is not something that stays true; this makes a reintroduced one loud.
6. **Gate rewiring, not weakening.** Moving the request building into the worker
   shard would have left `tools/update_gates.py`'s payload-free `forbid_text`
   list checking a file that no longer builds a request. The list now covers the
   worker and first-run shards too, plus two new gates: `CreateThread` required
   in the worker, `gui_update_send(` forbidden in the dialog.
7. **Not verified live.** The dialog change is compile- and gate-verified only;
   the user's running instance is the previous build, and exercising the new one
   would mean stopping their live service. Check/Install/auto-check round trips
   and the cold-cache open path remain unexercised on hardware.
8. **Still open:** `service_client_refresh_state_identity()` (5000 ms),
   `hardware_initialize()` -> `read_live_curve_snapshot_settled()` (2000 ms) and
   `refresh_global_state()` (2000 ms) still use caller-picked numbers. They were
   left alone deliberately -- the new BLOCKING log line will say if any of them
   ever runs on the message thread, which is the evidence that would justify
   changing them.

## 2026-09-11: Brief GUI "lost connection" flashes were the client's own deadline (F-PIPE-DEADLINE)

1. **Report:** the GUI window intermittently showed a lost connection to the
   service for a moment and recovered on its own.
2. **Evidence** (`%LOCALAPPDATA%\Green Curve\greencurve_debug.txt`, three
   incidents at 22:37:13, 22:37:25, 22:38:38):
   - `GUI service I/O: read kind=2 reason=telemetry cadence durationMs=500
     success=0 ... error=Timed out during reading service response`
   - immediately followed on the service side by
     `service_pipe_server: response write failed class=normal: Failed starting
     writing service response (error 232)` — ERROR_NO_DATA, i.e. the client had
     already closed the pipe on an answer the service had produced.
   - Distribution over the whole log: 36 711 successful telemetry reads at
     p50 15 ms / p99 47 ms / p99.9 203 ms, and **29 clipped at exactly 500 ms**.
     Full syncs: p50 250 ms, p90 1140 ms, max 1968 ms against their 2000 ms
     deadline — that lane was clipping too.
   - Each incident was preceded by a `service_runtime_pulse` (100–142 ms, fan
     thread holding `g_serviceRuntimeLock`) with telemetry reads already at
     141–203 ms.
3. **Root cause:** `gui_worker_send_state_request()` carried two magic literals
   (500 ms telemetry, 2000 ms full sync) chosen independently of what the
   service is allowed to spend. The service serializes every command under
   `g_serviceDispatchLock` and each read handler may then wait 250 ms for the
   runtime lock and read live hardware — a bound far above 500 ms.
   A second defect amplified it: `gui_worker_record_connection_result()`
   recorded a failed request as `serviceInstanceId = 0`, the same signal as
   "the service restarted", so one missed poll advanced the connection epoch,
   advanced the GPU epoch, wiped the model's revision/generation/topology,
   discarded live authority, dropped the applied-profile indicator to
   "Manual settings", and rebuilt the window — then did it again on recovery.
   That double rebuild is the flash.
4. **Why raising the number alone was not enough:** `service_send_request()`
   had ONE budget covering both "find a reachable pipe" and "wait for the
   answer". Every millisecond granted to a slow answer was also granted to
   spinning on a pipe that does not exist, so a stopped service would have been
   reported that much later. Splitting the two budgets is what made the
   correct deadline affordable.
5. **Fix:** new `source/service_request_deadline_policy.h` derives every client
   deadline from the server-side bound with `static_assert` enforcement;
   `service_send_request_deadlines()` takes `{connectMs, responseMs, totalMs}`;
   the async lane uses `service_send_state_read_request()`; synchronous callers
   keep one total cap and therefore an unchanged worst-case stall; a failed
   request no longer moves the tracked service instance.
6. **Diagnostics added** (none of this was observable before): the service logs
   its runtime-lock wait, a handler-budget overrun with the client deadline
   beside it, and the serialized dispatch-queue wait; the GUI logs
   `deadlineMs=` on every read.
7. **Coverage:** regression codes 5072–5096. Empirically verified to fail
   against the pre-fix behaviour (5072 for the deadline, 5082 for the identity
   rule) and pass after.
8. **Open question / stale-risk:** `service_client_ping()` (500 ms) and the
   synchronous `service_client_get_ready_state(..., 2000, ...)` in
   `refresh_service_snapshot_and_active_desired()` are behind the same
   serialized dispatch lock and are therefore the same bug class. They were
   left alone deliberately: they can run on the UI thread, where a longer
   deadline is a longer window with a frozen window, and they fail into an
   explicit message rather than a connection teardown. No ping failure appears
   anywhere in the incident log. Unverified whether they can clip in practice.
9. **Corrected stale wiki claim:** `windows-architecture.md` still described the
   pipe server as single-instance; it has been a bounded worker pool with a
   serialized dispatch lock since the transition-safe transport work.

## 2026-09-09: Power target with no control surface blocked every Apply (F-POWER-SURFACE)

1. **Report:** 0.25.0 on an RTX 3060 Laptop GPU. Every Apply failed with
   `Reset before apply failed: Power target did not reset`, even with the power
   limit left at default. NVAPI/NVML and VF access were otherwise healthy.
2. **Root cause chain, from the user's log:**
   - `nvml_read_power_limit()` returned early, so `powerLimitCurrentmW`,
     `powerLimitDefaultmW`, `powerLimitMinmW`, `powerLimitMaxmW` were all 0 and
     `powerLimitPct` was **0** (`OC range hints refreshed: ... power 50..150 %
     (from 0/0/0 mW)`, `control state readback validity: gpu=1 mem=1 power=0`).
   - The capability probe still reported power-limit **available**, because it
     only exercised `nvmlDeviceGetPowerManagementLimitConstraints` — which this
     board *does* answer (`min=1000 mW max=130000 mW`).
   - The GUI therefore displayed **0** in the power field and captured 0; in
     service mode `forceExplicitGlobals` makes every capture own the power
     field, so `hasPowerLimit=1 power=0` went out on every Apply.
   - `validate_desired_settings_for_ipc()` clamped that 0 up to the 50% floor —
     visible in the log as `service_apply_desired_settings: ... power=50` from a
     GUI that captured `power=0`. A silent request to halve the board's power.
   - `reset_oc_before_gui_apply()` read `powerLimitPct(0) != 100`, called
     `nvapi_set_power_limit(100)`, which refused at `powerLimitDefaultmW <= 0`
     **without logging anything**, and the whole Apply aborted before the VF
     curve was touched.
3. **The defect is not laptop-specific.** It fires on any board whose driver
   refuses the power *limit* while answering the *constraints*; notebook boards
   whose TGP the OEM/EC owns are simply where that happens. Mobile support was
   not missing — one domain fabricated a value and every consumer believed it.
4. **Fix — never fabricate 0 for an unknown power target:**
   - `POWER_LIMIT_DEFAULT_PCT` (100) in `gpu_core.h` is the unknown/neutral
     value; `power_limit_surface_available()`, `power_limit_pct_from_mw()` and
     `power_reset_before_apply_required()` in `control_readback_policy.h` are
     the one shared, unit-tested rule. `apply_control_readback_validity()` and
     `intent_readback_status.h` now call the same predicate they used to spell
     out inline.
   - `nvml_read_power_limit()` (moved with its write sibling into the new
     `source/gpu_backend_power.cpp` shard) reads the constraints FIRST and
     unconditionally, tolerates a missing `getPowerDefaultLimit` **pointer**,
     accepts either getter alone, logs every failure with `nvml_err_name()`, and
     publishes 100% + `readback.powerLimit=false` when neither answers.
   - `reset_oc_before_gui_apply()` gates the reset write on
     `power_reset_before_apply_required()` and logs the skip.
   - `validate_desired_settings_for_ipc()` maps the 0 sentinel to 100 before
     clamping, matching `normalize_desired_settings_for_ui()`.
   - `nvapi_set_power_limit()` logs all five refusal paths (previously silent).
   - The capability probe classifies POWER from the limit read, so such a board
     reports a PARTIAL surface and the one-time reduced-surface dialog (now
     naming notebook boards, not only Grace/Blackwell) explains it.
   - The GUI power field is disabled when the surface is absent, matching the
     GPU/memory fields' existing range-known gate.
5. **Linux parity:** the same request shape was refused by
   `linux_backend_preflight()` (`power limit cannot be snapshotted and written
   safely`) and by the `unavailableDomains` gate. Since a saved profile always
   carries the mandatory `power_limit_pct` key, every profile apply was blocked
   there too. `linux_power_request_is_inert()` now exempts a board-default
   request on a surfaceless board from both gates and from the write phase.
   **Ownership is deliberately not dropped** on either platform — profile
   equality, the applied-profile tick and active-intent recording are unchanged.
6. **Still loud when it matters:** a request for anything other than 100% on
   such a board fails by name — `Power limit N% cannot be set: this GPU's driver
   does not report a power target (constraints X..Y mW)`.
7. **Open question / stale-risk:** we still do not know *which* NVML call this
   board refuses (`nvmlDeviceGetPowerManagementLimit` vs the default-limit
   getter). The new per-call logging answers it on the next report; the fix does
   not depend on the answer.
8. Regressions 5040-5071 (F-POWER-SURFACE, F-POWER-SENTINEL, F-CAP-POWER) plus
   4756; existing assertion 72 was updated because it pinned the old 0 -> 50
   clamp. Source gates moved to the new `tools/readback_gates.py` (build.py was
   at its size ratchet). `python build.py --test` and the full `python build.py`
   matrix passed.

## 2026-09-07: Linux VRAM offset stored-unit migration (pre-parity overdose guard)

1. **Hazard found after the parity fix (`cfef13e`, unreleased):** pre-fix Linux
   stored `mem_offset_mhz` in NVML effective MHz; the fix reinterprets the field
   as display MHz and doubles on apply, so updating without migration would
   apply a stored +2500 as +5000 effective (2x overdose). Clamp-on-load alone
   did not fix in-range values.
2. **Fix:** one-time halve conversion (trunc toward zero), Linux only, Windows
   untouched. INI via `[meta] linux_mem_migrated` marker
   (`source/linux_profile_mem_migration.h`), daemon records via
   `LINUX_DAEMON_STARTUP_VERSION` 1->2 and `LINUX_DAEMON_RECORD_VERSION` 2->3
   (layouts unchanged; checksum validates over stored bytes before mutation).
   Marker-absent = pre-fix is safe because the parity fix shipped after the
   0.25.0 release.
3. **Key ordering invariant:** halve THEN clamp — +5000 effective must become
   +2500 display (surviving the +/-3000 cap); either wrong order yields 3000 or
   1500. Pinned by regression 5015-5017.
4. **Save-path subtlety:** `save_profile_to_config_path()` must convert the
   whole loaded bank before stamping the marker, or the slots it does not
   rewrite would keep effective units under a display-unit declaration.
5. **Fail-open property:** the conversion is a pure function of the file
   contents, so a failed rewrite just retries the identical conversion on the
   next load; the marker is what stops a second halve.
6. `python build.py --test` and the full build matrix passed; committed locally.
