## 2026-08-18: the cache round-trips, and a false-positive path in the new detector

`update cache: restored decision=2 published=0.23.10 installed=0.23.9` plus
`re-adopted the staged package ... (no re-download needed)`. Both halves, live,
across a service restart and a GUI restart. The longest-standing open question
in [updates.md](../updates.md) is closed; it took three sequential silent
failures to get there.

**Separately, a GitHub propagation lag was mistaken for our bug and is worth
recording.** After publishing 0.23.10, the client received a *validly signed*
0.23.9 manifest from `releases/latest/download/...` for roughly three minutes
after the 0.23.10 manifest was attached (asset uploaded 13:37:40Z, stale answers
through 13:40:55Z). Nothing was corrupt -- GitHub's `latest` alias simply
resolved to the older release's asset for a while.

That is exactly the shape `gc_update_channel_state()` treats as a regression. It
stayed quiet here only because the high-water mark and the advertised version
were equal. A user whose mark was already 0.23.10 and who checked inside such a
window **would** get "the update channel is offering an older release" for a
completely benign cause.

**Open question:** require a regression to persist across two consecutive checks
before warning. Cheap, and it removes the only known benign trigger. Not
implemented -- the alert is informational and fires once per process, so the
blast radius is small, but the false positive is real and now documented rather
than rediscovered.

---

## 2026-08-18: the cache was being deleted by a sweeper, all along

Third and (probably) final cause. `service_cleanup_legacy_programdata()` runs at
service start and deleted **every file** in `%ProgramData%\Green Curve` except
`shared-profiles.ini`. `service_update_restore_from_cache()` runs 24 lines
later. So: written by one service, swept by the next one's startup, then read --
FILE_NOT_FOUND, down the single path in that function that logs nothing, because
a missing cache is the documented ordinary state.

`update cache: restored` has never appeared in a log on any machine at any point
in this feature's life. Three independent bugs, each fixed in turn, each
revealing the next:

1. wrong write scope (caller profile vs machine config) -- `c913ce9`
2. wrong containment root (service LocalAppData vs ProgramData) -- `1ced66d`
3. deleted by the legacy sweeper before it could be read -- `29ac99b`

**The pattern is the lesson.** The sweeper's rule was "delete every file I do not
recognise", which destroys any new machine-scope file by construction, silently.
`machine_dir_policy.h` inverts it to a list of what currently belongs, and the
cache filenames moved there so the writer and the sweeper cannot disagree.

Also fixed a printf format/argument mismatch from `da5ba0a`: the dialog log
gained the channel argument but not its specifier, so `detail='%s'` consumed an
unsigned as a `char*`. It printed `(null)`; another value would have
dereferenced it. **Every `.replace()` in a scripted source edit needs an
assertion on the match count** -- this one silently did nothing while its
sibling edit applied.

---

## 2026-08-18: the blank dialog was never a state problem

`update cache: wrote ... (324 bytes)` -- **the manifest cache stores for the
first time**, two silent failures deep. That half is done.

The blank status text was diagnosed properly at the third report.
`gud_create_controls()` runs from WM_CREATE, dispatched from inside
`CreateWindowExA`, so the `g_updateDialog.hwnd = CreateWindowExA(...)`
assignment has not happened yet and `WM_DESTROY` had memset the struct on the
previous close. `gud_refresh_controls()` opens with a null-handle early-out, so
the initial population was a **no-op on every single open**, and the poll timer
it starts never started -- which is why the polling change in `c913ce9` did not
help. Fixed in `1958087`, gated as source order.

Two lessons worth more than the fix:

- **Twice misdiagnosed as state propagation.** Every log line said the state was
  correct, and it was. The question that resolved it was not "what is in the
  state?" but "is anything reading it?".
- **The change-gated log added to find this was blind to it.** "Same text as
  last time" and "never computed" both produce no line. The dialog now logs its
  open unconditionally. Change-gating is right for a line that fires on a timer
  and wrong for one that answers "did this run at all?".

## 2026-08-17: channel-trust detection, and two process failures worth keeping

Round 2 finished: 0.23 -> 0.23.3 -> 0.23.4 all installed, settings restored on
both hops (legacy-unversioned capture accepted on the first, version-bound on
the second). Two things came out of it beyond the fixes.

**The cache fix was wrong a second time.** `resolve_service_machine_data_dir()`
reads like the machine config directory and returns the service account's
LocalAppData. Neither the unit tests nor the new source gate could see it --
both assert a machine scope is *used* and neither knows what a runtime resolver
returns. Found only because the dialog logging added in the same commit printed
the new error on every check. `1ced66d`.

**A commit was pushed that did not compile.** `python build.py 2>&1 | tail -3 &&
git commit` reports the exit status of `tail`, not of `build.py`, so a Windows
compile failure was invisible; `--test` passed because the harness includes the
policy headers but not the two shards carrying the broken call sites. Fixed in
`5281076`. **Verify a build by its exit code, never by the tail of its output.**

**New: channel-trust detection** (`da5ba0a`, `source/update_channel_policy.h`).
Detects a replayed old manifest (high-water mark regression) and a suppressed
channel (thirty days of failed checks). See [updates.md](../updates.md) for why
it cannot live in the manifest -- the grammar is frozen by 0.23's
unknown-key refusal, which is the most consequential constraint on this feature
and was not written down anywhere before today.

Round 3 staged: `rel-0.23.5`.

## 2026-08-17: the fork test ran, and found four things

Both hops installed. The whole service-side network and install path — WinHTTP,
the redirect chain, the protected staging directory, verify-then-launch, the
GUI stop, silent install, relaunch — ran on real hardware for the first time and
worked. Details and fixes in [updates.md](../updates.md); the summary:

1. **0.23 -> 0.23.1 lost the user's settings.** 0.23 predates the capture's
   version binding, so its capture has no `expected_version` and the new client
   discarded it. Affects every existing 0.23 install; fixable only in the
   *reading* build, which is what was done. Hop 2 (new -> new) restored fine,
   which is precisely why hop 1 had to come from the released tag.
2. **The manifest cache had never written a file**, on any machine, since it
   shipped — the caller-profile containment check applied to a machine-scope
   path. Silent by construction.
3. **The tray gave no unprompted signal.** Balloon added on the alerting edge.
4. **The Updates dialog froze whenever the service was idle.** Now polls while
   open; the exact reported symptom is still unconfirmed, and the dialog now
   logs what it renders so a recurrence is answerable.

Commit `c913ce9`. `python build.py` (all four targets) and `--test` pass;
assertions 4279-4286 and 4400-4419 are new, plus
`check_machine_state_is_machine_scoped`.

The pattern across every live round of this feature holds again: **four
findings, four silent failures.** Not one produced a crash, a failing test or a
log line anyone had reason to read. Two of them (2 and 4) were features that had
shipped and never once done their job.

Round 2 is staged (0.23.3 and 0.23.4 built) to prove the
legacy restore, the cache round trip across a service restart, and the balloon.

## 2026-08-17: staging a live updater test on a throwaway repo

Goal: exercise the fetch/stage/install path end to end without publishing a
release real 0.23 users would be offered. Built in an untracked throwaway
worktree (details omitted):
detached worktrees at tag `0.23` and at HEAD with `GC_UPDATE_REPO_NAME`
repointed to `green-curve-update-test`, uncommitted; Windows x64 setup
executables for 0.23 (the client under test), 0.23.1 and 0.23.2.

The design point worth keeping is in [updates.md](../updates.md) — the client
must come from the *released tag*, because HEAD→HEAD would only prove the new
code updates itself, and the ~2400-line updater diff since 0.23 is exactly the
part users cannot receive if the shipped client's fetch or install is broken.

Two findings fell out before anything was published:

- **A release-signing precondition failed** (details omitted).
- **The wiki said 0.23 shipped from `6a298aa`; the tag and the GitHub release
  both say `53614ec`.** Corrected. `6a298aa` is the earlier version-bump
  commit, which is presumably how it got written down.

Open: nothing has been published or installed yet — the test itself is still
ahead, and the "install path has never run on real hardware" caveat in
updates.md stands until it does.

## 2026-08-15: a transport seam for the two read loops

Asked whether the fake-transport seam was worth building. Answer: for the
**read** loops yes, for the connection half no — and the reasoning is worth
keeping, because the obvious instinct is to seam the whole fetch.

The connection half's security-critical predicates are already pure and
asserted; what is untested there is the loop that calls them, and refactoring
the code that does TLS and connection handling to reach it is a poor risk trade
on the highest-consequence I/O path in the app. The read loops are different:
`gc_update_stream_asset()` bounds the transfer against the signed manifest
*between the read and the write*, and moving that check later is **completely
invisible** — the download is still refused, the digest still fails, nothing
logs anything odd. It just writes the whole hostile response to disk first.

`update_transport_policy.h` holds both loops behind `available` + `read` (a
one-to-one transcription of the two WinHTTP calls, deliberately not an
improved interface — otherwise the new tests assert new behaviour and the
refactor rests on review alone). Assertions 4370-4394, running on both hosts.

**The assertion that matters is 4384, and it measures the fake sink rather than
the return value.** Mutation-tested: moving the write ahead of the check still
returns `TOO_LARGE`, so a test checking only the return code passes it. Four
other mutations also caught — check removed, short-file check removed, document
truncated instead of refused, stalled read read as end-of-body.

**Expectation-setting recorded in updates.md**: of the seven findings the five
live runs produced, exactly one was in the transport layer, and it lives in
`gc_update_http_read_location()`, below any reasonable seam. This closes a real
hole; it is not the hole this feature keeps falling into. The open items that
are — the manifest cache across a real reboot, and the install path on real
hardware — are still open.

---
## 2026-08-15: the updater found updates and never told anyone

Audit of how an available update reaches the user. The answer was: through one
tray context-menu entry, and nothing else. The main window's Updates button, the
tray icon and the tray tooltip were byte-identical whether the machine was
current or had a verified installer staged and waiting. Worse, `auto_check`
ships `UNSET` and **nothing ever asked**, so a user who never opened the Updates
dialog got no checks at all, ever, and no hint that updates existed.

Full write-up in [updates.md](../updates.md); the shape worth remembering is
that every one of these is a *silence* bug. No crash, no log line, no failing
test, no build error — the code did exactly what it said, and what it said was
not enough. The existing gates all guard against doing something unsafe; nothing
guarded against doing nothing.

**Fixed**

- Three passive surfaces now answer to one policy
  (`update_presentation_policy.h`): the Updates button takes the F-PENDING
  orange outline the fan-curve button already uses, the tray tooltip gains a
  suffix, and the tray menu caption distinguishes installable from
  manual-install. `MANUAL_REQUIRED` had *no* surface outside the dialog despite
  being the case the user is genuinely stranded on. `NO_ASSET` deliberately
  stays quiet — unactionable.
- The tooltip's suffix **wins** the 128-char budget and truncates the base. The
  base duplicates what the window shows; the suffix is the only passive notice
  an update exists.
- `UNSET` became a question. Asked once per machine, from the main-window poll
  tick, gated on a visible+enabled window so a logon tray start is never
  ambushed. A dismissed dialog is not recorded as "no". The default is
  unchanged — nothing checks until a human says yes.
- **The last check's result now survives a restart** (`main_service_update_cache
  .cpp`). It previously did not, while `last_check` *did*, so a reboot five
  minutes after a successful check left the machine showing no update anywhere
  until a full interval elapsed — and re-downloading the verified installer that
  was already staged. The cache stores the signed manifest and its signature
  verbatim and the restore re-runs verify → parse → decide; the decision is
  **recomputed**, never restored, which is what makes it self-correcting after
  an install.
- Explorer-restart tray icon: `TaskbarCreated` re-added the icon only when the
  window was hidden, so an Explorer crash with the window open silently left the
  app with no tray icon (and therefore no update surface) until the next
  minimize. Startup adds it unconditionally; now so does this.
- `gc_update_split_timestamp`/`join` extracted from the Windows shard. The
  2038 case — a low half that no longer fits a signed int — was correct but
  unreachable by any test, and its failure mode is an updater that silently
  stops checking.

**Coverage** — assertions 4320-4366, two new source gates
(`check_cache_is_reverified_not_trusted`, `check_update_is_actually_surfaced`).
All mutation-tested. One mutation **not** caught and now recorded as an open
question: `if (false && verify(...))` preserves textual order, so the
`require_order` gates pass. That limitation is inherent to the structural
approach and predates this work.

**Split**: `ui_main_context_menus.cpp` out of `ui_main_window.cpp`
(1289 → 984). Source guards address the two as one surface via the new
`build_state.concatenated_gate_surface()` — the alternative, re-pointing each
guard by hand, risks a guard that passes while looking at the wrong file.

---

## 2026-08-15: second updater audit — install-failure paths; 0.23.1

Re-audited the updater end to end after the 4985fd7 hardening. Six defects, all
in failure paths (the happy path was and is correct), plus two design
compromises that a first pass had wrongly accepted as inherent trade-offs.

**Defects fixed**

- `main_service_update_worker.cpp`: the install reservation was released only
  when the installer had been *observed to exit*, so a setup process that
  overran `GC_UPDATE_INSTALL_TIMEOUT_MS` leaked it for the life of the service —
  fan pulse, all auto-restore, Apply/Reset and controlled restart disabled, with
  the fan thread's heartbeat still looking healthy so the watchdog never
  recovered it. Now: terminate the overrunner, release once the kill is
  confirmed, hold only if the kernel refuses.
- Pre-install enumeration treated a PID that died between the toolhelp snapshot
  and `OpenProcess` as an enumeration failure and aborted the install. Reachable
  in normal use: `greencurve.exe` is also the CLI binary, and the *previous
  update's own* `--apply-settings-file` restore helper runs under that name.
  `ERROR_INVALID_PARAMETER` now means "gone"; `ERROR_ACCESS_DENIED` still fails
  closed.
- A live process we could not name via `QueryFullProcessImageNameW` was silently
  treated as somebody else's — in the one routine whose job is proving it is
  not ours. Now fails closed unless the process has actually exited.
- Toolhelp snapshot handle leaked on the `OpenProcess` failure arm.
- `fail_closed` dereferenced `handles[i]` on the argument-validation path, which
  runs *before* the `!handles` check. Latent null-deref.
- GUI: Install stayed permanently greyed after a failed install (`phase` was
  `FAILED` with the package still staged and verified); recoverable only by
  pressing Check now, which nothing in the UI connected to it.

**Design corrections (previously mis-framed as unavoidable)**

- The "relaunch gap" did not need a service-side session launcher. The real
  problem was two fallible steps — building the command line and encoding it to
  UTF-16 — sitting *after* the destructive GUI stop. Both moved in front; the
  measured count now only selects between two pre-validated strings.
- The abandon timeout was not a tunable trade. See updates.md: the precondition
  is made false by killing the process, not waited out.

**Also**: registry probe moved out of the update state lock (it ran on *every*
service response); restore-discard log now names both versions and the age.

**Coverage added** — `tools/update_gates.py::check_install_failure_recovery`
plus reordered launch gates, and regression block 4301-4310 for patch-release
ordering (`0.23 -> 0.23.1`), which had no coverage: existing tests moved the
minor, and the patch component is the only one that can be *absent*, so it is
the one direction comparing a parsed field against a default. All new gates and
assertions were **mutation-tested** — reverting each fix fails the build (the
patch comparator mutation fails with 4302).

**Split**: `main_service_update_gui_stop.cpp` extracted from
`main_service_update_worker.cpp` (798 → 654 lines) to stay inside the 800-line
ratchet, same reason `..._worker_thread.cpp` was split out previously.

**Deliberately NOT changed**, both reversals of documented decisions:
- Settings are still discarded after a *failed* install (version-bound restore).
  The precise fix is a durable service-side "install launched, did not complete"
  record, letting the old build replay on a positive signal. Not implemented —
  user declined.
- The wizard's `ShellExecuteExW` launch fallback stays removed: it started the
  GUI elevated against its `asInvoker` manifest and would re-add a shell32
  dependency. The wizard is consequently down to one launch mechanism (shell
  token); `WTSQueryUserToken` needs `SE_TCB_NAME`, which an elevated admin does
  not hold, so that arm can never work interactively.

Version bumped `0.23` → `0.23.1` (patch release; `0.23` is already published
with a signed manifest, so its assets must not be replaced with different
bytes — the signed digest would no longer match and every client would download
and then correctly refuse).

Validation: `python build.py --test`, `--check`, and the full `python build.py`
(all six targets packaged) pass.

**Stale-risk / open:** unchanged and still the dominant risk — the install and
restore path has never run end to end on real hardware, and there is no
automated install test (a scripted VM run driving real setup against a real
service, with per-stage log assertions, was proposed and declined). Every fix
above was derived by reading, not by observation.

## 2026-08-14: audit-driven updater hardening (no release bump)

Implemented the findings from the full 16ee0bff..main audit, without changing
the release version:

- Install reservation (`main_service_update_guard.cpp`) acquired under the
  service runtime lock closes the apply-vs-install race; every GPU-write path
  defers while it is set.
- Pending settings restore is bound to the advertised release version and a
  24h freshness window (`update_restore_policy.h`), so a failed install cannot
  replay stale settings at a later startup; the `applying` capture is consumed.
- Checks re-validate a staged package and keep `READY`; a manual check can no
  longer grey out Install for an already-verified package.
- Setup relaunches the authenticated caller session via `--launch-session`,
  and no longer falls back to a SYSTEM environment or elevated launch.
- `ServiceUpdateState` is protocol-validated; high-S ECDSA signatures are
  rejected; signing-key handling was hardened (details omitted); the GUI
  update cache is SRWLOCK-protected; GUI enumeration is fail-closed.
- `verify_tree()` verifies symlink type, link text, target containment, and
  target pinning, with a build-time self-test (`toolchain.run_self_tests()`).

Validation: `python build.py --test`, `--check --target all`, `--fuzz
--fuzz-runs 20000`, `--tidy`, `--verify-toolchain`, update-signer self-tests,
and the full `python build.py` (build 12) all pass.

## 2026-08-14: fourth live finding -- setup runs in session 0; 0.27 released

**Symptom:** the install reached setup, replaced three files, then stopped.

**The log named it:** `stop: no running Green Curve window found`, then
`FAILED: Could not replace ...\greencurve.exe (error 5)`.

**Cause:** the service launches setup, so setup runs in **session 0**, and
window enumeration is session-scoped. `EnumWindows` there cannot see or even
detect a GUI in the user's session, so setup skipped its stop step and then
could not replace a file the GUI still had mapped. Setup's window-based stop is
correct for the interactive case it was written for; it cannot work from
session 0.

**Fix:** the service closes the GUI itself, because only it can. The request
travels the state every GUI already polls (`guiShutdownRequested`), so each GUI
runs its ordinary Exit path -- releasing the tray icon, mutex and service
connection. The service then verifies: it enumerates processes by **full image
path** under the install directory (not by name -- it runs as SYSTEM), waits,
escalates to `TerminateProcess`, and **fails the install closed** if any
survive, because proceeding only reproduces the error-5 failure with a
half-copied directory.

Side effect worth having: the service now knows whether a GUI was running, so
`--launch`/`--no-launch` became a measured decision rather than a hard-coded
guess.

**State left on the test machine by the failure:** `greencurve-service.exe` at
0.26, `greencurve.exe` at 0.25.1, ARP still 0.25.1, service stopped. A
mismatched pair refuses to talk (the ping compares versions), so the app is
non-functional until a manual install restores consistency. **Worth
remembering: a failed install at the extract step leaves a genuinely broken
installation, not a no-op.** Setup's ordering (extract after stop, register
after extract) means the window for this is narrow but real.

**Released 0.27**; local `0.26.1` built for the next test.

---

## 2026-08-14: third live finding -- the installer command line; 0.26 released

**Symptom:** the install appeared to hang on "Installing...", and the version
never changed.

**Evidence that named it:** the staged setup was present and verified, the ARP
entry still said 0.24.1, and there was **no** `greencurve-setup-error.log`
anywhere. That absence was the clue -- setup writes one whenever a *step* fails,
so nothing had failed because nothing had run.

**Cause:** the updater emitted `/D=C:\Program Files\Green Curve` unquoted.
`CommandLineToArgvW` split it, setup took the directory as `C:\Program`, met
`Files\Green` as an unknown switch, and refused the line (exit 3) exactly as it
documents it will. The default install directory contains a space, so this
failed for **every** standard installation. Now `--dir "<path>"`, built by a
pure function in `update_install_policy.h` and round-tripped in the tests
through the real `CommandLineToArgvW` and the real installer parser (4260-4269).

Writing that test also caught the builder leaving a truncated command line in
the buffer on overflow; it now empties it.

**Released 0.26** with the fix, signed manifest published. Tags 0.23-0.25 still
exist on origin even though their releases were deleted, so the workflow's
"tag already exists" gate forces the next number -- worth knowing before
planning a re-release at a used version.

**Deleted releases:** 0.23-0.25 were removed from GitHub to keep users away from
them. Note that no released user copy can reach the updater at all: the only
public release with an updater was 0.23+, and everything at or below 0.22.2 has
no update code. So a published release is only ever discovered by a machine
deliberately running a newer local build.

**Test setup:** local `0.25.1` (fix included) replaced `0.24.1` on the test
machine, to exercise the install against published `0.26`.

**Still unproven:** every installer step. The three findings so far all occurred
*before* setup did any work, so extraction, service re-registration and the
settings restore have still never been reached by the updater.

---

## 2026-08-14: second live run -- manual download gate and Install both wrong

**Symptoms:** a manual check found 0.24 but Install stayed greyed; with
auto-check ticked Install enabled, but pressing it did nothing visible.

**Cause 1 (greyed Install):** the download gate required auto-check ON for ANY
download. Right for the timer, wrong for a button -- pressing Check now IS the
consent the setting exists to infer. `gc_update_download_allowed()` now takes
`userRequested`.

**Cause 2 (nothing happens), two stacked:** the dialog decided whether to poll
from a `phase` fetched *before* the worker ran, saw IDLE, cancelled its timer
and never showed the outcome; and the install was refused by
`SHQueryUserNotificationState()` called from session 0, where a LocalSystem
service has no interactive desktop and `QUNS_BUSY` is a plausible answer on a
completely idle machine. The service now publishes `workerRunning` (set before
the thread is created, so the first response already says busy -- no timing
assumption), and the foreground check moved to the GUI, where it warns rather
than refuses. `applyInFlight` stays service-side; it is the gate that actually
protects the hardware and it is answerable there.

**Pattern across all three live findings** (including the URL buffer): each was
a decision correct in one context and applied in another, and each surfaced as
silence or as a message pointing away from the cause. Unit tests passed
throughout -- they encoded the same assumption the code did. The lesson is that
this feature's failures are not crashes; they are *quiet refusals*, so the
diagnostic value of showing the reason is worth more here than usual.

**Released:** `0.25` with both fixes, signed manifest published. `0.23` and
`0.24` remain and cannot install anything.

**Test setup:** a local `0.24.1` build carrying the fixes replaced the `0.23.1`
one on the test machine, so an installed-but-older *fixed* client can exercise
the full path against the published `0.25`.

---

## 2026-08-14: first live updater run failed; URL buffer too small; 0.24 released

**Symptom:** "update failed: redirect without a usable Location header" on the
first real check.

**Cause, ours:** `releases/latest/download/...` is two hops. The first Location
is 95 characters, the second **945** (SAS parameters + JWT +
response-content-disposition on `release-assets.githubusercontent.com`).
`GC_UPDATE_URL_MAX_CHARS` was 512, so the header was refused as oversized and
reported as absent. Raised to 4096; see [updates.md](../updates.md).

**Why no test caught it:** every URL fixture was hand-written and therefore
short. The real 945-character URL is now a fixture (redacted to same-length
filler), with a 2x-headroom assertion. Setting the constant back to 512 makes
4174 fail -- verified both ways.

**Released:** `0.23` (with the bug) and `0.24` (fixed), both with signed
manifests. A client that cannot fetch cannot be fixed by an update, so every
0.23 install must be replaced by hand.

**Test setup for the next run:** a local `0.23.1` build carrying the fix was
produced and installed by hand, so an installed-but-older *fixed* client exists
to exercise the full path against the published `0.24` -- without spending
another public release on a throwaway version. `VERSION` was restored to `0.24`
afterwards and the tree left clean.

**Still unexercised after this:** the download, the protected staging
directory, verify-then-launch, and the install itself. The redirect handling is
the only part the failed run reached.

---

## 2026-08-14: 0.23 published; updater's fetch path proven, install still untested

**Change:** pushed the four updater commits and ran the Release workflow.
`0.23` exists from commit `6a298aa` with all six targets, attestations, and a
signed `greencurve-update-manifest.txt` + `.sig` uploaded afterwards from the
maintainer's machine per [updates.md](../updates.md).

**Procedure note that matters:** the manifest was built from the *downloaded*
release artifacts, not a local build. The CI setup executables are ~60% larger
than locally-built ones because the Linux host stores the installer payload
instead of compressing it (see [installer.md](../installer.md)), so a manifest
signed over a local build would have described bytes GitHub does not serve, and
every client would have refused the download after fetching it.

**Verified live with `curl`, from the client's own URLs:** both fixed documents
fetch through `releases/latest/download/`, the redirect lands on
`release-assets.githubusercontent.com` (in the allowlist), the signature
verifies over the network-fetched bytes against the key embedded in the binary,
and the asset's size and SHA-256 match the manifest exactly. The manifest
digests were also cross-checked against GitHub's own `.sha256` assets.

**Still unexercised:** everything that will actually do this at runtime --
WinHTTP, the in-code redirect validation, the protected staging directory, the
verify-then-launch sequence, and the install. A locally-built `0.22.2` carrying
the updater is installed on the test machine and should offer `0.23`; that run
is the first real exercise of the service-side path.

**Expect the settings restore to be the weak point.** It has failed on all three
previous live upgrades and is still unconfirmed, so this update may leave the
GPU at stock. Note the applied settings before testing.

## 2026-08-14: in-app updater implemented (phases 1-4); unproven on hardware

**Change:** Green Curve can check GitHub for a newer release, download and
verify the setup executable, and — on an explicit click — run it silently. The
LocalSystem service does all of it; the GUI triggers and displays. New page:
[updates.md](../updates.md).

**The security question that shaped it.** The starting premise was "we have
GitHub attestation, is that enough, and we have no digital signatures". Both
halves needed correcting. Attestation certifies *CI built this from repo X at
commit Y*, so an attacker who can push a commit can run `release.yml` and mint a
genuinely valid attestation for hostile code — forensics, not a gate. The
published `.sha256` files sit in the same release under the same account as the
`.exe` and contribute nothing to authenticity. And a self-managed signing key is
not the same thing as a CA certificate: an updater does not need a CA, it needs
a key the maintainer controls and a public key compiled into the binary. So the
trust root is an ECDSA P-256 key whose private half never enters CI, and TLS,
attestation and the hashes became defense in depth around it.

**Orderings that are the actual property**, both gated as source order because
neither fails visibly when it regresses:
- the manifest signature is verified *before* the manifest is parsed, so the
  parser only ever sees authenticated bytes;
- the staged installer is re-verified through a write/delete-denying handle that
  is held across `CreateProcessW`, closing the TOCTOU window in a SYSTEM
  process.

**The downgrade refusal is load-bearing and easy to miss.** A replayed older
release is genuinely signed, genuinely attested and correctly hashed; nothing
but the version comparison rejects it.

**Rejected:** verifying Sigstore bundles in-process (DSSE + X.509 to Fulcio +
Rekor inclusion proof against the log timestamp = a large untrusted-input parser
in the highest-consequence path, for a guarantee the signing key dominates); and
the GitHub API (a JSON parser running *before* any signature check, plus a
60/hour shared rate limit). `releases/latest/download/<fixed name>` avoids both
and excludes drafts/pre-releases by GitHub's own definition of "latest".

**Protocol v19** adds four commands and a `ServiceUpdateState` stamped onto every
response. The commands are triggers, never payloads: the whole client-supplied
surface is two bounded integers, and the validator refuses a path, settings, a
GPU or a profile identity on any of them, because a command that accepted "which
file" from an unprivileged GUI would be a local privilege escalation.

**Files:** `source/update_{version,manifest,url,schedule}_policy.h`,
`source/update_verify_keys.h`, `source/service_protocol_update.h`,
`source/main_service_update_{state,fetch,verify,worker,commands}.cpp`,
`source/gui_update_{client,dialog}.cpp`, `tools/update_signing.py`,
`tools/update_gates.py`, plus protocol/GUI/tray wiring.

**Size ratchets:** five files were at or over their limits, so per the project
rule things were split rather than ratcheted — `ServiceUpdatePhase`/
`ServiceUpdateState` to `service_protocol_update.h`,
`validate_service_snapshot_for_ipc()` into `service_protocol_validation.h`
(where it belonged by name anyway), the `GET_TELEMETRY` handler to
`main_service_snapshot_request.cpp` beside the snapshot handler it mirrors, the
update handlers to their own shard, and `APP_LICENSE_TEXT` to
`app_license_text.h`.

**Verification:** `python build.py --test`, `--check --target all --arch x64`,
and 20,000 runs of the new `update_manifest` fuzz target pass. Assertions
4100-4259. The signer/verifier known-answer test (4230-4249) proves the pure-
Python RFC 6979 signer and the Windows CNG verifier agree on curve, hash,
encoding and byte order — a disagreement there would be invisible until every
published update was refused in the field. It was confirmed live by corrupting
the fixture signature and observing 4233 fail.

**Stale-risk / not done:** nothing has run against a real GitHub release or a
real install. The install path depends on the installer's settings transfer,
which [installer.md](../installer.md) records as having **failed on all three
live upgrade runs** and still unconfirmed — so an update today would stop the
service (returning the GPU to stock) and might not restore settings. Auto-check
therefore ships UNSET, and the feature should be treated as unproven until that
is closed. `SHQueryUserNotificationState` from a LocalSystem service across
sessions is also unverified.

## 2026-08-02: tray OC/fan class treats an active lock as OC (build 549)

**Report:** a profile with pinned clocks and a custom fan (profile 3: hard
547 MHz pin + custom fan curve) was classed as "Custom Fan" in the tray icon
theme and hover tooltip, because the OC detector only looked at offsets, power
limit, and VF curve deltas -- a hard NVML pin owns the clocks without writing
any of those.

**Fix:** the tray's live-state OC classification is now the pure
`gui_tray_live_state_has_custom_oc()` rule, which also returns true when an
applied lock/pin is active (`appliedLockMode != NONE` with a resolved
frequency). Profile 3 now reads as **OC + Custom Fan** and uses the
`TRAY_ICON_STATE_OC_FAN` theme. The live-state helpers moved to
`tray_presentation.cpp` (keeping `main_fan_runtime.cpp` under its size
ratchet), with source gates pinning the pure rule and the lock-aware check.

**Files:** `source/gui_apply_in_flight_policy.h`,
`source/tray_presentation.cpp`, `source/main_fan_runtime.cpp`,
`tests/regression_main.cpp`, `tools/ui_gates.py`, `build.py`,
`llm-wiki/{windows-ui-layout,testing}.md`.

**Verification:** `python build.py --test` (new cases 4031-4036), `--tidy`
(38 baselined, no new), and a full `python build.py` release/package matrix at
build 549. Not re-watched on hardware.

## 2026-08-02: lock-anchor VF field no longer stuck at the previous profile (build 548)

**Report:** after the ownership-release preview fix (build 547), applying/loading
profile 2 while profile 3 was applied drew the graph correctly at 2957 MHz, but
the lock anchor's VF MHz field still showed the old hard-pin value 547.

**Root cause:** `populate_desired_into_gui()` calls `apply_lock()`, which runs
`gui_pending_changes_refresh()` before the projection records the profile's
lock target in GuiDraft. The refresh resolved the anchor from the stale
previous-profile draft (547) and wrote it back into the anchor field. Later
refreshes skip an absolute anchor (`guiLockTracksAnchor == false`), so the
stale value stayed on screen even though the graph preview was correct.

**Fix:** immediately after `apply_lock()` returns, the projection re-states the
profile's authoritative `lockMHz` in the anchor field, seeds GuiDraft's anchor
entry, and logs the re-state. A new `check_pending_changes` order gate pins the
field re-write after the tracks-anchor assignment.

**Files:** `source/config_profiles_gui_state.cpp`, `tools/ui_gates.py`,
`llm-wiki/{windows-ui-layout,testing}.md`.

**Verification:** `python build.py --test`, `--tidy` (38 baselined, no new),
and a full `python build.py` release/package matrix at build 548. Not
re-watched on hardware.

## 2026-08-02: ownership-release VF preview (build 547)

**Report:** after applying profile 3 (hard 547 MHz pin, flat from point 49),
loading profile 2 (exclude first 70 VF points) left the VF preview silent
below point 70 instead of showing those points return to stock.

**Root cause:** F-PENDING only marked points the loaded editor owned (plus
offset shifts and the pending locked tail). Points the APPLIED state owned
but the loaded profile released were invisible, even though every non-fan-only
GUI Apply runs reset-before-apply and would move them back to stock. The stale
GuiDraft entry from the previous profile made it worse: the preview resolution
treated the draft value as authoritative for those unowned points, so released
points drew at the old applied value instead of the stock curve.

**Fix:** `gui_pending_curve_point_changed()` treats an applied-owned point the
editor no longer owns as pending, and `GuiGraphPreviewInput.releasedToStock`
projects it from `curve_base_khz_for_point()` plus the pending offset
component before any draft value. Applied HARD pins are folded into per-point
ownership by `gui_applied_curve_mhz_for_pending()`, so a pin that stored no
explicit curve entries still releases correctly. The pending refresh logs the
release count.

**Files:** `source/gui_pending_changes_policy.h`,
`source/ui_pending_changes.cpp`, `tests/regression_main.cpp`,
`tools/ui_gates.py`, `llm-wiki/{windows-ui-layout,testing}.md`.

**Verification:** `python build.py --test` (new cases 4024-4030), `--tidy`
(38 baselined, no new), and a full `python build.py` release/package matrix at
build 547. Not re-watched on hardware.

---

## 2026-08-02: exact selective-mode mirror and true grid rounding (build 545)

Two review follow-ups from the pre-ship pass over the latest 15 commits; both
are display/analysis-layer only and do not touch the service or the apply
request construction.

**The GUI's selective-offset mode is now a pure, exact mirror of the
backend.** `gpuPolicyViaCurveBatch` reads the applied exclude count straight
from `current_applied_gpu_offset_excludes_low_points()`, while the GUI helper
re-gated it on `appliedGpuOffsetMHz != 0`. The new
`gui_pending_offset_mode_selective()` in `gui_pending_changes_policy.h` is a
pure, unit-tested rule: the applied side consumes the resolver's effective
exclude count (already offset-gated at every normal source), and the pending
side normalizes like the backend's desired settings (exclude counts count only
while the offset is nonzero). In the remembered-request edge (stale exclude
count with a zero offset) the old GUI would treat the offset as uniform while
the backend would enter the curve batch; the mirror now agrees. Consumers are
unchanged: pending point marking, graph preview projection, and the VF MHz
field sync. Cases 4010-4016.

**The graph axis helpers now round with true floor/ceil semantics.**
`gui_grid_floor()` / `gui_grid_ceil()` in `gui_graph_axis_policy.h` replace
C++ truncating division, whose toward-zero behaviour would step a negative
pre-clamp floor the wrong way. All real inputs (positive data, 500 MHz / 50 mV
grids, clamps at 500/700) render identically; the helpers are pinned with
negative pre-clamp cases 4020-4023 so future callers cannot depend on the
clamps to hide the truncation.

**Verification:** `python build.py --test` (new cases 4010-4016 and 4020-4023),
`python build.py --tidy` (38 baselined, no new findings), and a full
`python build.py` release build/package matrix at build 545 (all six targets,
both setup executables, four archives, hardening and private symbols
verified). The known duplicate llvm-mingw `__guard_check_icall_fptr` linker
warning remains unchanged and the hardening gates pass.

**Files:** `source/gui_pending_changes_policy.h`,
`source/ui_pending_changes.cpp`, `source/gui_graph_axis_policy.h`,
`tests/regression_main.cpp`, `tools/ui_gates.py`, and the wiki pages below.

## 2026-08-02: hard NVML pins are clean, light-blue, and flat from the first VF point

Three Windows GDI GUI pinning fixes from user feedback.

**The redundant post-apply dialog for pins was a severity bug, not a dialog
bug.** `apply_desired_settings_service()` already skipped VF tail verification
for `LOCK_MODE_HARD` (`verify: HARD lock mode — skipping tail verification`),
but the post-apply bookkeeping still fed `tailOff` into
`service_apply_outcome_severity()` as `flattenFailed`. A successful NVML pin
whose VF readback kept the pre-pin shape therefore came back as
`SERVICE_OUTCOME_SEVERITY_WARNING` and F-RESULT raised the OK-box. The backend
now routes through the new pure
`service_apply_outcome_severity_for_lock_mode()` in
`source/service_apply_severity_policy.h`: hard pins ignore tail readback
(NVML min=max is its own verification), while boost-region partials and failed
steps still warn/error.

**The pinned graph is a whole-curve flat line in light blue.**
`GuiGraphPreviewInput.hardPinned` makes the pending preview resolve every
plotted point to the lock target, `gui_pending_mark_locked_tail()` marks the
whole visible curve pending for a hard pin, and
`applied_curve_mhz_for_gui_point()` returns `appliedLockFreq` for every point
while `appliedLockMode == HARD`. Both series use the new `COL_CURVE_PINNED`
(light blue) in that state; default and flatten keep the normal green/orange.

**Verification:** `python build.py --test` (new exit codes 2330-2333 and
4006-4009), full `python build.py` (build 540, all six targets + packages),
and `python build.py --tidy` (38 baselined, no new findings).

**Files:** `source/service_apply_severity_policy.h`,
`source/gpu_backend_apply.cpp`, `source/gui_pending_changes_policy.h`,
`source/ui_pending_changes.cpp`, `source/ui_main_graph.cpp`,
`source/theme_palette.h`, `tests/regression_main.cpp`,
`tools/ui_gates.py`, `build.py`, and the wiki pages above.

**Follow-up:** the first pass accidentally recoloured the applied VF point
markers to match the line. Restored the original red `COL_POINT` centers (the
ring alone follows the curve/pinned colour) and kept pending markers orange.

**Follow-up (Save and tray tick):** Saving a pinned/curved profile no longer
clears the displayed pin. `populate_desired_into_gui()` re-ran live curve
detection through `populate_edits()`, which can only infer FLATTEN from the VF
shape and overwrote `appliedLockMode=HARD`, so the applied curve stayed green
until Refresh re-read the service. It now preserves the applied lock fields
across the projection. The no-edit Save path also used `build_full_live_desired_settings()`
whenever the user had not typed, which intentionally omits lock/curve and made
the saved slot stop matching the active intent (tray tick gone). Save now uses
the GUI capture whenever the editor OR the applied state owns curve/lock intent
(`profile_save_uses_gui_capture()`), and re-syncs the applied-profile metadata
immediately after writing.

**Follow-up (profile 2 tray tick):** the remaining tick loss was the fan
domain. A GUI Apply is a delta and leaves `hasFan=false` when fan did not
change, while saved profiles always carry fan fields, so the strict ownership
comparison cleared the tick for ordinary OC/flatten applies. The post-write
service confirmation and the GUI applied-profile indicator now allow an
unclaimed fan domain (`profile_ownership_policy.h`); the strict pre-write
profile claim check still requires full equality.

## 2026-08-01: final pre-ship review fixes — durable auto restore and proven installer exit

Two targeted correctness fixes from the review of the latest 30 commits; no
refactor or protocol change.

**Linux automatic restore no longer reports a hardware-only success as a
committed success.** `daemon_automatic_restore_write()` previously set
`outcome.success = true` and replaced the in-memory active intent before its
ACTIVE record was stored. A filesystem failure then set `g_stateUncertain`,
but resume still returned `SERVICE_STATUS_OK`/exit 0 and the log said
"committed" while future unattended writes and fan reassertion were disabled.
It now captures a rollback snapshot before writing, commits ACTIVE before
publishing success, and on persistence failure rolls the hardware back, writes
UNCERTAIN, latches the automatic-restore guard, logs both rollback/persistence
results, and returns failure. Source ordering gates would fail on the old
success-before-store sequence.

The explicit recovery edge is transactional too. Apply/Reset used to clear the
in-memory guard and ignore failure to persist it, so the response/snapshot said
re-armed until the old on-disk lockout returned after restart. The runtime now
restores the previous guard when its cleared record cannot commit. The hardware
and active-state mutation remains a successful operation, but its response is
`SERVICE_OUTCOME_SEVERITY_WARNING` with the guard-store error; retries replay
that warning through the existing operation record.

**Installer shutdown accepts proof, not absence of an error branch.** Failed
`EnumWindows`, `WaitForMultipleObjects`, and zero-time per-process probes used
to retain `allExited=true`; setup could replace files without proving the GUI
was gone. Every enumeration/wait result now has an explicit fail-closed arm,
only `WAIT_OBJECT_0` proves exit, timeout escalation remains bounded, and
service/post-termination waits distinguish timeout, API failure, and unexpected
results in their diagnostics. New F-STOP-GUI-ENUMFAIL / WAITFAIL / PROBEFAIL
source gates join the existing open/termination gates.

**Verification:** `python build.py --test`, x64 and ARM64 all-target check
builds, `python build.py --tidy` (38 baselined, no new findings), and the full
`python build.py` release build/package matrix passed at build 539. Windows x64
CFG/CET, both ARM64 BTI/PAC/AUT, private symbols, both setup executables, and
all four archives verified. The known duplicate llvm-mingw
`__guard_check_icall_fptr` linker warning remains unchanged and the hardening
gates pass. Persistence failures and installer wait-API failures are covered by
source contracts, not injected on real hardware/live setup.

**Files:** `source/linux_auto_restore_runtime.h`, `source/linux_daemon.cpp`,
`source/installer_stop.cpp`, `tools/linux_gates.py`,
`tools/installer_build.py`, `llm-wiki/{linux-scaffold,installer,testing}.md`.

---

## 2026-08-01: A Linux host now owns the full release matrix; x64 Linux gained LTO

**Request:** bare `python build.py` on Linux should build Windows as well as
Linux, and the cross-built x64 targets should retain the optimized release
flags rather than becoming second-class artifacts.

**Why deleting the target filter was insufficient.** The cached llvm-mingw
bundle contained Windows-host PE tools (`clang++.exe`, `llvm-rc.exe`, etc.). A
Linux process could not execute them. The matching upstream llvm-mingw 20260519
release has an Ubuntu 22.04 x64 host bundle with the same UCRT/LLVM 22.1.6
target sysroot, so `build.py` now selects that tarball on Linux and pins both its
published archive SHA-256 and the extracted compiler-entry SHA-256. It lives in
`llvm-mingw-linux/`, separate from the Windows `llvm-mingw/` cache.

The target-prefixed entry point is mandatory:
`x86_64-w64-mingw32-clang++` selects `x86_64-w64-windows-gnu`; the bundle's
generic `clang++` selected `x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu` and the first negative run
correctly died on Windows-only `-mguard=cf`. Every helper path is host-native
now (`llvm-rc`, objcopy/strip/readobj/pdbutil and target-prefixed nm). The tar
extractor recreates symlinks/hardlinks only after resolving them inside the
verified extraction root. Generic `llvm-nm` handles both PE and ELF inspection.

`resolve_targets("all")` is now always `["windows", "linux"]`. The Linux host
builds x64 Windows GUI/service with llvm-mingw, ARM64 Windows GUI/service with
Zig, and both Linux architectures with Zig. Resource compilation, PDB/DWARF
verification, PE/ELF hardening, CET/BTI/PAC, exact archive read-back, and both
setup executables all run. Linux has no `cabinet.dll`, so setup uses the
installer's existing byte-verified `METHOD_STORE` fallback; the output is
larger, not weaker or unverified.

**LTO result.** Windows x64 already used `-Oz -flto` plus ICF/CFG. Linux x64 now
keeps `-flto` in both its parallel object compilation and link. The check build
retained split symbols, ELF hardening and 559 `endbr64` sites; the final shipped
binary fell from roughly 511,448 bytes to 477,208 bytes. ARM64 was tested rather
than excluded by assumption: Linux full LTO, Linux ThinLTO, and Windows full
LTO all linked, but every result contained **zero** BTI/PAC/AUT instructions.
The artifact verifier rejected them. Both ARM64 paths therefore remain the
proven `-O2 -fno-lto` object-first build.

**Regression contract.** The build-script suite pins the full default matrix,
the Linux native tar selection, the absence of `.exe` tool paths on Linux,
x64 compile+link LTO, and ARM64 no-LTO. `python build.py --test` passed. A final
bare `python build.py` on Linux passed at build 40: six binaries, four verified
archives, both verified setup executables, PDB/DWARF/private-symbol gates,
Windows x64 CFG, Linux x64 CET, and ARM64 BTI/PAC/AUT.

---

## 2026-08-01: F-PENDING VF preview no longer hides selective GPU-offset moves on an applied profile

**Report:** after applying profile 2 (+475 MHz global GPU offset, exclude first
70 VF points), retyping the offset to +200/+600 or changing the exclude-low
count produced no unapplied preview in the VF curve graph.

**Root cause:** the preview policy treated every `guiCurvePointExplicit` point
as an absolute target that a global offset must not stack onto. An applied
base-plus-offset profile is decoded into explicit absolute curve points, so
after Apply every visible point was "owned" and the offset/exclude preview was
skipped -- even though `gpu_backend_apply.cpp`'s selective path
(`gpuPolicyViaCurveBatch`) re-places every populated point through per-point
deltas and ignores the explicit-absolute rule.

**Fix:** the pending diff and graph preview now distinguish offset modes.
`gui_pending_offset_shift_changed()` keeps the editor-owned carve-out only for a
**uniform** offset (neither side excludes low points); with a **selective**
offset active on either side, owned points are marked and projected through
stock + the pending offset component, matching the curve batch. The VF MHz
fields follow the graph for selective offsets too (the field being typed is
still never rewritten). `sync_vf_curve_field_values()` consults the same
`gui_pending_offset_mode_is_selective()` helper.

**Tests:** regression cases 4000-4004 pin the selective `GuiPendingOffsetShift`
behaviour (offset retype, exclude retype, equal components, locked tail) and
4005 pins `GuiGraphPreviewPoint` projecting an owned point when
`offsetMovesOwnedPoints` is set; existing uniform-offset carve-out cases stay.
Source guards in `tools/ui_gates.py` require the mode split and the field-sync
use. `python build.py --test` + full `python build.py` green (build 537).

**Files:** `source/gui_pending_changes_policy.h`, `source/ui_pending_changes.cpp`,
`source/ui_main.cpp`, `tests/regression_main.cpp`, `tools/ui_gates.py`,
`llm-wiki/windows-ui-layout.md`, `llm-wiki/testing.md`.

---

## 2026-08-01: pre-ship targeted fixes — uncertain-state gate, stop fail-closed, installer fast-click

Three targeted fixes from the pre-ship review of `cba7d63..HEAD` (no
refactors/rewrites), each with regression coverage and source gates.

**Linux: `g_stateUncertain` is now a real gate, and a pre-write failure no
longer poisons the daemon.** The snapshot has always published an uncertain
state as a lockout reason, but `linux_auto_restore_decide()` never consulted
the flag — a resume write could run while the TUI/JSON said "locked out" — and
the "GPU not available" arm of `daemon_automatic_restore_write()` set
`g_stateUncertain = true` even though it wrote nothing. That one transient
resume failure disabled the fan reassertion thread (`!g_stateUncertain` is
required to run it) and published `LOCKOUT_AUTOMATIC_APPLY_FAILED` until an
explicit Apply/Reset, because no automatic path ever cleared the flag.

- `linux_auto_restore_decide()` takes `stateUncertain` and returns the new
  `LINUX_AUTO_RESTORE_DENY_STATE_UNCERTAIN` verdict (a latch outranks it, and
  an exhausted boot is reported as the less specific cause after it).
  `auto_restore_authorize()` passes `g_stateUncertain`, so every automatic
  trigger — including the otherwise unrationed resume — is denied while the
  daemon state is unsettled.
- The pre-write failure arm no longer sets the flag (`F-PREP-NO-UNCERTAIN`):
  a GPU that was merely not ready disturbed no state, the attempt stays spent,
  and the next resume is allowed to try again. `apply_startup_profile_policy()`
  sets the flag only when the failure reached a hardware write
  (`outcome.attempted`).
- Tests 3228-3235 pin the new verdict across all three triggers, the
  latch-precedence, `NO_TRIGGER` precedence, and exhausted-attempt ordering.
  Gates require the flag to reach the decide call and the prep arm to carry
  its no-uncertain marker.

**Installer: the stop steps fail closed when a process handle cannot be
taken.** `gc_stop_gui_processes()` silently skipped processes whose
`OpenProcess` failed and reported success, and a terminated GUI whose exit
wait timed out was still counted as gone; `gc_stop_service()` proceeded
without waiting when the service-process handle could not be opened. Each now
fails the stop step (a PID already gone is the one legitimate service
exception), so "everything stopped" is a fact rather than an assumption before
files are replaced. The stop code moved to a new included shard
`source/installer_stop.cpp` because `installer_apply.cpp` hit the ~800-line
ratchet; the ratchet was not raised. Gates pin all three fail-closed arms
(F-STOP-GUI-OPENFAIL / F-STOP-SVC-OPENFAIL / F-STOP-TERM-WAIT).

**Installer: fast double-clicks on the wizard buttons are no longer
swallowed.** A fast double-click on a `BS_OWNERDRAW` button is delivered by the
real button control as `BN_CLICKED` followed by `BN_DBLCLK` — NOT as a second
`BN_CLICKED`, and the trailing release adds nothing (measured against the real
control and pinned by the extended native fixture). The wizard's
`BN_CLICKED`-only filter dropped the `BN_DBLCLK`, so rapid page navigation
ignored clicks. New pure `source/installer_ui_click_policy.h` accepts
`BN_DBLCLK` on the action buttons (Next/Back/Cancel/Browse) and keeps
checkboxes one-toggle-per-gesture; `installer_ui.cpp` routes every notification
through it, static-asserts the host-neutral codes against winuser.h, and logs
both accepted and suppressed double-click halves. Tests 3236-3239 plus the
full-sequence native fixture; gates require the policy call and forbid the old
filter spelling.

Validation: `python build.py --test` (including installer source gates and the
native owner-draw fixture), `--check --target all --arch x64`, `--tidy` (no new
findings, 38 baselined), and a full `python build.py` at build 535 with both
setup binaries rebuilt and all four archives packaged. Not verified on real
hardware/installer interaction: the fast-click fix is reasoned from the
measured button notification sequence, not from clicking through a live setup
window.

## 2026-07-31: parallel build scheduler + object-first x64; release 0.22.2

`build.py` gained `--jobs N` (default `auto`) with **no flag, artifact, or
gate changes**; `--jobs 1` is the legacy serial build exactly as before.

- Phase 1: the six per-target binaries (Windows GUI/service + Linux, x64 +
  arm64) now run concurrently on one `ThreadPoolExecutor`. Their `dist/`
  folders, PDB/symbol paths and temp outputs were already isolated.
  `generate_icon()` + `compile_resources()` are hoisted to run once before the
  pool, and worker prints are atomic for the duration so logs do not
  interleave.
- Phase 2: each binary parallelizes its own translation units. Windows x64
  compiles the 10 TUs to LTO bitcode objects (`-flto -c`, `-gcodeview` kept)
  then links with the unchanged `WINDOWS_FLAGS` set; Linux x64 compiles the 23
  TUs to `.o` in parallel then links; the ARM64 object loops are parallelized
  with the exact same commands and BTI/PAC link as before.
- One `JobLimiter` semaphore spans every nested pool, so total concurrent
  compiler/linker processes never exceed `--jobs`. `auto` bounds by CPU count
  and ~4 GB RAM per job (7 on this 32 GB / 16-thread host; small CI runners
  stay serial).
- `tools/build_scheduler.py` owns the scheduler and its deterministic
  self-tests; `tools/build_state.py` owns the moved size ratchets,
  fingerprint/version/resource-script builders and `any_newer()`, keeping
  `build.py` at 5223 lines (ratchet 5304).
- Verification: `--test` (incl. scheduler self-tests), Windows/Linux x64
  `--check`, both ARM64 `--check` concurrently, and full `python build.py` at
  build 534. Every hardening gate (PE/ELF, version match, private symbols,
  CET/`endbr64`, BTI/PAC) passed on all six binaries; packaging and both
  installers passed. Installer ARM64 compiles still use the serial object
  path (unchanged).
- `VERSION` 0.22.1 → **0.22.2**; README setup command and wiki current-version
  claims updated; archives/setup now named `greencurve-0.22.2-*`.

---

## 2026-07-31 (newest): The VF graph showed hidden low points and wasted the 700-750 mV cell

Reported as: clock labels of low VF curve points (below the first meaningful
point, around index 49) appear in the graph although those points are
deliberately not shown, and the whole 700-750 mV horizontal cell is empty,
wasting space on the left.

The graph ignored the editor's visibility contract: it scanned all 128 curve
points instead of `visibleMap` (voltage >= 700 mV **and** base frequency >=
500 MHz), and its voltage axis started at a fixed 700 mV.  On GPUs whose curve
only becomes meaningful at 750 mV (e.g. RTX 5070: point 49 = 547 MHz @ 760 mV)
that left an empty 700-750 mV cell and let the first on-curve label sit at the
plot's left edge.

Fix:

- the graph now plots exactly the visible VF list (`g_app.visibleMap`), the
  same points the editor shows, so hidden low points and their labels can
  never appear;
- the voltage axis is data-driven like the frequency axis:
  `gui_graph_voltage_axis()` in `gui_graph_axis_policy.h` rounds the first
  visible voltage - 10 mV down to the 50 mV grid (clamped to 700) and the last
  visible voltage + 10 mV up (clamped to 1250), with a 300 mV minimum span
  that expands downward first; the 50 mV grid lines / 100 mV labels are
  unchanged;
- on-curve MHz labels that would spill left of the plot area are skipped, so
  the first point's label no longer wastes the left margin;
- the axis log line now also carries the voltage range
  (`... voltage %d..%d mV (data %d..%d)`).

Tests 3222-3227 (pure) pin the voltage-axis fallback, normal low/high ends,
the floor clamp, the minimum-span downward expansion, and the 1250 mV cap.
`check_graph_frequency_axis` now also pins the visible-list plotting, the
voltage-axis rule, and the label spill guard.  `--test` / `--check` /
`--tidy` / full build pass at build 532.  **Not confirmed on hardware**: the
left-edge appearance has not been watched on a real GPU.

---

## 2026-07-31: The y-axis "Frequency" title overlapped the top clock label

Nit follow-up on the dynamic axis: the horizontal "Frequency (MHz)" title was
drawn at the top-left (`dp(2), mt - dp(4)`) and ran straight into the top
frequency label (e.g. the new "4000").

The title is now drawn with a rotated font (escapement/orientation 900), so it
runs bottom-to-top in the left margin, vertically centred beside the labels
(start y = `mt + ph/2 + textWidth/2` because rotated text extends upward from
the origin).  It is created per paint and deleted immediately; the horizontal
comment and draw are gone.  Gate: `check_graph_frequency_axis` pins the rotated
font (`900, 900`) and forbids the old horizontal placement.  `--test` /
`--check` / `--tidy` / full build pass at build 531.  Not watched on hardware.

## 2026-07-31: The VF graph clamped high clocks against a fixed 3400 MHz ceiling

Reported as: with very high clocks (a large GPU offset, a high flatten target)
the curve hits the top of the graph / looks truncated above 3000 MHz; the fixed
500..3400 MHz axis also left a lot of empty space for low-clock curves.

The frequency axis is now derived from the plotted data instead of the fixed
band.  `gui_graph_frequency_axis()` in the new pure
`source/gui_graph_axis_policy.h`:

- ceiling = next 500 MHz grid boundary at or above `dataMax + 200` (a 3450 MHz
  curve gets a 4000 MHz top, so the pending/applied curve never touches the
  edge);
- floor = previous 500 MHz boundary at or below `dataMin - 200`, clamped to the
  500 MHz hard floor (a 1400..3000 MHz curve gets a 1000 MHz bottom instead of
  500);
- minimum span 1000 MHz keeps a tight curve from filling the whole plot
  height;
- no plottable data falls back to 500..1500 MHz.

`draw_graph()` scans both series (applied + pending, visible voltage range
only) into the rule and logs the resolved axis change-gated
(`gui graph frequency axis: min..max MHz (data min..max)`), so a reverted
offset or GPU switch is diagnosable without per-tick log spam.  The 500 MHz
grid and labels are unchanged.

Tests 3215-3221 (pure) pin the no-data fallback, the high-clock ceiling, the
minimum span, the floor clamp, the single-point case, very high clocks, and
the raised low floor.  The new `check_graph_frequency_axis` gate in
tools/ui_gates.py pins the derived-axis call, forbids the old
`MAX_FREQ_MHz = 3400` constant, and pins the axis log and the minimum-span
rule.  `--test` / `--check` / `--tidy` / full build pass at build 530.
**Not confirmed on hardware**: the high- and low-clock graph appearances have
not been watched on a real GPU.

---

## 2026-07-31: The VF MHz fields still showed live readback while a global GPU offset was pending

Follow-up on the same report: with a pending global GPU offset and no lock, the
MHz fields turned orange but kept showing the applied/live values — only the
graph projected stock + the pending offset. The first fix had filled only the
disabled tail boxes of an active lock.

Generalized: `sync_vf_curve_field_values()` (renamed from
`sync_locked_tail_field_values()`) now walks every visible VF point and writes
the same value the graph draws:

- unowned points: `pending_curve_mhz_for_gui_point()` — stock + the pending
  offset component while the component moved, live readback when it did not,
  so reverting the offset restores the old numbers;
- the locked region: the resolved flat target.  The flatten anchor joins only
  while it still tracks its anchor and the draft is valid; a retyped
  (absolute) anchor and every owned point keep their field text, so a
  programmatic write can never steal the caret mid-typing.

`populate_edits()` calls the sync unconditionally after its trailing refresh,
so a full render cannot leave the fields behind the pending model even when
the summary is identical.  Gates pin the renamed helper, its use of
`pending_curve_mhz_for_gui_point`, and the unconditional populate_edits hook.
Pure regressions unchanged (3208-3214 still cover the resolution math);
`--test` / `--check` / `--tidy` / full build pass at build 529.  Still not
watched on hardware.

## 2026-07-31: A freshly ticked flatten lock previewed the tail at stock while Apply wrote stock+offset

Reported as: enter +475 MHz global GPU offset, tick the 925 mV lock checkbox to
flatten the curve tail there — the graph preview shows the tail at ~2482 MHz
even though the points are marked pending; Apply writes the correct flat tail
(~2962 MHz) and the graph then looks right.

The preview resolver and Apply disagreed about what the lock target is. Apply
(`capture_gui_desired_settings()`) prefers the draft anchor, then — when the
lock **tracks its anchor** (`guiLockTracksAnchor`) — adds the anchor's
GPU-offset component delta (pending minus applied). The preview's
`gui_pending_lock_target_mhz()` stopped at the raw draft anchor. The draft is
captured from applied/live MHz and typing the offset field never rewrites the
per-point draft values, so a fresh lock at 925 mV previewed the whole tail at
stock even though Apply was about to write stock+offset.

Fix (preview-only; Apply/persistence untouched):

- `gui_pending_lock_target_mhz()` now takes `tracksAnchor` plus the applied and
  pending offset components and mirrors capture: base (draft anchor, else
  `lockedFreq`) plus `(pending - applied)`, clamped to ≥ 1. Equal components or
  `tracksAnchor=false` (an absolute lock) return the base unchanged.
- `ui_pending_changes.cpp` resolves the GPU-offset numbers **before** the lock
  block; `gui_editor_lock_target_mhz(out)` computes the anchor components from
  them, `gui_pending_resolve_graph_preview()` uses the same call, and the new
  `gui_pending_graph_lock_target_mhz()` getter serves the graph headline from
  the same cache — drawn tail, lock diff, and headline cannot disagree.
- The disabled tail MHz boxes now show the resolved flat target (still
  F-PENDING orange until Apply) instead of their previous readback:
  `sync_locked_tail_field_values()` runs in the pending refresh's changed
  branch and at the end of `populate_edits()`'s lock block. The editable
  flatten anchor is never overwritten; a HARD lock's disabled anchor joins the
  tail.
- The `GUI pending changes:` line now logs `lockTarget=` and `tracks=` so a
  future preview-vs-apply divergence is diagnosable from one line.

Tests 3208–3214 (pure) pin the delta math, including the exact regression
(2482 + (475 − 0) → 2957), the absolute-lock and equal-component carve-outs,
the negative clamp to 1, and incremental movement; assertions 1552–1557 were
extended for the new signature. Gates pin the tracks-anchor consultation, the
cached headline getter (renderer must not re-derive), and the tail-field sync
wiring.

Also fixed on request: `tidy-baseline.txt` had been deleted by commit
`a2153f6` ("Remove generated clang-tidy baseline"), which made `--tidy` fail
with 85 findings "not in the baseline" — the ratchet requires the tracked file.
Restored verbatim from `HEAD~1` (38 baselined entries, generated by LLVM
22.1.8); `--tidy` passes again on this host's 22.1.6 with no new findings, and
none of the touched files contribute findings.

`--test` (incl. 3208–3214), `--check`, `--tidy` and a full four-target
`python build.py` pass at build 528. **Not verified on hardware**: the
reporter's +475 MHz / 925 mV gesture has not been re-run on a real GPU.

## 2026-07-31: The VF graph knew the new offset and repainted for the wrong reasons

Reported as: typing into the **global GPU offset** field does not update the VF
curve graph preview; it appears only after clicking **Refresh** once. The
"exclude first *x* VF points" field next to it works instantly.

The pending model was already correct — which is why Refresh "fixed" it.
`refresh_curve()` queues a resync whose completion calls
`invalidate_main_window()`, and the repaint then read a projection that had been
right since the keystroke. The defect was purely in **when the graph is
invalidated**.

`gui_pending_changes_refresh()` gated the graph repaint on `curveOrLockFlipped`:
the `CURVE|LOCK` bits of the domain mask, XOR'd, plus a `memcmp` of the
changed-point **set**. Both describe the pending *presentation* (dashed runs and
orange markers). Neither moves when an edit changes a plotted **value** without
changing which points are pending:

- global offset +100 -> +150: every unowned point shifts, exactly the same set
  is marked;
- an already-pending VF point retyped 2900 -> 2950: same;
- a lock retargeted over the tail: same.

Only the very first keystroke off a zero offset flipped anything (0 -> 1 MHz
adds `GUI_PENDING_CURVE` and marks the set), and a 1 MHz shift is invisible — so
the field read as completely dead. The exclude-low count *does* change the
marked set on every keystroke, which is why one field of the pair looked healthy
and made the bug read as offset-specific.

Fix (structural, not a fourth condition on the gate): the graph no longer
re-derives what to plot.

- `GuiGraphPreviewPoint` / `gui_graph_preview_point()` in
  `gui_pending_changes_policy.h` is a pure record of the **editor half** of a
  plotted point — an absolute MHz the editor asserts, or "stock base + this
  offset component", or neither — resolved in the apply path's order (locked
  tail, moved offset component on an unowned point, draft value).
- `gui_pending_resolve_graph_preview()` fills
  `GuiPendingChanges::preview[VF_NUM_POINTS]` on **every** evaluation path,
  including the clean-editor early returns (a clean editor still projects its
  attached draft onto the graph). It runs last, because the offset numbers it
  reads are resolved mid-diff.
- `pending_curve_mhz_for_gui_point()` is now only the materialisation of that
  record against live readback, and `gui_pending_changes_equal()` compares it.
  What is drawn and when it is drawn are the same value, so a future preview
  input cannot be added to one and forgotten in the other.
  `gui_pending_gpu_offset_projection()` is gone; the graph no longer touches
  `g_app.guiCurvePointExplicit` (gated).

**The live half stays out of the record on purpose.** `g_app.curve` /
`curve_base_khz_for_point()` drift under boost and temperature and the snapshot
path already repaints for them; folding the finished frequency into the gate
would repaint the graph on every telemetry tick — the exact churn
`ui_pending_changes.cpp` was built to avoid. Verified that
`gui_draft_capture_clean_projection()` only re-snapshots on a **full render**
(`needsFullRender` in `gui_service_state.cpp`), not per tick, so the record is
genuinely drift-free.

Fallout the change exposed and closed: the graph now reads a **cached**
projection, so "every editor mutation calls `gui_pending_changes_refresh()`" is
load-bearing. Two lock transitions skipped it — same-point flatten -> hard pin,
and unlock (`unlock_all()` refreshes while still clean, then
`set_gui_state_dirty(true)` re-snapshots `GuiDraft`) — each reachable from both
the checkbox (`ui_lock_checkbox.cpp`) and the right-click lock menu
(`ui_main_window.cpp`). Pre-existing: they already left a stale Apply enable.

Diagnostics: the `GUI pending changes:` line now carries the resolved
applied->pending offset pair, the number of preview points that moved, and the
first moved curve index — enough to tell "the model did not move" from "the
model moved and nothing repainted", which is the distinction that made this bug
hard to see from a log.

(Follow-up on the same day: the disabled tail boxes now show the resolved flat
target instead of their previous readback — see the newest entry above.)

Tests 3195-3207 (pure): the resolution order including the unresolved-lock
fall-through and the owned/tail carve-outs, the three set-identical /
value-moved regressions above, and that equality ignores the struct padding.
Gates: `gui_graph_preview_point_equal` in the policy header, the resolver call
and the widened repaint condition in `ui_pending_changes.cpp`,
`gui_pending_graph_preview(ci)` in the renderer, `g_app.guiCurvePointExplicit`
**forbidden** there, and the refresh hook required in `ui_lock_checkbox.cpp`.

Not verified on hardware: the reporter's original gesture (type into the offset
field, watch the dashed curve follow each digit) has not been re-run on a real
GPU.

## 2026-07-31: The tray menu borrowed the main window's foreground, and the window came with it

Reported as: with the Green Curve window open behind another window, clicking
the tray icon's context menu pulls Green Curve to the front until the menu is
closed.

`show_tray_menu()` did what the shell documentation asks for —
`SetForegroundWindow(hwnd)` before `TrackPopupMenu()`, without which the popup
outlives the click that should dismiss it. The trap is that
`SetForegroundWindow()` **raises** its target, not just focuses it. For the
tray-resident case (the one the tray work was built around) the window is
hidden, so nothing is visible to raise, and the defect never showed up there.

Fix: the shell wants *a* foreground window in the process, not that one. New
`source/gui_tray_menu.cpp` owns a `GreenCurveTrayMenuOwner` — zero sized,
`WS_POPUP`, never shown, `WS_EX_TOOLWINDOW`, no `WS_EX_NOACTIVATE` — created
lazily on the first menu, destroyed with the tray icon in `WM_DESTROY`. Notes:

- **The owner's styles are read back and checked**, not assumed:
  `gui_tray_menu_owner_is_neutral()` in `gui_tray_callback_policy.h` is a pure
  rule (host-neutral booleans, so the Linux-host harness compiles it), and
  `ensure_tray_menu_owner_window()` destroys an owner that fails it. Nothing
  about a window that exists to be invisible should be taken on trust.
- **`WS_EX_NOACTIVATE` is the tempting wrong answer.** It sounds like exactly
  what an invisible helper wants and it reinstates the sticky menu: the window
  is there *to* be activated.
- **A distinct window class is load-bearing.** `activate_existing_instance_window()`
  finds the resident instance with `FindWindowA(APP_CLASS_NAME, nullptr)`; an
  owner sharing that class could be handed a second launch's activation.
- **`TPM_RETURNCMD | TPM_NONOTIFY` + a manual `PostMessageA(hwnd, WM_COMMAND, ...)`**
  keeps every existing menu command handler in `ui_main_window.cpp` untouched;
  the alternative was a forwarding `WM_COMMAND` case on the owner (kept anyway
  as a net, since it only fires if `TPM_RETURNCMD` is ever dropped).
- **Dark-mode ordering matters.** `allow_dark_mode_for_window(owner)` has to run
  *before* `refresh_menu_theme_cache()` (which calls `FlushMenuThemes`), which
  is the order `WM_THEMECHANGED` already uses; the opt-in is re-applied on every
  popup so an OS theme change since creation is picked up.
- **Fallback is a raise, loudly logged.** If the owner cannot be created or is
  not neutral, the menu is tracked against the main window as before: a
  tray-resident instance has no other way to quit, so a stuck menu is worse than
  a raise.
- `main_fan_runtime.cpp` was at its size ratchet (929), which is why this became
  its own shard rather than 60 more lines in place; the ratchet moved down to
  913. `main.cpp` sits exactly at the 800-line soft limit, so the new
  `#include "gui_tray_menu.cpp"` was paid for by dropping the now-redundant
  `show_tray_menu()` forward declaration.

Guard: `check_tray_menu_does_not_raise_the_main_window` in `tools/ui_gates.py`,
verified to fail when `SetForegroundWindow(owner)` is changed back to `hwnd`.
Regressions 1155-1158. Not watched on a real tray right-click — the dismissal
behaviour and the dark palette are reasoned from the identical hidden-window
path the app already ships.
