## 2026-07-31: Every Apply ended in an OK-box, so the box stopped meaning anything

Reported as: applying settings always raises a confirmation the user has to
click away, and the line that *should* say what happened instead reads
`GPU operation started in the background`. Both halves are the same defect —
the outcome was announced in the place you have to dismiss, and the place you
can read said nothing about the outcome.

**The blocker was that "did what was asked" was not a value anywhere.** An apply
can verify every requested value, or it can commit while the driver quietly
declines some of it — a VF point that refuses its selective offset, a flatten
target the tail will not hold. `apply_desired_settings_service()` deliberately
**accepts the live value** for those points rather than failing a whole profile
over one stubborn point, so both cases return `true`, both answer
`SERVICE_STATUS_OK`, and they differ only inside `message`
(`"3 of 8 boost points matched"`). Prose. So "only pop up when it matters" had
nothing to key off, and scraping the message was never an option.

Hence **protocol v18**: `ServiceResponse.outcomeSeverity`
(`SUCCESS`/`WARNING`/`ERROR`). Notes worth keeping:

- **Derived, never assigned per branch.** Both producers resolve it with
  `service_response_resolve_outcome_severity()` at the single point they write
  a response out (`main_service_pipe.cpp` before `service_pipe_write_exact()`,
  the tail of `handle_request()` on Linux). Dozens of handlers set only
  `status` and `break`; per-handler assignment would have shipped a branch that
  forgot. The rule is total (non-OK ⇒ ERROR, OK ⇒ SUCCESS|WARNING) and
  idempotent, which is what lets the mutation guard resolve it earlier, before
  persisting, without conflicting with the later stamp.
- **Validated as a pair.** A response whose severity disagrees with its status
  is damaged, not a lesser answer to trust selectively — checked before the
  payload-free-refusal shortcut so stateless refusals are covered. This forced
  several test fixtures to start stamping the field, which is the point.
- **Retries replay it.** `ServiceOperationRecord` and both persisted operation
  records (Windows `service_operation.bin` v2, Linux `operation.bin` v2) carry
  the severity, because a deduplicated retry after a lost response is answered
  *from* that record. Without it the one client that already lost the original
  answer would be the one told the apply was clean. A v1 file fails its version
  check and is discarded — the pre-existing "no valid persisted result" path.
- **Linux's hardware mutation backend has no partial-verify WARNING class**:
  its phases are transactional. The later 2026-08-01 pre-ship persistence fix
  added one non-hardware warning when explicit Apply/Reset commits but its
  automatic-restore guard cannot be durably re-armed. Only the Windows backend
  reports a partial verify.

Window side (F-RESULT, `gui_mutation_result_policy.h`): a clean success is
confirmed on the profile status line (`Profile 3 applied.`) and raises nothing;
a warning or error still opens the themed box with the service's wording, and
the status line names the outcome ahead of that wording so it survives being
clipped. `gui_mutation_result_severity()` is the gate — a completion the window
could not fully adopt is an ERROR here regardless of the envelope, **including
the zeroed response a transport failure leaves behind**, which carries the
SUCCESS value by default. That rule, not the field, is what stops a lost
transport from reading as a silent success.

Then, mid-change, the second half of the report: **a tray context-menu profile
switch left the status line describing something else entirely.** Same fix,
gated on `service_apply_origin_is_explicit()`: a tray/hotkey pick is a user
action and gets the queued line, the outcome line, and — the case that most
looked broken — `Profile 2 is already applied.` when the idempotency skip means
nothing was written. A rule-driven foreground switch stays silent; it was not
asked for and must not overwrite a line the user is reading. This does not
breach F-PRESENTATION-SILENT: that contract forbids creating a surface and
taking focus, and setting the text of a resident child label does neither.

Two files had to make room under the size ratchet rather than have it raised:
`reset_oc_before_gui_apply()` moved to `gpu_backend_reset_baseline.cpp` and the
three client-requested file writes to `main_service_pipe_file_commands.cpp`,
both included at the position they occupied so the amalgamated ordering is
unchanged. The second one also had to be added to build.py's
`_service_server_surface` concatenation, or the impersonation gates would have
stopped seeing the code they guard.

While in the protocol page: its "Version: 14" had been stale since v15, and its
`resetOcBeforeApply` row claimed the baseline reset also resets the memory
offset. It does not, on purpose — dropping VRAM from a large offset to 0 under
load causes TDRs, so the new profile's memory offset is written directly in the
apply phase. Both corrected.

`--test`, `--tidy` (38 baselined, no new) and a full four-target
`python build.py` pass at build 526. **Not verified on hardware**: the
protocol bump means the running v17 service answers `VERSION_MISMATCH` until it
is reinstalled/restarted, so the new dialog-free path has not been watched on a
real apply. Do that before shipping.

## 2026-07-31: The daemon could stop restoring settings forever and still report a healthy GPU

Found during a pre-ship review of `cc6d8bf..HEAD` (44 commits). Everything else
in that range held up — `--test` and `--tidy` were already clean, and the
destructive surfaces (uninstall deletion, install-target validation, the
signal-handler crash reporter, the redraw-transaction depth counter) read
correctly. This was the one defect worth fixing before the tag. **v0.22.1.**

`linux_daemon_snapshot_runtime.cpp` derived
`ServiceSnapshot::autoRestoreLockoutReason` from `g_stateUncertain`. That flag
is a proxy for exactly one of the two arms that latch the new crash-loop guard:

- a failed unattended write sets both the flag and the guard, so that case
  happened to publish correctly;
- `LINUX_AUTO_RESTORE_DENY_ATTEMPTS_EXHAUSTED` latches the guard **before any
  write is issued**, so nothing set the flag.

The consequence is the bad kind of silent. The lockout is sticky *across boots*
by design, and only an explicit Apply or Reset clears it — so a machine that
tripped the guard once would never restore settings at boot again, while the
TUI, `--status` and the JSON export all reported `LOCKOUT_NONE` and a healthy
GPU. The only evidence was one `dlog` line. Windows has always fed the same
protocol field from its real lockout state via
`service_auto_restore_is_locked_out()`; Linux was the outlier.

Fixed at the source rather than by adding a second flag to the snapshot:

- `linux_auto_restore_published_lockout_reason()` is the pure decision and is
  the only thing the snapshot calls. An uncertain state with a clear guard still
  reports a lockout, because an unsettled rollback genuinely does block
  unattended writes.
- The guard now carries the **reason**, and it is persisted rather than logged
  once: the field is published in every snapshot, so a reason lost across a
  restart would report "an automatic write failed" for a lockout no write ever
  caused. `LINUX_DAEMON_GUARD_VERSION` → 2; a v1 file is read as corrupt and
  fails closed to locked out, which is the safe direction on upgrade and is the
  behaviour that already existed for damaged records.
- Attempts-exhausted latches `LOCKOUT_UNSTABLE_APPLY` (the stored settings did
  not survive repeated replay — the same statement Windows makes when an apply
  fails its proving period), a failed write latches
  `LOCKOUT_AUTOMATIC_APPLY_FAILED`. First cause wins: a later automatic refusal
  is a consequence of the original latch, not a new explanation for it.
- The record is coherent or rejected in both directions, and
  `linux_daemon_guard_initialize()` applies the same rule on the way out, so a
  guard mutated field-by-field cannot write a file the validator would refuse.
- Surfaced in `linux_live_output.cpp` (`Automatic restore:` line + an
  `auto_restore` JSON object), since a latched lockout is the one daemon state
  that makes settings quietly stop applying.
- `tools/linux_gates.py` forbids the old spelling outright and pins the pure
  decision, the record field and the version bump. The pre-existing gate that
  pinned the literal `g_autoRestoreGuard.lockedOut = 1;` for the fail-closed
  path was re-pointed at the policy call that replaced it.

`--test` (assertions `3162`–`3164`, `3177`–`3194`), `--check` on both Linux
arches, and `--tidy` (no new findings, 38 baselined) pass. **Not verified on
hardware** — the crash-loop arm still has never been observed on a real machine.

Also in this bump: `llm-wiki/index.md` described overview.md as "protocol v16"
while `SERVICE_PROTOCOL_VERSION` has been 17 since the resume work. Remaining
v16 references in the pages are historical ("v16 gave that snapshot its own
response member") and are correct as written.

### Answered while here: the Linux setup script is host-independent, and the resume unit is not its job

Asked why `tools/greencurve-setup.sh` contains no suspend/resume items, and
whether the script differs between a Windows-built and a Linux-built archive.

**It does not differ.** It is one tracked file, not generated: `.gitattributes`
pins `*.sh text eol=lf` so a Windows checkout cannot produce CRLF in the working
tree, `stage_release_file(..., normalize=(os_name == "linux"))` rewrites Linux
release text to LF at packaging time regardless of host, and
`release_member_mode()` stamps `0755` into the tarball. `verify_linux_tarball()`
checks both. Byte-identical either way.

**The resume unit is written by the binary, not the script.** `cmd_install`
delegates to `"$BINARY" --service-install`, and `linux_service_install.cpp`
writes *both* `greencurve.service` and `greencurve-resume.service`, then enables
the latter as its own activation step (`LINUX_SERVICE_STEP_ENABLE_RESUME`, split
out precisely so a failure names the right unit). `cmd_uninstall` →
`--service-remove` disables the resume unit first. So standby restore is
installed and removed correctly — the script never mentions it because it never
touches it.

**Real gap, worth a follow-up:** `cmd_status` only reports `$UNIT_NAME`. It says
nothing about `greencurve-resume.service`, which is exactly why the resume
support looked absent. A machine whose resume unit failed to enable would look
identical to a healthy one in `status` output. Not fixed here — it is a
diagnostic addition, not a defect in the shipped behaviour.

---

## 2026-07-31: The crash reporter announced itself by littering, and no exit path could have swept up after it

Reported: `/home/<user>/greencurve/` accumulating 0-byte
`greencurve_crash_<stamp>_pid<N>.txt` files. Three were present — pid 1936
(`sudo ./greencurve --service-install`, root-owned), pids 2254/2266 (the TUI and
the copy it relaunched into konsole). Every one of them a placeholder from a run
that never crashed.

**Root cause.** `linux_crash_report_configure()` *created* the report file at
startup (`O_WRONLY|O_CREAT|O_APPEND`) so the signal handler would only ever need
`write(2)`. The empty placeholder was supposed to be removed by
`linux_crash_report_close()`, whose `if (written == 0) unlink(...)` did exactly
that — **and which nothing in the tree ever called.** `grep` found it only in
its own definition and in `configure()`'s re-arm.

Adding the missing calls was the tempting fix and would have been wrong. `main()`
has ~20 `return` statements, and the paths that leak worst cannot run cleanup at
all: the TUI's terminal relaunch ends in `execve()` (the `O_CLOEXEC` descriptor
closes, `main` never returns), plus `_exit()`, `SIGKILL`, power loss. Any
cleanup-based design leaks by construction; the fix had to remove the thing
being cleaned up.

**Fix.** Defer the `open()` to the handler. Only the *path* is formatted at arm
time — which was always the sole reason for the early open, since `open()` is
async-signal-safe and `snprintf` is not. `gc_crash_report_open_on_demand()` in
`linux_crash_breadcrumb.h` creates the file on the first byte a fatal path
emits, and `gc_signal_safe_emit()` returns early on `length == 0` so a
zero-length emit cannot leave a 0-byte file. The invariant is now structural:
**a report file exists ⇔ a crash wrote bytes into it.** No exit path can forget
it because no exit path is involved.

Consequences worth knowing:

- `linux_set_crash_report_fd()` / `linux_crash_report_raw_fd()` are gone,
  replaced by `linux_set_crash_report_path()` / `linux_crash_report_path()`.
  `linux_main.cpp` arms once for the whole program (unchanged property).
- Deferring the open cost the guarantee that a descriptor was available.
  Descriptor exhaustion is a plausible *cause* of the crash being reported, so
  `gc_crash_report_reserve_slot()` holds a `/dev/null` fd from arm time and
  releases it immediately before the real `open()`. Per-process `EMFILE` is
  covered; system-wide `ENFILE` is not, and cannot be.
- The fd slot gained a sticky `GC_CRASH_REPORT_FD_FAILED` (`-2`) state so a
  dying process does not retry a doomed `open()` per emitted fragment.
- Rotation now counts only real reports. Placeholders had been consuming the
  10-file `GC_CRASH_ARTIFACT_MAX_KEEP` budget — i.e. the bug was actively
  deleting genuine crash evidence, not just making noise.
- `linux_crash_report_close()` survives as the re-arm helper only, and its
  header says correctness does not depend on calling it.
- Arming logs `directory writable`/`NOT writable` from `access(W_OK)`. Purely
  diagnostic: `access()` answers for the real uid and misreads ACLs, so a false
  negative must not disarm a report that would in fact be written.

**Coverage.** New `tests/linux_crash_report_regression.cpp` (native Linux, joins
`linux_transport_regression.cpp`; build.py now loops over both and
cross-compiles them on other hosts). It asserts both directions: arming/re-arming/
closing leave the directory empty, an empty emit creates nothing, a fatal write
creates exactly one `0600` non-empty file and later fragments reuse the same
descriptor, a failed open stays sticky and is cleared by re-arming, rotation
trims to 10 by embedded stamp without touching a foreign file — and, end to end,
a forked child that `raise(SIGSEGV)`s leaves exactly one report containing the
breadcrumb, the phase and the maps block, while children that `execve()` or take
`SIGKILL` leave nothing. All fork/raise/waitpid, so nothing is timing-dependent.

Guards (`tools/linux_gates.py`, F-LNX-CRASH-LAZY): `O_CREAT` **forbidden** in
`linux_crash_report.cpp`; `gc_crash_report_open_on_demand`, the zero-length-emit
early return, `O_CLOEXEC`, the path slot and the reserve slot **required** in
`linux_crash_breadcrumb.h`; plus guards that the fixture asserts all three
directions and that build.py runs it. Verified negatively: reintroducing an
`O_CREAT` open at arm time trips both the source guard and five fixture
assertions independently.

Windows was checked and is unaffected — `main_crash_artifacts.cpp` only ever
creates a dump inside the exception filter, and deletes it if the write fails.

---

## 2026-07-31 (newest): Crash dumps landed where nobody looks, and the two crashes worth having left nothing at all

Asked whether crash-dump creation was actually proper: Windows dumps beside
`config.ini`, Linux dumps beside the binary, real symbols, and whether the app
"crashes honestly" instead of limping on broken functionality.

Honesty was already fine — there is not one `catch (...)` in the tree, the
Windows filter returns `EXCEPTION_EXECUTE_HANDLER`, and Linux re-raises after its
breadcrumb. The one place execution resumes after an access violation is the
service VEH, and only for a fault inside `nvml.dll`/`nvapi64.dll`, after dumping,
followed by a nonce-bound controlled restart. That stayed as-is. Four real gaps
did not.

**1. Fast-fail crashes wrote nothing.** `cfg_glue.cpp`'s CFG violation issues
`int 0x29`, commented *"so the OS can capture a crash dump"* — but `__fastfail`
bypasses the vectored handler *and* `SetUnhandledExceptionFilter` by design, and
nothing in this project registers a WER LocalDumps entry (and the filter
returning `EXCEPTION_EXECUTE_HANDLER` elsewhere means the process has opted out
of WER's own collection anyway). So a corrupted call target — one of the two
highest-signal crashes possible — left no dump, no breadcrumb, nothing. Fixed
with `source/fatal_dump_hook.h`: a null-by-default function pointer invoked
*before* the uncatchable instruction, one-shot because
`__guard_check_icall_fptr` runs before every indirect call including the hook's
own, so an unguarded call would recurse until the stack ran out.
`green_curve_report_fatal_dump()` synthesises an `EXCEPTION_RECORD` from a live
`RtlCaptureContext`, so the dump opens normally in cdb and `.ecxr` lands on the
detecting frame. `__stack_chk_fail` uses the same hook; it *did* reach the filter
before, but only incidentally, via `__debugbreak()` raising a catchable
`STATUS_BREAKPOINT` — which stops working the moment a debugger is attached. A
pointer rather than a direct call because `cfg_glue.cpp`/`ssp_glue.cpp` are also
linked into the setup program, which has no diagnostics at all; a null hook keeps
the installer's behaviour identical.

**2. The dump directory could fall back to the working directory.**
`crash_artifact_data_dir()` ended in `StringCchCopyA(out, outSize, ".")`. That is
`%SystemRoot%\System32` for a service, the install directory for a
shell-launched GUI, an arbitrary folder for a CLI run — and for the service it
also put a SYSTEM-process dump outside the admin-only directory the whole
`%ProgramData%` policy exists to avoid. `set_default_config_path()` already
refuses the analogous fallback for `config.ini` itself; the dump path now does
too. Fail-closed: no resolvable directory means no dump. An unresolved user path
re-derives from `%LOCALAPPDATA%` — environment only, because
`SHGetKnownFolderPath` is COM and not callable from an exception filter.

**3. Rotation would have deleted the wrong file.** Only `greencurve_veh_*.dmp`
was swept; terminal `greencurve_crash_*.dmp` and the append-only breadcrumb were
unbounded, and the GUI's dumps were not rotated at all. Worse, the sweep compared
whole filenames — and `greencurve_crash_` sorts before `greencurve_veh_` for
*every* possible date, so merging the two prefixes would have consistently
deleted the newest terminal crash dump while keeping months-old recovered ones.
Ordering is now by the embedded `YYYYMMDD_HHMMSS_mmm` stamp, per prefix, against
its own budget. Assertions 1941/1942 pin the trap and its avoidance.

**4. Linux shipped stripped with no symbols anywhere.** `LINUX_FLAGS` carried a
bare `-s`, `COMMON_FLAGS` had no `-g`, and `dist/symbols/` held only
`windows-x64` and `windows-arm64`. A systemd-coredump core was correct and
completely unreadable. Now `-g -gdwarf-4` + prefix maps + `-Wl,--build-id=sha1`,
with `zig objcopy --strip-all --extract-to` splitting a
`dist/symbols/linux-<arch>/greencurve.debug` whose build-id is *verified equal*
to the shipped binary's — a mismatched `.debug` is one gdb silently ignores,
which is indistinguishable from having none. Verified end to end:
`0x2ef0b -> main linux_main.cpp:418`, with no developer path in the binary.

Linux also gained a real crash report (`SA_SIGINFO`: `si_code`, `si_addr`, PC/SP,
and streamed `/proc/self/maps`) written next to `config.ini` — which on Linux
*is* the binary's directory by default. Still no in-process minidump writer, on
purpose: the kernel writes the core correctly for a process whose own memory may
already be corrupt. `RLIMIT_CORE` is deliberately left alone; overriding a user's
own `ulimit -c 0` is a side effect this program has no business having, and a
gate forbids `setrlimit(` so it does not get "fixed" back in.

**Two things worth remembering.**

- I claimed an out-of-bounds read in `gc_crash_artifact_stamp()` and was wrong: a
  NUL fails every character-class check, so the fixed-width scan always stopped
  in bounds. The length precondition stayed anyway — it makes the guarantee
  stated rather than incidental — but it is not a bug fix. The test meant to
  prove it passed identically with and without the check, because string
  literals sit adjacent in `.rodata` and the "overread" simply read a neighbour.
  Sized heap buffers are what make a boundary assertion mean anything.
- `main_diagnostics.cpp` split into itself plus `main_crash_artifacts.cpp` (debug
  log vs crash artifacts — genuinely different constraints: the latter may take
  no lock, allocate nothing, and resolve paths from the environment only). The
  three near-identical `MiniDumpWriteDump` blocks collapsed into one writer;
  having three is how the VEH copy had drifted onto a different directory rule.
  A gate now pins the count at 1.

**Rejected:** registering a WER LocalDumps registry entry for the fast-fail case.
It is a machine-wide setting that changes collection behaviour for the whole
process image regardless of who launched it, which is a larger side effect than
the problem warrants; reporting in-process before the fast-fail is contained and
needs no privileged write.

**Concurrent-session note.** A separate session was editing this tree throughout
(Linux resume-restore, protocol v16→17). Its files were left untouched and are
**not** in this commit. At the time of writing that work still fails regression
assertion 81 (`SERVICE_PROTOCOL_VERSION != 16`), assertion 482 (`expectReset[2]`
versus its new lock-owns-VF rule), the `SERVICE_PROTOCOL_VERSION = 16` gates in
`build.py` and `tools/linux_gates.py`, and the source-size ratchet
(`linux_daemon.cpp` 800 → 900). This commit's own footprint in that file is zero
lines. Verification here was done with those four points temporarily aligned and
then reverted.

---

## 2026-07-31: Linux had a restart net but no standby restore, no crash-loop guard, and no reset-before-apply

Asked whether the Linux unit had parity with the Windows service's persistence
features. It did not, and two of the gaps were live defects rather than missing
features.

**The dangerous one: no automatic Linux write reset the OC baseline.**
`service_merge_desired_after_mutation()` strips `resetOcBeforeApply` before the
intent is persisted — correctly; it is a one-shot transaction instruction, not
durable state. Both boot-apply paths then applied that persisted
`DesiredSettings` **directly** to `linux_backend_apply()`, so the flag was
always false and every unattended Linux write laid a curve on top of whatever
the driver already held. That is the exact "apply on top of apply" case Windows
resets to stock to avoid.

All three unattended writers (restore-last replay, profile boot apply, and the
new resume restore) now go through one `daemon_automatic_restore_write()` that
builds its request with `service_build_full_restore_request()` — the same pure
builder Windows standby and driver recovery use. `linux_backend_apply(` is
forbidden in `linux_startup_policy.h` by a source guard so a fourth caller
cannot quietly reintroduce the direct path.

**The shared builder was itself wrong about locks, on both platforms.** It
derived "owns VF policy" from `hasGpuOffset || any hasCurvePoint`, while
`service_intent_owns_vf_cleanup()` — in the same header, used by the profile
transition path — has always counted `hasLock` too. Every lock mode composes a
VF anchor/tail write, so a lock-only profile reset its baseline when it
*replaced* another profile but not when standby restored it. One predicate now.
Regression case 2 of the restore matrix flips `false` → `true`, and the
profile-transition assertion at 534 flips with it; new cases pin that
memory-only and power-only intents still do not reset (those fields can belong
to another tool).

**`restore-last` + a restart net was a replay loop.** Windows can forbid a
mutating service start because a logon coordinator applies instead; Linux keeps
`restore-last` because it has no such thing. So a setting that hung the driver
was replayed by every restart, forever. New `linux_auto_restore_policy.h` is a
pure, clock-free guard: three start-time writes per `boot_id`, then a sticky
lockout only an explicit Apply/Reset clears; a failed unattended write latches
immediately (the Windows rule verbatim); a resume is never rationed because a
machine event cannot repeat by itself. Persisted through the same root-owned
checksummed atomic store as `active.bin`, **written before the hardware write**
(a crash mid-write must still count), and failing closed to locked out when the
record is unreadable.

**Standby resume did not exist at all.** `SERVICE_CMD_RESUME_RESTORE` (protocol
v16 → v17) plus `greencurve-resume.service`, both written by
`--service-install`. `WantedBy=` + `After=` the same sleep targets is what makes
a unit run on the way back *up*. Readiness is answered by ordering rather than
waiting: also `After=nvidia-resume.service`, so there is no sleep and no retry
loop, and `ConditionPathExists` on the socket makes a machine without a running
daemon *skip* the unit instead of failing it once per wake. The request carries
nothing — no settings, no target, no operation id, no flags — and the validator
refuses all of them, so a group-reachable socket message cannot become an apply
nobody typed.

**Unit hardening.** `Restart=on-failure` → `always` (the daemon exits 0 for any
stop signal, so an out-of-band `kill -TERM` left the machine with no GPU control
while the same thing on Windows is exactly what the SCM covers);
`StartLimitIntervalSec=600`/`StartLimitBurst=5` in `[Unit]` because systemd's
5-in-10s default is tripped instantly by a 2s `RestartSec=` and leaves the unit
`failed` until a manual `reset-failed`, where the SCM's reset period re-arms
itself; `WatchdogSec=120` with real pings from the accept loop; and
`After=multi-user.target` **removed** — the unit is `WantedBy=` that target, so
the ordering scheduled Green Curve dead last in boot, behind everything else
that might touch the GPU. Deliberately not reproduced: the 2s/5s/10s escalation
(`RestartSteps=` needs systemd ≥ 254 and the start limit already covers it).

Two smaller ones found on the way: `pl_thread_start` failure for the fan worker
was recorded in a flag and otherwise ignored, so the daemon served while nothing
re-asserted the fan — now fatal, matching Windows' worker-creation rule; and
`linux_systemd_notify.cpp` unset `NOTIFY_SOCKET` after READY, which would have
made `WatchdogSec=` silently dead, so the address is resolved into a static
first.

Coverage: pure guard state machine (new-boot reset vs sticky lockout, the
3-attempt budget, resume exemption, clean-stop vs explicit-success clearing),
guard record round-trip and four tampering rejections, resume-request
validation including one-past-the-command-set, and the reset-before-apply
matrix. New `check_auto_restore` gate in `tools/linux_gates.py` pins the single
write path, the persist-before-write order, and every unit directive above.

Not verified on hardware: no suspend/resume cycle, no crash-loop, and no
`--service-install` run on a real machine. Everything here is
cross-compile-verified and covered by pure tests only.

## 2026-07-31: Refresh dropped the window out of the taskbar and the tray icon out of its theme

Reported: clicking Refresh made the tray icon flip briefly, and the main window
disappear from the taskbar window list for the same moment. Two independent
root causes, both in presentation code that a re-read should never have touched.

**1 — the taskbar.** `gui_top_level_redraw_begin()` suppressed painting with
`SendMessage(hwnd, WM_SETREDRAW, FALSE)` on the *top-level* window.
`DefWindowProc` implements that message by clearing `WS_VISIBLE`, and the shell
derives the taskbar button, the Alt-Tab entry, and its z-order bookkeeping from
exactly that bit — so every structural projection read as a hide followed by a
show. Verified directly rather than argued: on a real visible window,
`WM_SETREDRAW(FALSE)` leaves `GetWindowLongPtr(GWL_STYLE) & WS_VISIBLE == 0` and
the TRUE message restores it.

This is the *same* mechanism as the 2026-07-15 tray ghost-window bug, which was
fixed only for the hidden direction (`gui_top_level_redraw_uses_wm_setredraw()`
returned the initial visibility). Half a fix: the rule is unconditional, and a
top-level HWND is now never redraw-toggled. Suppression moved to
`g_guiTopLevelRedrawDepth` — `WM_PAINT` validates without drawing,
`WM_ERASEBKGND` returns without filling, `invalidate_main_window()` defers via
`gui_window_invalidation_must_defer()`, and the outermost transaction issues the
one settled repaint. The counter lives in `main_runtime_gpu.cpp` beside the
invalidation helpers because the service binary compiles those too (there the
depth never leaves zero). Nesting is depth-counted, so an inner transaction can
no longer paint out from under an outer one — the old `ownsRedrawTransaction`
flag only covered the one call site that knew to check it.

Two side benefits: `IsWindowVisible()` is now truthful *inside* a transaction
(it used to report the suppressed window as hidden to our own code), and a
transaction that failed to reach its `end` would no longer strand the window
outside the taskbar permanently.

**2 — the tray icon.** `refresh_curve()` called `gui_service_begin_full_sync()`,
which is a connection-level *transition*: it forces the model to SYNCING, drops
live authority (`loaded`, control state, telemetry) and repaints the phase-only
presentation before the read is even sent. `update_tray_icon()` then computes
`hardwareLive == false`, so `hasCustomOc`/`hasCustomFan` collapse and the icon
falls back to the neutral theme — i.e. for one round trip the tray claimed no
Green Curve settings were in effect, purely because the user asked to re-read
them. The syncing overlay flashed over the graph for the same reason.

New `gui_service_request_resync()` + `gui_service_resync_decide()`: a coherent
READY model with live authority, re-read against the same GPU/service, keeps its
whole presentation and lets the completion decide. That is strictly more
truthful — a transport failure or a non-READY envelope still transitions through
the normal completion paths — and nothing is weakened by waiting, because an
Apply issued during the re-read still stamps the state identity it was computed
against and the service refuses it fail-closed if the fresh envelope moved it.
The GPU selector and the service install/removal pass `identityMayChange` and
keep the transition, which is correct: those reads invalidate the presentation
by construction. Refresh now reports itself through the status line
(`guiManualResyncPending` -> "Live GPU state refreshed."), since without the
overlay flash it would otherwise produce no feedback at all; any real
presentation transition supersedes that line.

Coverage: a second native fixture drives a real off-screen visible top-level
window and asserts the toggle pair clears `WS_VISIBLE` while the replacement
projection leaves it set throughout and still lands its control updates; pure
cases pin all four new policy decisions. Source guards (moved into
`tools/ui_gates.py` to stay under the build-script ratchet) forbid `WM_SETREDRAW`
in `gui_window_redraw.cpp` outright and forbid `gui_service_begin_full_sync("manual
refresh")`. `python build.py --test`, `python build.py`, and `python build.py
--tidy` (no new findings, 39 baselined) all pass. Not verified by clicking
Refresh in the running GUI.

Stale-risk: `gui_service_state.cpp` is at 799 lines against the 800-line
guideline — the next addition there should split the file rather than shave
comments.

## 2026-07-30: version bumped to 0.22

`VERSION` 0.21 -> 0.22. That file is the whole change: `build.py` reads it,
rejects anything that is not `MAJOR.MINOR[.PATCH]`, and injects `APP_VERSION`
into every compile/test/LSP command while also feeding PE `VERSIONINFO`,
archive names, and installer metadata. `icon.rc` is regenerated per build and
untracked, so it needs no edit. Build number moved to 512.

Also updated the README's unattended-install example filename
(`greencurve-0.22-windows-x64-setup.exe /S`), which is the only version string
outside `VERSION` in tracked non-test sources.

Deliberately *not* changed: `GC_SETTINGS_EXPORT_MIN_MINOR 21` in
`source/installer_plan_policy.h`. It is a compatibility floor — the oldest
release whose `greencurve.exe` understands `--export-active-settings` — not the
current version, and the `0.21`/`0.21.4` literals in
`tests/regression_main.cpp` are fixtures for `gc_version_at_least()` and the
prior-install path. All of those stay pinned to 21.

`python build.py`, `--check`, and `--test` pass. Stale `greencurve-0.21-*`
release artifacts remain in the repo root: the packaging step only prunes
name variants of the *current* version, so previous-version archives are left
for manual cleanup (they are gitignored).

## 2026-07-30: the enable checkbox set a flag, the tray toggle made a decision

Reported: auto-profiles enabled, `Brave.exe` configured for slot 1, no switch
away from default slot 2.

The config was right and the GUI was running. What settled it was what the log
did *not* contain: after `auto-profile: config reloaded (enabled=1 rules=1)`,
silence — no resolve, no apply, nothing, while telemetry kept ticking. The
answer was 58 seconds earlier: `auto-profile: applied slot 1 (manual)`, a tray
profile pick.

**The reset that clears a manual pin lives in `ap_set_enabled()`, and only the
tray toggle called it.** The dialog's checkbox persists `enabled=1` and reaches
the driver through `auto_profile_reload_config()`, which called
`ap_controller_sync_config()` — a value sync. The controller therefore stayed in
`AP_MODE_MANUAL`, `ap_controller_is_driving()` stayed false, and every foreground
event and 3s backstop tick returned immediately, for the rest of the session.

Two things were wrong, and both are worth stating as rules:

1. **A flag with a transition has exactly one entry point.** `enabled` is not a
   value you store, it is a state you enter: enabling clears the pin and
   re-converges, disabling reverts to the default slot so an OC profile is never
   stranded. A second UI writing the value directly gets none of that, and the
   failure is silent — no error, the feature simply does not exist.
   `ap_apply_config_change()` now owns the comparison and delegates; a source
   gate forbids `ap_controller_sync_config()` inside any enable-flipping surface.
2. **A pick while auto is disabled must not pin.** The pin exists to override
   automatic switching; with auto off there is nothing to override, and the
   documented release ("press the same slot again") is unreachable — so the pin
   was permanent and poisoned the next enable. It is now just an apply.

The deeper miss was diagnostic. The entire detect→resolve→decide path logged
**nothing** unless it applied, so "auto is on and nothing happens" produced an
empty log and looked like a rule-matching problem. Two `debug_log_on_change`
lines now cover it: one naming the gate that is blocking (`not driving …
mode=manual-pin pinned=1 …`) and one showing what detection saw and what the
rules resolved (including the per-rule presence bits, so a focus-optional rule
is visible even when its process never takes the foreground). On-change is the
right cadence here — a steady desktop costs one line, not one per backstop tick.
`SetWinEventHook`/`SetTimer` failures are now logged too; a hookless session
still converges on the backstop, which reads as a sluggish rule rather than a
failure.

The regression tests were checked the honest way: `auto_profile_controller.cpp`
was shadowed from a scratch `-I` directory with the pre-fix logic and the real
harness compiled against it — 253 for the pin, 254 for the missing transition,
each failing independently, with the working tree untouched.

---

## 2026-07-30 (latest): the banner sat on the GPU selector

Reported with a screenshot: the GPU selector "bleeding through" the in-flight
banner.

Cause, and the lesson worth keeping: **every control in this window is a child
window, and a child paints OVER its parent rather than being clipped by it.**
The banner was pinned to client (0, 0) so it could never be scrolled out of
view, which put it exactly on the GPU selector row at `y = dp(10)`. The result
is not a hidden combo — it is a combo sitting inside the banner.

Pinning it to the client area cannot be rescued by moving it down, either:
controls are placed at `y - scrollY`, so scrolling slides a different set of
them under any fixed client rectangle. The fix is to draw in the graph's own
**content** space, below `MAIN_LAYOUT_GRAPH_TOP_MARGIN_LOGICAL` — the strip
`draw_graph()` already reserves as its top margin, which is the same strip the
selector row occupies, and is now one shared constant instead of a `dp(35)`
literal in each place. That band holds no child control at any scroll position.

`gui_apply_in_flight_banner_band()` is pure and asserted (2259-2274), including
across every graph height the layout can produce, so a narrow window cannot
slide the banner back up over the row. A graph too short for the whole banner
gets none rather than a shrunken one creeping upward.

Re-verified with the scratchpad probe, this time mocking the GPU selector row at
its real coordinates so the overlap itself is what gets checked, not just "does
the banner render".

**Also confirmed in the same report:** the tick was still missing after the
first reinstall and correct after the second. That is precisely the asymmetry
predicted in the entry below — the export runs from the OLD build, so the first
upgrade had no `[transfer]` section to read and the second did. Nothing left to
fix there; the settings-transfer identity carry is now confirmed working on real
hardware.

---

## 2026-07-30 (later): same symptom, second cause — and an invisible indicator

Both reported after installing the build below, and both fair.

**1. Still no tick, this time only right after an installer upgrade.** The
service-side fix works — the log of the installed build proves it end to end
(`profile metadata proven by resulting active intent source=1 slot=2` ->
`tray profile: ... -> "Profile 2"`). But the upgrade path lost the identity
somewhere else entirely: `settings_transfer_apply()` restored the exported
intent as hard-coded `AD_HOC`.

That was a *deliberate* choice with a stated reason — the file may no longer
match the slot it came from, so claiming the slot could show a stale profile as
applied. The reasoning was sound and the answer was wrong: it paid for a
hypothetical staleness with a guaranteed loss on every single upgrade. And it is
obsolete now, because the service verifies a claimed slot instead of trusting
it. The identity travels in a `[transfer]` section and the service decides. Note
the asymmetry: the export runs from the OLD build, so the first upgrade that
benefits is the one *from* this build, not *to* it.

**2. "I am not seeing any status indicator" during an apply.** Also correct. The
first version set the background-service status line and the tray tooltip. That
label turns out to be one clipped 18 px line placed ~370 dp into the service row
near the bottom of the window — the long explanatory text I put there could not
even fit — and a tray tooltip only exists while the cursor hovers the icon. Both
were technically working and practically invisible.

There is now a banner across the top of the graph with an indeterminate sweep
(indeterminate on purpose: an apply has no meaningful percentage). Wording is
split by how much room each surface actually has, still from one shared phrase.

Verified before shipping this time: the banner was rendered headlessly by
compiling the **real** paint function against stubs in a scratchpad probe and
saving a bitmap — the same trick the uninstall self-delete question was settled
with. No GPU touched, and the rendered UI was inspected before shipping.

## 2026-07-30: the tray tick, again — and this time the log proved it

Reported: with a profile active, no tick in the tray menu at GUI start; still
none after switching profile in the main window; a tick only after picking a
slot from the tray context menu itself.

Tempting read: another GUI indicator bug, one day after the last one. It was
not. **The GUI indicator was correct every single time.** The service had
recorded `SERVICE_PROFILE_SOURCE_AD_HOC` / slot 0, and "not a personal slot" is
exactly what the indicator should say about that.

Diagnosed from `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Green Curve\greencurve_debug.txt` rather than by
reasoning — the two lines that settled it:

```
service APPLY: ignoring unverified profile metadata source=1 slot=1:
    fan ownership differs profile=1 active=0
applied profile metadata sync: verdict slot=0 reason=active profile is not a
    personal slot (authoritative=1 activeIntent=1 source=4 serviceSlot=0)
```

`service_validate_requested_profile_metadata()` proved a claimed slot against
the request **payload**. The tray/hotkey/app-start paths load a slot and send it
whole, so they passed. The GUI's Apply cannot: `capture_gui_apply_settings()`
emits a **delta** and drops domains the editor is not changing — dropping the
fan on purpose, because re-writing an unchanged fan policy would disturb a
running curve mid-game. Hence: every main-window Apply recorded ad-hoc, and the
"at GUI start" symptom is just that state observed later.

Fixed by asking the right question. Not "is the payload the profile" but "is the
profile what is now in force", which after a successful write the service holds
exactly. Two proofs now: `FROM_REQUEST` (pre-write, and the only one that may
replace ownership outright — treating a delta as a complete declaration would
default every domain it omits, i.e. reset the very fan curve the delta was
protecting) and `FROM_ACTIVE_INTENT` (post-write, records identity only). The
post-write check evaluates the *same* predicate the GUI indicator does, so the
recorded identity and the displayed tick cannot disagree again.

Also fixed in passing: the service was reading the stored record with the
default **editor** projection, i.e. the same live-curve exposure yesterday's
entry is about, latent on the service side. It now uses
`PROFILE_READ_FOR_OWNERSHIP`.

See [config-profiles.md](../config-profiles.md#the-service-must-not-lose-a-slot-identity-to-a-delta-apply).

**Feature, same session: F-INFLIGHT.** An apply deliberately takes seconds
(reset to a stock baseline, settle so boost drift is not mistaken for a failed
write, then write), and nothing said so — the tray kept the old profile's theme
and tooltip throughout. There is now a fifth, greyscale tray theme, generated by
`_grayscale_style("tray_default")` (Rec. 709 luminance; alpha byte-identical, so
it cannot drift from the artwork it greys out), plus `changes pending` wording
in the tray tooltip and the main window's status line. Both transitions are
driven from the mutation queue, the one point every apply path passes through.
See [windows-ui-layout.md](../windows-ui-layout.md#f-inflight-the-transitional-presentation-while-a-write-is-running).

**Two pre-existing breakages cleared on the way:**

- `python build.py --test` did not compile on master. The `ProfileReadMode`
  change had given `repair_profile_locked_curve_readback_artifacts()` a fifth
  parameter without updating the harness call, and `config_profile_repair.cpp`
  had started calling `gpu_offset_component_mhz_for_point()`, which the pure
  fixture never provided. The harness now supplies a documented no-exclusions
  stand-in and calls the repair in **both** read modes. Worth knowing: the
  regression suite was silently red, so nothing added in that window was ever
  actually run.
- The deterministic icon renderer moved to `tools/icon_render.py` because the
  new theme pushed `build.py` past `BUILD_SCRIPT_SIZE_RATCHET`. The ratchet is
  now 5304 (was 5648) and `main_service_pipe.cpp` 828 (was 831) — both lowered
  rather than raised, per the ratchet's own instruction.

---

## 2026-07-30: four reported UI defects

Four unrelated reports handled together. Two were one-line causes; the tray one
was not.

**1. The setup window showed the generic Windows icon.** `INSTALLER_RC` embeds
icon 101, so Explorer showed the right icon on the setup *file* — but
`gc_create_wizard_window()` registered `windowClass.hIcon =
LoadIconW(nullptr, IDI_APPLICATION)`, and a window takes its icon from its
class. Both class slots are now filled from the module resource, DPI-sized and
reapplied on `WM_DPICHANGED`. See [installer.md](../installer.md#ui). The id is
asserted equal across `installer_common.h` and `tools/installer_build.py`.

**2. The tray tick vanished when a profile was loaded without applying it.**
The interesting one. `applied_slot` is confirmed by re-reading the named slot
and comparing it with the service's active intent — but
`load_profile_from_config()` projects a stored profile onto the **live** VF
curve first (`lockTracksAnchor` is re-derived through
`curve_base_khz_for_point()`, which is readback minus live offsets), and
`lockTracksAnchor` is compared field-for-field. So the verdict flipped with
ordinary boost drift.

The reason it looked like Load's fault: none of that live state was in
`AppliedProfileSyncCache`, so the flip was **not noticed when it happened**. The
next event that did invalidate the cache applied the stale verdict — and a
profile Load is that event, because it writes `selected_slot`. The reporter's
"only applying again brings it back" is the tell: the recomputed 0 is then
cached as consistent, so nothing short of a new Apply re-establishes ownership.

This is the *second* time live readback has broken this indicator; the first
(direct absolute-MHz comparison) is already in
[config-profiles.md](../config-profiles.md#ownership-versus-live-drift). Fixed
with an explicit `PROFILE_READ_FOR_OWNERSHIP` read mode, a pure decision table
with a typed reason, the populated-point mask added to the cache key, and a
verdict line logged on every evaluation. Full write-up:
[config-profiles.md](../config-profiles.md#the-applied-indicator-must-be-drift-free).

Deliberately kept: a genuinely edited slot still clears the tick. The bug was
the false positives.

**Not verified on hardware.** Reasoned from source plus the reporter's
observation; no live Windows run has produced the new log line.

**3. The Linux TUI's VF graph had no axis labels.** It ended with one centred
`450-1240 mV • 180-3187 MHz` summary — the endpoints were the only numbers on
it. Now: MHz down the left margin, mV along the row under the plot. Two things
that were not obvious while writing it: the margin has to come out of the
plot's own width (so a narrow terminal drops labels, not the graph), and
reserving the right edge for a standalone `mV` silently suppressed the maximum
voltage, so the unit rides on the highest tick and ticks are placed right to
left.

**4. Typing into a populated TUI field concatenated.** `tui_begin_edit()` seeds
the buffer and the handler appended, so a field showing `100` became `10050`.
Entering a field now selects its contents (rendered inverted, so the state is
visible); a second click into the already-open field drops the selection so
typing amends. That second click is deliberately *not* a commit-and-reopen —
that would clamp the value and discard a partially typed number.

Both TUI changes are in [linux-scaffold.md](../linux-scaffold.md#tui---tui).

Verified: `--test`, `--test --asan`, `--tidy` (no new findings over the 39
baselined), `--target linux --check` for x64 and arm64, and the Linux-host
mingw syntax gate over `main.cpp` in both modes plus all nine installer shards.
New assertions 2054-2099 and 2206-2209; negative controls run for the graph
axes (2071/2073) and the field selection (2083/2094). `config_profiles.cpp` was
at its size ratchet, so the legacy base+gpu-offset decode moved to
`config_profile_repair.cpp` rather than raising it; `main.cpp` is back under the
800-line guideline.

## 2026-07-30: the tidy ratchet failed CI on findings it already carried

Linux CI (`python build.py --tidy`) failed with "3 finding(s) not in the
baseline" plus "5 baselined finding(s) no longer reported", on a tree where no
source file had changed. Both numbers came from the same root cause: the ratchet
was comparing text that **clang-tidy's version decides**, so a baseline written
on the dev host could not match the one CI runs.

Reproduced exactly before changing anything, by installing CI's analyzer beside
the host's: `pip install clang-tidy==18.1.1` in a throwaway venv and running
`--tidy` with it on `PATH` reproduced all three "new" findings and all five
stale entries, byte for byte, against `af0503c`.

**1. Alias names.** clang-tidy prints every *enabled* alias of a check. LLVM 20
promoted `cert-env33-c` to `bugprone-command-processor` and `cert-err34-c` to
`bugprone-unchecked-string-to-number-conversion`. The same untouched finding
therefore prints `[cert-err34-c]` on 18 and
`[bugprone-unchecked-string-to-number-conversion,cert-err34-c]` on 22, and the
baseline key was that literal string. Three findings, three failures.
Fixed by recording the alias set and matching by intersection (`_covered`).

**2. The analyzed surface.** The amalgamated `.cpp` shards are `#include`d, so
their diagnostics come from a non-main file. clang-tidy 18 suppresses those
without a header filter; 20+ prints them. CI had therefore never analyzed
`linux_backend_discovery.cpp` or `linux_daemon_transport.cpp` at all — the gate
was quietly weaker there than on the dev host, which is worse than the failure
that exposed it. Fixed by passing `SHARD_HEADER_FILTER` explicitly (shards in,
`.h` out); clang-tidy 18 then reports the shard findings too (4 `cert-err34-c`
rather than 2), and the finding sets match across both versions.

**3. The advice printed on every failure was a trap.** "rerun --tidy-baseline to
shrink the baseline", followed on the older host, would have deleted entries the
newer analyzer still reports, handing the dev host a failure over a pre-existing
finding — the same bug mirrored. `--tidy-baseline` now only adds, dropping an
entry solely when its source file is gone; the stale list is advisory, names the
running version, and says to delete the line by hand once the finding is
confirmed fixed everywhere.

Residual and expected: analyzer capability itself differs. LLVM 22 finds a
`clang-analyzer-optin.taint.TaintedAlloc` in `linux_daemon_transport.cpp` that
18 does not. This is now an advisory naming the version, not a failure.

Verified: `--tidy` clean on both 18.1.1 and 22.1.8; still exits 1 when a
baseline line is deleted (with a "baseline was generated by ... this host runs
..." note); `--test`, `--test --asan`, and a full `python build.py` all pass.
New coverage in `static_analysis.run_self_tests()`, wired into the regression
suite. Runner logs its clang-tidy path/version and the header filter, and the
baseline records its generator, so the next version skew is visible in the log
rather than inferred.

**Not done:** the analyzer stays unpinned (CI takes Ubuntu's `clang-tidy`, dev
takes the distro's). Pinning it the way llvm-mingw/Zig are pinned would remove
the residual capability drift; it is the open question here, not a silent gap.

---

## 2026-07-29: the Linux archive was only usable when packaged on Linux

Reported from the field: a Linux release built on Windows shipped a setup script
that did not work; the same source packaged on a native Linux host did. Two
independent, both-fatal, host-dependent defects, neither of which any gate could
see.

**1. CRLF (fatal on its own).** This repo has `core.autocrlf=true` and had no
`.gitattributes`, so `git ls-files --eol tools/greencurve-setup.sh` read
`i/lf w/crlf`: the index blob is LF (11585 bytes), the Windows working copy is
CRLF (11893 bytes — exactly one extra byte per line). `package_release_archive()`
copied the *working* copy with `shutil.copy2`, and the shipped archive member was
11893 bytes, confirming CRLF went out. On Linux the shebang then reads
`#!/usr/bin/env bash\r`, so `env` looks for an interpreter named `bash\r`
(`env: 'bash\r': No such file or directory`). Working around it with
`bash greencurve-setup.sh` does not help: the heredoc at
`tools/greencurve-setup.sh:184` would install a `.desktop` entry with
`Exec=... --from-desktop\r` and `Terminal=true\r`, and `$UNIT_NAME`/`$UNIT_PATH`
comparisons carry CRs too.

**2. The Unix executable bit.** `build.py` had
`os.chmod(staged, 0o755)` with a comment explaining why it was needed. On
Windows that call cannot do it — measured: the mode afterwards is `0o666`,
because Windows `os.chmod` only toggles read-only. And 7-Zip on Windows records
no Unix mode at all (the shipped member listed `Attributes = A`; 7-Zip 26.02 has
no switch for it — only `-snh`, `-snl`, `-sni`, `-sns`), so *both* the script and
the `greencurve` ELF extracted non-executable. That is not cosmetic: the script's
own `require_binary` aborts on `[ -x "$BINARY" ]`.

**Why nothing caught it.** `linux_gates.check_release_packaging` was entirely
`require_text`/`forbid_text` substring checks — line-ending agnostic, and they
never looked at the staged copy. `_verify_archive_manifest` compared path names
only. `write_text_if_changed()` already forced `newline="\n"` for files build.py
*generates*; the setup script was copied, so it never went through that path.

**Fixes (root cause, both ends).**

- `.gitattributes` pins `*.sh text eol=lf`; the working-tree copy was rewritten
  to LF in the same commit.
- `stage_release_file()` *rewrites* Linux release text (`*.sh`, `*.md`,
  `LICENSE`) through `normalize_release_text()` instead of copying it, so a
  clone predating `.gitattributes` still packages correctly. A lone CR surviving
  CRLF→LF is a hard error, not something to silently rewrite. Binaries are still
  copied byte-for-byte.
- **The Linux release container changed from `.7z` to `.tar.xz`**
  (`release_archive_extension()`), written by `tarfile` (`w:xz`, preset 9) with
  explicit `mode`/`uid 0`/`gid 0`/`root:root`. This is the only way a Windows
  host can emit correct Unix modes, and tar is what a Linux user can unpack
  without installing p7zip. Windows keeps `.7z`.
- `verify_linux_tarball()` reads the finished archive back: names, per-member
  `release_member_mode()`, zero ownership, LF-only text, `#!` shebang.
  `_verify_archive_manifest` moved to `release_manifest.verify_seven_zip_manifest()`.
- Packaging deletes every name a target could have produced
  (`release_archive_paths()`, both containers + `.sha256`), so a stale, broken
  `…-linux-<arch>.7z` cannot ship beside the new tarball. The four stale ones in
  the build directory were removed by the first run.
- A missing 7-Zip now withholds only Windows: `report_packaging_skipped()`
  *raises* on a non-Windows entry. CI's Linux job no longer installs p7zip, so a
  reintroduced dependency fails instead of passing quietly.

**Coverage.** `security_gates.check_linux_release_packaging()` runs the real
helpers over a CRLF, mode-less fixture and asserts the archive read back is LF,
`0755`/`0644`, `uid/gid 0`, shebang intact, and that the ELF fixture's own CR
bytes survived; three hand-built broken archives must be rejected.
`linux_gates.check_setup_script_line_endings()` reads the tracked script as bytes
(the check `.gitattributes` cannot make for an existing clone).
`BUILD_SCRIPT_SIZE_RATCHET` 5650 → 5648.

Verified: full `python build.py` (build 501, all four targets + both installers)
and `python build.py --test` both pass. Both tarballs inspected: `greencurve` and
`greencurve-setup.sh` at `0755`, docs at `0644`, `root:root`, zero CR bytes in
any text member, first line `#!/usr/bin/env bash`.

**Not yet verified on hardware:** nobody has extracted either new `.tar.xz` on a
real Linux box and run `sudo ./greencurve-setup.sh install` end to end. The mode
and line-ending properties are asserted from the archive headers/bytes, which is
what failed before, but the install itself is still unconfirmed for this format.

---

## 2026-07-29: 7-Zip is optional for a build; privacy gate over tracked files

**Packaging no longer fails a build when 7-Zip is absent.** `main()` resolves
`find_seven_zip()` once *before* staging; when it returns `None` it calls
`report_packaging_skipped()` (loud warning + every built binary path + install
hint) and exits 0. Previously `package_release_archive()` raised
`RuntimeError("7-Zip is required...")` after the binaries were already built and
verified, discarding a good build with a traceback. The guard inside
`package_release_archive()` is kept as a defensive check for direct callers.
Verified end-to-end by driving a real `--target linux --arch x64` run with the
lookup patched to `None`: build succeeded, warning printed, exit code 0.

**Privacy scan of the repo (all tracked non-`source/` files):**

- No secrets, keys, tokens, emails, or credentials anywhere; CI uses no secrets.
- Tracked docs and test fixtures use placeholders (`%USERPROFILE%\...`) and a
  synthetic fixture account (`testuser`) rather than real account data (details
  omitted).
- New `security_gates.check_no_developer_profile_paths(ctx, tracked)` fails the
  build if any tracked text file hardcodes a Windows profile directory that is
  not a placeholder, an env var, a synthetic account, or a one-letter fixture.
  Verified by planting a canary path in a tracked file (detected) and removing it.

**A gate that could never fail:** `require_text(build_script, "unexpected linker
side product", ...)` passed only because the needle appeared in its own source
line in `build.py`. Moving it into `release_manifest.check_all()` exposed this.
It now points at `tools/security_gates.py`, where the real fixture (staging a
stray `main.lib` into a payload) lives. **Stale-risk: other `require_text`
guards that name `build_script` and quote a string that exists only in the guard
itself are equally tautological — worth an audit.**

**Size ratchet forced the layout.** `build.py` must stay ≤ 5650 lines, so the
new code went to `tools/`: `find_seven_zip()`, `report_packaging_skipped()`,
`check_packaging_skip_warning()` and the packaging guards (`check_all()`) to
`release_manifest.py`; the privacy gate to `security_gates.py`. `build.py` ended
at 5649. Note `build.py` is CRLF like the rest of the tree — a scripted rewrite
that emits LF silently reformats the whole file.

## 2026-07-29: pre-ship capability projection and truthful self-tests

Targeted review fixes for `914b09d6..HEAD`, with no protocol-version change or
large rewrite:

- The Windows service now projects its `GpuCapabilityProbe` into the GUI
  snapshot. Seven two-bit domain states and memory topology reuse the former
  five reserve bytes in `ServiceGpuHealth`; size remains 216 bytes and v16
  peers that leave them zero decode conservatively as `UNPROBED`/`UNKNOWN`.
  This makes the limited-surface warning and unified-memory confirmation use
  the authoritative service probe instead of the GUI process's zeroed copy.
- Windows `--self-test` uses a pure verdict policy. `FULL` requires successful
  NVML, VF curve, and private CONTROL reads plus writable/full capability; every
  lesser verdict returns nonzero (1 unusable, 2 degraded/inconclusive).
- Linux `--self-test` captures the read-only rollback snapshot before reporting
  health and capability, so it no longer prints uninitialized per-domain state
  and a PASS also proves the pre-mutation snapshot is available.
- ARM64 inspection text now names the required native `nvapia64.dll`, and the
  reload comment is architecture-neutral.

Regression codes 2178-2205 cover wire round-trip/validation, legacy-zero
compatibility, and the verdict/exit matrix. Source gates pin both producers,
the GUI decode, and self-test exit propagation. Verified with normal and
ASan/UBSan regression suites, all five fuzz targets at 20,000 runs each,
clang-tidy with no findings outside the 39-entry baseline, the four-target
Windows/Linux × x64/ARM64 check matrix, and the full packaged build 497.
