## 2026-07-29: closing the silent path — an integrated part reporting a discrete family

Tracing what a user would actually SEE on an RTX Spark exposed a gap. Which
warning fires depends on GB10's architecture id, which nobody knows yet:

- Unrecognized id → best-guess tier fires ("not tested yet"). Fine.
- Reports `0x1B0` (Blackwell) + a domain missing → reduced-surface tier. Fine.
- Reports `0x1B0` + **every domain answers** → **nothing at all**. Untested
  silicon treated as a validated discrete card, in silence.

Closed without guessing, using a contradiction we can already observe: every
GeForce card Green Curve has validated owns its VRAM, so a *recognized discrete
family* reporting a *unified* pool can only be an integrated part wearing that
family's architecture id. `gpu_capability_topology_contradicts_discrete()` is
now a second, independent trigger for the reduced-surface tier.

Still positive-evidence-only, so it remains unreachable on existing hardware:
discrete GPUs report DEDICATED, and a failed or absent topology query reports
UNKNOWN — both return false. Pinned both directions by codes 2190-2202,
including the zeroed-probe and null cases.

Because this trigger can fire with NOTHING missing, the dialog needed a second
text: "reports a reduced control surface (full) — does not expose: (none)" would
have been nonsense. `integratedOnly` selects integrated-part wording (title,
main instruction, body) in both the TaskDialog and the MessageBox fallback.

Live x64 re-check on the RTX 5070 after the change: topology `dedicated`,
surface `full`, verdict FULL — nothing new fires.

---

## 2026-07-29: the arm64 driver really does carry the private OC surface

Scanned driver 616.00's NvAPI images for the 32-bit entry-point ids Green Curve
passes `nvapi_QueryInterface`. Exports could never answer this — that function
is NVAPI's only export and everything else is an internal dispatch-table id.

**Result: every id, including all four private VF ids, is in the ARM64 image
with counts identical to x64** (2x each; the x86 build shows 4x). So VF-curve
OC/UV is built into the arm64 driver. The remaining unknown narrows from "does
the surface exist" to "does the driver honour a write", which needs hardware.

Two lessons worth keeping:

- **Scan the `*_impl` images.** `nvapi64.dll` is an ~800 KB shim over a ~6 MB
  `nvapi64_impl.dll`; the table is in the latter. The first version excluded
  `_impl` on an unverified assumption and reported every id MISSING in EVERY
  architecture — including the x64 one we resolve daily. That impossible result
  is what identified the filter as the bug rather than the driver.
- **The control pair is what makes the scan self-checking.** Scanning arm64
  alone would have read as "NVIDIA stripped the OC surface for arm64" and been
  believed. Two architectures from one package cannot both be wrong.

Also fixed a false positive in the new tracked-file privacy gate
(`security_gates.check_no_developer_profile_paths`): it flagged the very wiki
line documenting its own fix, because that line spells the placeholder `<dev>`
and the allowlist only enumerated `<username>`/`<admin>`/`<youruser>`. Now any
angle-bracketed segment is accepted structurally — `<` and `>` are invalid in a
Windows account name, so such a segment provably is not a real profile, and the
allowlist no longer has to keep up with placeholder spellings.

Driver-inspection source gates moved into `tools/driver_inspect.py`
(`check_source_gates`), following the linux_gates/ui_gates pattern, to keep
build.py under its ratchet.

---

## 2026-07-29: RTX Spark follow-through — arm64 NVAPI is real, and we were loading the wrong DLL

Downloaded and inspected the actual RTX Spark developer-preview driver
(`616.00_DeveloperPreview_win11_arm64_International.exe`, 739 MB, 266 files).
**Verdict: FULL.** Both control surfaces ship as native ARM64 images and every
entry point Green Curve needs is exported.

The inspection immediately caught a **blocker bug in our own loader**. NVIDIA
names the NVAPI runtime per architecture and ships all of them side by side in
`Display.Driver\`:

| Image | Machine |
|---|---|
| `nvapia64.dll` | ARM64 |
| `nvapi64.dll` | x64 (emulation copy) |
| `nvapi.dll` | x86 (emulation copy) |
| `nvml.dll` | **ARM64** (native — no per-arch handling needed) |
| `nvml_arm64ec.dll` | x64 / ARM64EC compat |

`nvapi_qi()` tried `nvapi64.dll` then `nvapi.dll` — on arm64 both are the wrong
machine type, so the arm64 build would have found **no NVAPI at all** and had
zero VF capability. Fixed via the pure `nvapi_module_policy.h` table (arm64
prefers `nvapia64.dll`); x64 ordering unchanged and pinned by codes 2180-2188,
which exercise the arm64 ordering from an x64 host.

Also landed items 2-5 of the remaining-work list:

- **Windows `--self-test`** (`main_self_test.cpp`), counterpart of the Linux
  one. Dispatches before the service gate and drives NvAPI directly rather than
  through the service-mediated init — otherwise a machine without a healthy
  service reports "VF curve read failed" and tells you nothing about the driver.
  Reports the loaded NVAPI module path, elevation (unelevated ⇒ `INCONCLUSIVE`,
  never a hardware verdict), and the full per-domain surface.
- **Windows memory topology via DXGI**, replacing the hardcoded `UNKNOWN`.
  Needed device-id normalization: NvAPI reports a packed `0x2F0410DE` while DXGI
  reports the bare `0x2F04`, so the naive compare never matched.
- **Unified-memory confirmation** wired to it, gated on positively-reported
  `UNIFIED` so no discrete GPU can reach it. The missing service-to-GUI
  capability projection was fixed in the pre-ship entry above.
- **Linux capability symmetry**, derived from the daemon's existing
  `availableMutationDomains` rather than re-probed, so the two cannot disagree.
  Tegra detection now exists exactly once (`linux_platform_is_integrated_soc`).

**A false positive the live test caught**, worth remembering: the first probe
called only the legacy `nvmlDeviceGet{Gpc,Mem}ClkMinMaxVfOffset` and reported
**both offset domains as "refused" on a working RTX 5070** — current drivers
answer NOT_SUPPORTED there and serve the modern P-state-scoped
`getClockOffsets`. That would have raised the new reduced-surface warning on
healthy x64 installs. Root-cause fix: probe through `nvml_get_offset_range()`,
the apply path's own resolver, so probe and apply can never disagree. Gated by
`forbid_text` on the legacy getter.

Live x64 verification on the RTX 5070: `Control surface: full`, every domain
`available`, topology `dedicated`, `NvAPI module: …\nvapi64.dll` — i.e. nothing
changed for existing hardware.

Ratchet-driven splits (all "split, don't raise"): `nvapi_loader.cpp`,
`main_cli_help.cpp`, `main_self_test.cpp`, `nvapi_module_policy.h`. Several
build gates were repointed from `entry.cpp` to the parser/help shards; the
settings-transfer gates now anchor on `main_cli_options.cpp`, which is the
better anchor anyway (the parser is what makes a verb real).

Still hardware-gated and unchanged: whether the private pstates20 **ordinals**
resolve on GB10 silicon, and whether VF writes are honoured. RTX Spark systems
ship fall 2026.

---

## 2026-07-29: F-CAP — per-domain control surface for integrated Grace/Blackwell (RTX Spark / GB10)

Prompted by NVIDIA's RTX Spark developer preview. The post ships a Windows 11
arm64 GeForce driver (`616.00`) and CUDA 13.4, and is Windows-on-Arm only — it
says nothing about NVAPI, NVML, clocks, power, fan, or Linux arm64. RTX Spark is
the productized GB10 Grace Blackwell module: 20-core Arm CPU + 48-SM Blackwell
GPU, up to 128 GB **unified** LPDDR5X, ~80 W for the whole package. So the
build/porting leg was never the blocker — the hardware shape is.

The problem this fixes: a GB10-class part reports Blackwell, so
`vf_backend_for_architecture()` selects the (correct) Blackwell struct layout
and, as a side effect, marks it a *validated discrete board* and suppresses
every caveat. Family detection was answering a question it was never asked.

Added `source/gpu_capability_policy.h` — pure, no OS headers, testable on both
hosts. Per-domain capability (`UNPROBED`/`AVAILABLE`/`REFUSED`/`ABSENT`), memory
topology, derived surface class. **Core invariant:** the layer only subtracts a
domain the driver actually refused, so a zeroed probe is byte-identical to
pre-F-CAP behavior. Asserted at compile time and pinned from both directions by
regression codes 2100–2166.

Two inferences were deliberately **rejected** as regression risks on hardware
that works today:

- `min == max` power window ⇒ absent. Fixed-TGP laptop boards report exactly
  that and would have started warning.
- Unresolved entry point ⇒ absent. That is an older driver, not absent
  hardware; it stays `UNPROBED`.

Also: second GUI warning tier with its own opt-out
(`hide_limited_control_warning`) so it cannot be masked by the
unrecognized-family one; `--inspect-arm64-windows-driver` with a built-in PE
export parser, which answers whether the arm64 driver ships NVAPI at all
(validated against real x64 `nvapi64.dll`/`nvml.dll` — all symbols resolved,
correctly reported as wrong-architecture).

Refactors forced by the size ratchets, both done by splitting rather than
raising: `tools/driver_inspect.py` (both driver inspectors, build.py 5840 → 5607)
and `source/main_capability_warning.cpp` (both warning tiers behind one
`show_gpu_support_warnings()` call site, so `entry.cpp` returned to its ratchet
unchanged at 861).

Verified: `python build.py --test` green; full 4-target matrix
(windows/linux × x64/arm64) builds, verifies and packages. **No hardware
validation** — the entire point is that the honest per-domain report is what
reaches the first person with a Spark.

Closed by the follow-through above: Windows now obtains memory topology from
DXGI, and the later pre-ship fix projects that service result to the GUI.

---

## 2026-07-29: pre-ship targeted fixes after review of 7248a75..HEAD

Implemented the four ship-blocking or worthwhile targeted findings without a
refactor:

- **Linux sparse VF replacement now releases old ownership.** The daemon
  already replaced the requested VF domain in durable intent, but the backend
  built its hardware mask from only the incoming sparse request. An omitted old
  explicit point therefore stayed offset in hardware while disappearing from
  `active.bin`. The transaction now builds the post-merge committed curve and
  adds zero-offset writes for points owned only by the previous curve mask.
  Those writes share the existing verified curve phase and full-snapshot
  rollback. Cleanup count is logged; pure cases 2051-2053 cover both an actual
  released point and a point that remains owned by a composed policy.
- **Unsafe installer roots fail before disruption.** The direct-child-of-
  Program-Files check used to run only after settings capture, GUI shutdown and
  service stop. It now runs before all three, the folder page rejects the same
  paths before accepting them, and source-order gates pin both boundaries.
- **Uninstall no longer deletes a coincidentally matching Run value.** The old
  predicate was a case-insensitive substring search. It now parses the first
  executable token and requires its leaf to equal `greencurve.exe`; prefixes,
  suffixes, malformed quoting and argument-only mentions fail closed.
- **The tidy ratchet is green again.** All 37 unbaselined split-file findings
  were fixed in code. `--tidy-baseline` removed only the three obsolete entries
  that referred to the pre-split files, reducing the baseline from 42 to 39.

Validation: normal, sanitizer and ASan regression runs; clang-tidy with no new
findings; all-target Windows/Linux x64/ARM64 check builds; five fuzz targets at
5,000 runs each; and the complete setup/archive release build all pass at build
480. No Linux GPU transition or live Windows install/uninstall was performed in
this pass.

---

## 2026-07-29: uninstall left the autostart tasks and its own binary behind

Two user-reported leftovers after an uninstall, plus two more found while fixing
them. See [installer.md](../installer.md#uninstall).

**1. The logon tasks stayed registered.** `Green Curve Startup - <user>` is
written by the GUI into Task Scheduler and lives nowhere near the install
directory or the ARP key, so nothing in `gc_uninstall_execute()` touched it. The
same is true of the `HKCU\...\Run` value that starts the resident tray process —
not reported, but the identical bug, so both are removed now by
`source/installer_autostart.cpp` (`ITaskService` enumeration of the root folder,
verified by re-enumeration because both deletion paths report failure for
"already absent" too; `HKEY_USERS` sweep for the Run value). Both registrations
are per-user, and the elevated uninstaller is the only component that can reach
every account's copy.

**2. `uninstall.exe` stayed in the folder.** It was handed to the session
manager with `MOVEFILE_DELAY_UNTIL_REBOOT`, so the folder survived until the
next restart. Now unlinked immediately by renaming the running image's `::$DATA`
to an alternate stream and then deleting it.

**The published form of that technique does not work.** Every write-up of it
uses `FileDispositionInfo` after the rename. Measured on Windows 11 26200 with
one fresh probe binary per combination:

| Attempt | Result |
|---|---|
| `FileDispositionInfoEx(DELETE\|POSIX)` alone | `ERROR_ACCESS_DENIED` |
| rename + `FileDispositionInfo` (classic) | `ERROR_ACCESS_DENIED` |
| rename + `FileDispositionInfoEx(DELETE\|POSIX)` | file and folder gone, process alive |

Only the POSIX-semantics delete gets past the image-section check after the
rename. The first implementation used the classic form and would have silently
kept the reboot-only behavior; a source gate now pins the POSIX call as the
first attempt.

Two defects found on the way:

- **The setup stub scheduled itself for deletion.** `gc_uninstall_execute()`
  also runs inside `greencurve-<ver>-setup.exe --uninstall`, which sits in a
  downloads folder; the self-delete was unconditional, so an uninstall run that
  way quietly took the user's setup file at the next reboot.
  `gc_uninstall_self_is_installed_copy()` now gates it.
- **The service binary could still be locked.** `--service-remove` waits for
  SERVICE_STOPPED, which the service reports from inside its own process; the
  install path already waits on the process *handle* for exactly this reason,
  but the uninstall path did not. The handle is now taken before the
  registration disappears and waited on after.

Also: the working directory is moved out of the install folder first (an
uninstaller double-clicked in Explorer holds it as CWD), and the folder is
scheduled for restart removal only when the files left in it are *ours* — the
old code scheduled it even when the leftovers were the user's, where the pending
rmdir would have failed anyway.

Toolchain notes worth keeping:

- `-ltaskschd` is not usable. mingw ships those GUIDs in a **static UUID
  archive**, not an import library, and Zig's arm64 link step fails with
  "unable to find dynamic system library 'taskschd'". `<initguid.h>` before
  `<taskschd.h>` emits them in-place instead; a gate keeps the lib out.
- `FileDispositionInfoEx` is gated differently by the two toolchains:
  llvm-mingw on `NTDDI_VERSION >= 0x0A000002` (satisfied at this project's
  target), Zig's bundled mingw copy on `_WIN32_WINNT >= 0x0A000002`, which a
  `_WIN32_WINNT` value can never reach. x64 sees the enumerator, arm64 does not.
  The ABI value 21 is spelled out where it is missing and `static_assert`ed
  where it is present.

Assertions 2041-2050. `python build.py --test` and full `python build.py` pass
for both architectures. **The uninstall has not been run end to end on a real
machine** — the self-delete sequence was measured there directly, but the task
enumeration has not met a real registered task.

At this point `python build.py --tidy` failed on 37 findings and 3 stale
baseline entries that were identical on a clean tree. The later pre-ship pass
above fixed all findings in code and removed only the stale baseline entries.

---

## 2026-07-29: review of 7248a75..HEAD — the boot-apply detector read the wrong field

Pre-ship review of the whole 52-commit range. Two findings worth fixing, three
minor. Everything else checked clean: payload container parsing, installer DACL
and secure-temp handling, request/response validation, the Windows pipe
authorization gates, shared-only policy enforcement, the sync-client stamping
chain, the GUI mutation worker's locking, and the fan-curve dedup.

**1. The v15 staleness detector was reporting on the applied settings.**
`daemon_handle_get_startup_policy()` wrote the boot snapshot into
`resp->desired`; `daemon_stamp_state_envelope()` runs at the end of *every*
`handle_request()` and rewrites that member with `g_activeDesired`. The snapshot
never left the daemon. `linux_startup_snapshot_report()` therefore compared
whatever was applied right now against the profile on disk:

- applying anything other than profile N (routine) painted `PROFILE N STALE` in
  the TUI and printed "the startup snapshot differs from profile N" from
  `--show-startup`;
- a genuinely stale snapshot read as in-sync whenever the applied intent
  happened to match the profile;
- `--show-startup`'s `boots:` line printed the active intent — the exact
  diagnostic that exists because the original diagnosis needed a hex dump of
  `startup.bin`.

`g_hasActiveDesired` is set true by `apply_startup_profile_policy()` itself, so
on a machine with boot-apply configured this was always live after boot. The
push half of 4dcccb2 (Save → `REFRESH_STARTUP_PROFILE`) was fine; only its
verification half was wrong.

Fixed at the root rather than by reordering: protocol **v16** gives the snapshot
its own response member, `startupProfile` + `startupProfileValid`. `desired`
now has exactly one meaning on the wire, the stamp owns it outright, and there
is no handler/stamp ordering left to get wrong. Coherence is enforced at the
trust boundary (`service_response_startup_profile_is_coherent`): the flag is a
boolean, unset means byte-zero, and a set flag is accepted only while the
published policy really is `profile N`. `daemon_publish_startup_snapshot()`
fills it from the *committed* record for all three startup commands, so a set
or refresh that failed to reach disk still answers with what will actually boot.

Why the suite missed it: 1970-2005 assert the pure decisions with hand-supplied
snapshots. Same shape as 1801-1805 in the 769827f entry below — **a decision
suite with no counterpart for how the inputs arrive proves nothing.** 2026-2035
now cover the carriage.

**2. The installer's export gate inferred a capability from a version.**
`gc_prior_supports_settings_export()` gated on ARP `DisplayVersion >= 0.21`,
documented as "the first release whose greencurve.exe understands
`--export-active-settings`". The verb actually arrived in 23a4397 — twelve
commits *after* `VERSION` moved to 0.21 — so builds satisfy the test and do not
know the argument, fall through `handle_cli()` to opening the GUI, and setup
sits in front of it until the timeout. 0.21 is unshipped, so the version number
stays; the fix is that the capability is now **recorded**, not inferred: every
install writes `GreenCurveSettingsExport = 1` beside the ARP entry, and that
marker wins in both directions. The version comparison survives only as the
fallback for installs predating the marker. The export also got its own 20 s
bound (`GC_APP_EXPORT_TIMEOUT_MS`) so a wrong guess costs seconds rather than a
minute of an unattended update looking hung.

**Minor, same pass:**
- `build.py --test` printed `ERROR: test-tool SHA-256 mismatch` on a *passing*
  run — the negative control in `run_build_script_regression_tests()` driving
  the tamper path on purpose. It now says "tampered binary rejected as
  expected" via `mismatch_expected`; an ERROR line that always appears trains
  everyone to ignore the one that means a real toolchain was swapped.
- `daemon_open_listener()` reported a `configure_daemon_socket_permissions()`
  failure as "listen() failed" with whatever errno had survived from an earlier
  call. Socket ownership is exactly what goes wrong there, so the two are now
  separate branches with their own messages.
- `GcInstallContext::capturedSettingsPath` was `MAX_PATH` while the path is
  built in `GC_INSTALLER_MAX_PATH_CHARS` storage and copied with an unchecked
  `StringCchCopyW`. Latent (the real path is ~85 chars), but a truncation would
  aim `--apply-settings-file` at a bogus path and then `RemoveDirectoryW` a
  truncated directory. Sized like every other installer path buffer.

**Found while verifying: `--fuzz` had been broken on Windows for a week.**
Every Win32 fuzz target failed to link on `set_message`, `trim_ascii` and
`parse_int_strict`. When 9d07f84/fe91674 moved the shared text helpers out of
the Win32-only `config_utils.cpp` into `config_text_utils.cpp`, three lists
needed the new TU: `LINUX_SOURCE_FILES`, `WINDOWS_SOURCE_FILES` and the Win32
fuzz link. The first two were updated; the third was not, and it went unnoticed
because every `--fuzz` run afterwards was on a Linux host, which takes the
`FUZZ_LINUX_EXTRA_SOURCES` path instead. Confirmed pre-existing by stashing:
identical failure at 769827f. The Win32 link list is now the module constant
`FUZZ_WIN32_SOURCES` so `check_fuzz_target_wiring()` can assert against it —
the same rule the Linux list already had. All five targets compile and pass
20 000 runs each on Windows.

Verified: `--test`, `--check` (windows x64/arm64 + linux x64/arm64), `--fuzz`
(all five targets, Windows host) and a full `python build.py` all pass.
Negative controls: disabling the coherence rule fails 2030, ignoring the
recorded marker fails 2036, dropping `config_text_utils.cpp` from
`FUZZ_WIN32_SOURCES` fails the wiring gate, and re-adding either forbidden line
fails its source gate.

**Not exercised on hardware:** no `GET_STARTUP_POLICY` has crossed a real socket
since the field moved. First thing to check on the next Linux install — apply
something other than the bound profile and confirm the row stays green.

## 2026-07-29: every CLI mutation was refused, and the refusal never arrived

Reported: running setup while Green Curve was running left the GPU at driver
defaults afterwards; the previously active settings were not restored.

The 01:25 run in `greencurve_debug.txt` shows the capture succeeding
(`exporting active intent gpu=475MHz … curvePoints=57`), the new service
reaching READY, `applying restored intent`, and then:

```
service_pipe_server: rejected malformed v12 request command=4 pid=4448
service client mutation: operation=118517… command=4 initial transport failed:
    Service response contains an invalid state envelope
```

with `greencurve_cli_log.txt` reporting only "Operation … is still pending or
its outcome is unknown". Both client and service were protocol 14, build 470 —
no version skew. Three separate defects, stacked:

**1. The synchronous client never stamped the preconditions it had just read.**
`validate_service_request_for_ipc()` requires every APPLY/RESET to carry
`expectedServiceInstanceId`, `expectedGpuGeneration` and (for a VF domain)
`expectedTopologySignature`. Only `gui_mutation_stamp_request()` ever set them,
from `GuiServiceModel`. `service_client_build_apply_request()` /
`…_build_reset_request()` never did, and nothing else filled them in — so every
CLI apply, `--apply-settings-file`, and `--reset`, plus the reset
`--service-remove` performs before deleting the service, went out with three
zeros and was refused. The log confirms the same rejection on 2026-07-27 for a
`command=5` RESET from an uninstall.

The 2026-07-27 "fix" (fetch a READY envelope before applying, assertions
1801-1805) fixed the *prerequisite* and not the *carry*: those assertions test
the validator with hand-built requests, so they passed while the product path
stayed broken. Two live upgrades restored nothing.

**2. A refusal could not reach the client that caused it.** The service only
calls `populate_service_state_response()` when the caller cleared the
session/PID/integrity gates, so a protocol-level rejection answers with a
message and an all-zero envelope. `validate_service_response_for_ipc()` rejected
that as damaged, the client reported "invalid state envelope" as a transport
failure, and the mutation path then queried the operation and announced
"pending or unknown" — turning a definitive "your request was malformed" into an
unknown outcome. Every protocol and authorization refusal was undiagnosable.

**3. Across a protocol bump the capture cannot work at all.** The capture runs
the *payload's* `greencurve.exe --export-active-settings` against the *old*
running service (deliberately, so the verb is always understood). The tree is
now protocol 15 while installed builds are 14: verified live —
`refresh_background_service_state: ping FAILED err=[Service response protocol
mismatch (magic=0x47535643 version=14)]`. The export would report "no active
settings", and the upgrade would restore nothing while reporting success. Not
what broke the 01:25 run, but it would have broken the very next one.

Fixes, all root-cause:

- `service_client_precondition_policy.h` (new, pure): the identity a mutation
  must name, adopted only from a coherent READY envelope, cleared when the
  envelope is not usable. `main_state_sync.cpp` adopts it in
  `apply_ready_service_envelope_to_app()` (`g_syncClientStateIdentity`); the
  apply/reset clients stamp from it and fail closed without it.
- `service_client_execute_mutation_request()` refuses an unstamped mutation
  **before the send**, so the outcome stays knowable (nothing was attempted) and
  no future path can regress into the zero-precondition rejection.
- `service_client_reset()` fetches the identity on demand — a Reset derives
  nothing from published state, and `--service-remove` has no preamble.
- `validate_service_response_for_ipc()` accepts an error response whose whole
  payload is absent (byte-exact over all four members; a half-populated envelope
  is still damaged). A successful response must still carry one.
- `service_request_reject_reason()` **is** the request rule set;
  `validate_service_request_for_ipc()` is now "no rule is broken", so the log
  line and the decision cannot drift. The service logs the reason and the real
  protocol version instead of a hardcoded "malformed v12".
- The capture asks the **installed** binary first (it matches the running
  service by construction), gated on `gc_version_at_least(prior->version, 0, 21)`
  and on the ARP directory equalling the SCM service directory, and falls back to
  the payload binary. An unknown or older build is never run with a verb it may
  not know.

Assertions 2006-2016 (envelope → identity → stamped request → validator),
2017-2020 (stateless refusals), 2021-2025 (which binary is asked). Gates:
F-SYNC-STAMP in `build.py`, capture-order gates in `tools/installer_build.py`.

Not yet exercised on a real machine: the end-to-end upgrade restore. The client
probe against the live v14 service could only confirm the protocol-skew
diagnosis, since the installed service predates the v15 bump.

## 2026-07-29: the boot-apply profile was a photograph, not a link

Reported: fan hysteresis set to 4 °C and the poll interval to 2000 ms, visible
in the TUI, saved to profile slot 1, applied successfully — and back to the
2 °C/1000 ms defaults after a reboot, with `profile 1` auto-apply configured.

The on-disk evidence settled it before any code was read. `config.ini` held
`hysteresis_c=4`/`poll_interval_ms=2000` in both `[profile1_fan_curve]` and
`[fan_curve]`, so saving was never broken. `/var/lib/greencurve/startup.bin`,
dated **the previous evening**, held `1000`/`2`, and `active.bin`, dated *after*
the successful apply, held `1000`/`2` as well — because the daemon had restarted
in between and rewritten it from the startup record. The daemon log's
`startup profile 1 (profile 1) applied` line was the moment the stale values
went back on the GPU.

Root cause: `SERVICE_CMD_SET_STARTUP_POLICY` stores a **snapshot** of the slot,
because `ProtectHome=yes` means the daemon cannot read the user's `config.ini`.
Nothing ever refreshed it. `linux-scaffold.md` even documented this as intended
("Editing the profile afterwards does not update the boot snapshot — re-run the
command"), which is why it survived: it was written down as a limitation instead
of recognized as a silent revert. The TUI meanwhile rendered `PROFILE 1`, which
any user reads as "profile 1 as it is now".

The snapshot itself is not the mistake — the daemon genuinely cannot resolve
"slot N" — so the fix keeps it and makes it follow the profile:

- **v15 `SERVICE_CMD_REFRESH_STARTUP_PROFILE`**, content-only. It carries a slot
  and settings; the validator rejects it if it carries a `targetGpu` or a
  `startupMode`, and the daemon rebuilds the record from its **own** stored mode,
  slot, name and GPU binding. Rejected via `startup_snapshot_refresh_allowed()`
  unless a `profile` policy already names that slot.
  - Why a new command rather than re-sending `SET`: the client does not know
    which GPU the policy is bound to. Re-sending `SET` with the *currently
    selected* GPU would silently move the boot-apply to another card whenever a
    profile was saved from a different GPU's editor. A refresh that structurally
    cannot re-bind was worth the protocol bump.
- **Clients push after every profile write** (TUI Save, `--save-config`), always
  reloading the slot from disk rather than reusing the in-memory draft.
  Clearing the bound slot **unbinds** the policy instead of leaving a deleted
  profile applying at boot. An offline daemon is reported, not swallowed.
- **Divergence is still detected**, because hand-editing `config.ini` bypasses
  every push path: the snapshot is read back and compared with
  `desired_settings_equal()`; the TUI shows `PROFILE N STALE` in red and
  `--show-startup` prints both sides field by field.
- Only where the answer can have changed — TUI start, a write, a policy change —
  never on the 1 Hz telemetry tick.

Diagnosis cost far more than it should have: nothing logged what the boot apply
actually wrote. The daemon now logs the snapshot's own fan/offset/power values
on every set, refresh and boot apply, and the client logs every refresh and
every detected divergence.

Splitting fallout: the client half went into `linux_startup_sync.{h,cpp}` shared
by the TUI and CLI, which also kept `linux_tui_actions.cpp`, `linux_main.cpp`
and `linux_daemon.cpp` under the 800-line ratchet they had each just crossed.

Two traps found on the way, both now permanent: the systemd readiness assertion
spelled `"protocol 14"` as a literal (it now derives the number from
`SERVICE_PROTOCOL_VERSION`, so a bump cannot leave systemd advertising a stale
version with a green test), and the 1860–1894 exit-code range was already
F-LNX-VFSCROLL's — the new block is 1970–2005.

---

## 2026-07-29: protocol v14 had a producer on Linux only

Finishing the v14 work found the contract implemented on exactly one side. The
wire struct, the validator, the Linux daemon producer, the shared comparator,
the TUI and the text/JSON exports were all in place, and `overview.md` plus
`windows-architecture.md` already claimed that "configured intent never
substitutes for a failed hardware read". The Windows service never set any of
the five bits.

The effect was not a build break, which is why every local gate stayed green:
`ControlState` is zero-initialized, so Windows shipped `readbackValid = false`
everywhere. Under the documented contract that reads as *every domain is
permanently unavailable*, while `populate_control_state_locked()` went on
publishing `current_applied_gpu_offset_mhz()` — a function whose persisted-
request and active-desired branches deliberately return remembered intent. The
exact substitution v14 exists to prevent was still happening, now with a bit
next to it asserting the opposite.

The provenance is per domain and not uniform. GPU offset needed an out-parameter
on `current_applied_gpu_offset_mhz()` because two of its branches answer with
intent, and `detect_clock_offsets()` then replaces that scalar unconditionally —
including with the 0 that both "no offset" and "nothing readable" produce — so
detection, not the earlier NVML read, owns the final answer. Power additionally
requires a nonzero default and current, since a percentage from a missing
default is arithmetic. Fans record per-fan flags: a partial answer is unknown,
never a selective match.

That fan work exposed a real defect in the shared comparator. Windows only reads
the target duty while a fan is manual, so an external controller putting the
fans back on the automatic curve suppresses the very readback the comparator was
demanding before it would report anything — the override was downgraded to
"unknown". It now checks policy first and only requires a duty once the policy
matches. Linux never hit this because its capture reads the target
unconditionally, so the bug was invisible on the platform where the feature was
developed.

The source-size ratchet shaped the implementation, correctly. `gpu_backend.cpp`,
`gpu_backend_apply.cpp`, `main_gpu_state.cpp` and `main_runtime_nvml.cpp` all sit
at their pinned ceilings, so none of this could be written inline. The logic went
into the pure `source/control_readback_policy.h`, the shards kept only fact
recording, and the room was reclaimed by joining existing declarations. The
header being pure is why the Windows producer has direct unit coverage
(F-WIN-READBACK, 1740–1751) on a Linux host at all.

Two process notes worth keeping:

- `git checkout -- <file>` to undo an in-progress edit also discards *another
  agent's* uncommitted work in that file. It destroyed concurrent P0 offset changes
  in `main_runtime_nvml.cpp`; they were restorable only because the full diff was
  still in context. Uncommitted multi-agent state has no undo.
- Windows shards can be parsed from a Linux host with host `clang++` plus
  mingw-w64 headers. See the recipe in [../testing.md](../testing.md). It is a
  syntax gate, not a build, but it is the difference between "unverified" and
  "at least it parses" for the Windows-only half of a change. The installer
  security work was in the same unverified state and also parses clean.

---

## 2026-07-28: an external tool reset the clocks; Green Curve then displayed intent as live

The incident was two separate facts, and conflating them would have produced
the wrong fix.

The hardware chronology exonerated Green Curve's apply path: its profile
startup completed and verified five mutation phases, and the immediate
post-apply VF readback still carried the custom tail. Another GPU tool on the same machine
initialized roughly 100 ms later and reset the clocks; its configuration
contained no offset/curve values, so the reset remained. The TUI's first later
refresh was merely when the replaced state was observed. Disabling that tool
stopped the competing writer.

Green Curve still had a presentation bug: when `activeDesiredValid` was set,
the Linux editor copied `response.desired` and labeled it “live state” even
though the same response carried fresh hardware values in `controlState` and
the VF snapshot. Active desired is Green Curve ownership/configuration metadata,
not proof that no other process wrote afterward.

The new invariant is explicit:

- editable fields preserve configured intent;
- the TUI and text/JSON exports separately show hardware readback;
- GPU/memory/power/fan and owned VF/lock domains are classified as matching,
  overridden, or unavailable;
- expected VF boost/temperature movement has a 30 MHz presentation tolerance;
- detecting an override never queues a reapply. Only explicit Apply writes.

Protocol v14 makes the distinction fail-closed by carrying explicit validity
for each scalar hardware read. An unreadable GPU/memory offset, power limit, fan
policy, or fan target is `unavailable`; it cannot fall back to the configured
value and masquerade as a match. A Reset mutation also clears the prior
curve/VF ownership from the retained active intent before any downstream
request adds new points, preventing stale custom curve data from surviving a
successful reset.

The same audit found a portability risk in modern NVML offset handling. The API
is P-state-scoped, but both platforms followed the transient current P-state.
An idle P8 write could therefore succeed while leaving performance P0
unchanged. Green Curve now consistently reads, writes, verifies and rolls back
P0. Linux capture prefers `nvmlDeviceGetClockOffsets` and retains the deprecated
global getters only as fallback.

Release-gate follow-through mattered. Linux clang-tidy had been trying to run a
Windows executable/compile command and was advisory even when it did run. The
runner is now host-correct and a hard baseline ratchet. Its first real Linux
pass exposed object-representation `memcmp`s over `termios` and
`DesiredSettings`, static `strerror()` storage retained across two calls,
header-defined external functions, and duplicate-branch code; all were fixed.
CI now runs native tidy, bounded fuzzing, and full verified release packaging
on both Windows and Linux.

The diff security audit also found and fixed four privilege-boundary defects:
Windows setup no longer executes a captured helper from a user-writable temp
parent, accepts only direct Program Files install roots and hardens the target
before extraction, and never truncates/follows a caller-selected log path.
Windows pipe failures disclose the coherent GPU/user state envelope only after
active-session/PID/integrity authorization. The root Linux wrapper now creates
the per-user desktop entry entirely under that user's token, closing a planted
symlink overwrite. All five candidates (including the duplicate log instance)
were suppressed by the final working-tree controls.

---

## 2026-07-28: the install told you to join a group it had just added you to

Reported as "I'd like the install script to add the user to the greencurve
group". It already did, and had — the group existed on the dev box with the
account in it, and the socket was `root:greencurve 0660`. The request was really
about the *messaging*, which is what the follow-up question ("it confusingly
mentions that users should add themselves?") pinned down.

`--service-install` ended with an unconditional "To control the GPU without
sudo, add your user: sudo usermod -aG greencurve $USER". The wrapper runs
`--service-install` first and enrolls afterwards — it has to, the group does not
exist until that step creates it — so the instruction printed on the line
immediately before the script carried it out. A re-install by an already
enrolled user got the same prescription for a non-problem.

The rule was already written down elsewhere in this codebase.
`linux_daemon_transport.cpp` gates its own remedy behind an actual `access()`
failure, commented *"advertising usermod on a run that works would send someone
chasing a non-problem"*. The installer simply never followed it. Worth
remembering: when a message feels wrong, check whether the project already
decided the principle somewhere adjacent.

Now a pure decision (`linux_group_enrollment_advice`, assertions 1950-1965):
already-enrolled confirms access with no command; a resolved account gets its
name in the command rather than `"$USER"`; an unresolved one gets the generic
form. Membership reads `/etc/group` **and** the primary GID, since a user whose
primary group is `greencurve` never appears in `gr_mem`. The wrapper sets
`GREENCURVE_SETUP_OWNS_GROUP=1` to suppress the paragraph entirely when it owns
the enrollment.

**Second defect found while reading:** `target_user()`'s comment promised a
fallback to "the owner of the login session" that did not exist — the code
checked `GREENCURVE_USER` and `SUDO_USER` and then returned empty, so a plain
root shell enrolled nobody. Added `logname` and a sole-`loginctl`-session
fallback behind a `usable_account()` filter (exists, not root, UID ≥ 1000).
Ambiguity yields nothing on purpose: enrollment grants GPU control, so guessing
between two logged-in users is worse than asking.

Testing note worth keeping: the first pass at exercising the resolution chain
was worthless. Every rejection case fell through to `logname`, which returns the
right answer on this machine, so all six cases "passed" without proving
anything. Re-ran it with `logname`/`loginctl` stubbed to fail, which made the
three rejection paths actually observable. A test where every row passes for the
same incidental reason is not a test.

---

## 2026-07-28: the duplicated fan curve was an out-of-bounds write

The fan-curve duplication had been filed as low-risk cleanup. It was not. It was
ranked without diffing the two copies — the lesson is that "duplicate code" is
not a severity estimate, and the diff is cheap.

**Two defects, both proven.** `linux_port.cpp`'s `fan_curve_normalize` handled
the `enabledCount < 2` branch by setting `enabledCount = 2` and *falling
through*, where `fan_curve.cpp` resets to the default curve and returns. So:

- The disabled-point loop then writes `config->points[enabledCount + i]` up to
  `points[8]` (one enabled point) or `points[9]` (none), in an 8-element array.
  Reproduced as a 12-byte ASan stack-buffer-overflow. Reachable from the daemon
  socket, profile load, and the TUI. The daemon runs as root.
- The curve it built kept only the first two default points, so it capped at
  **35% fan at every temperature above 45 °C** where the tested implementation
  reaches 90% at 84 °C. On a fan controller.

**Why the new fuzzing could not have found it.** The `service_request` target
stops at `validate_service_request_for_ipc()`, which canonicalizes the `enabled`
flags but never requires two of them — it *accepts* the input. The defect is one
step downstream, in the daemon's own normalize call. Recorded in testing.md as
the most valuable remaining fuzz work: a target that drives post-validation
request handling.

**The merge had a real hazard of its own.** `fan_curve.cpp` wrote the degree
sign as `\xB0` (CP-1252, what Windows ANSI GDI wants); `linux_port.cpp` wrote
`\xC2\xB0` (UTF-8, what the TUI wants). Linking the Windows-encoded one into the
Linux build would have put an invalid UTF-8 byte into terminal output. Added
`GC_DEGREE` to `platform.h`, plus a guard forbidding the hardcoded form and an
assertion that validates every byte of the summary as UTF-8 on Linux.

**Also fixed: bare `python build.py` died on Linux** with a `PermissionError`
traceback out of `subprocess`, because the default `--target all` walked into
the Windows build and tried to execute `llvm-rc.exe`, a PE binary.
`resolve_targets()` now narrows the *default* to Linux with a printed reason,
and makes an *explicit* `--target windows`/`all` a clean exit 1. Resolved before
any toolchain download or build-number bump, so an impossible request costs
nothing.

Two negative-control notes worth keeping: the first attempt at the
"fan_curve.cpp missing from the Linux link" control silently patched
`WINDOWS_SOURCE_FILES` instead (`str.replace(..., 1)` takes the first match, and
the Windows list comes first in build.py) and the suite passed, which looked
like a broken guard but was a broken test. Always assert the patch applied.
Incidentally that also showed there is no guard on the Windows source list —
the Windows link failure is the only thing catching it there.

Still open: no hardware pass since the fan fix, and the Windows build has only
been cross-compiled on this host, never linked or run.

---

## 2026-07-28: the shared text parsers, and a duplicate the linker forced out

Follow-up to the gate work below. Moving `parse_fan_value`,
`parse_cli_point_arg_w` and `config_section_header_matches_ascii` out of the
Win32-only `config_utils.cpp` into `config_text_utils.cpp`, to unlock the
`config_strings` fuzz target on Linux and delete `linux_port.cpp`'s private
`parse_fan_value`.

**The scope could not stay at `parse_fan_value`.** It was one of *five*
duplicates: `linux_port.cpp` also carried verbatim `trim_ascii`,
`streqi_ascii`, `parse_int_strict` and `set_message`. Deleting only
`parse_fan_value` is impossible, because making the Linux binary use the shared
one means putting `config_text_utils.cpp` into `LINUX_SOURCE_FILES`, and the
moment it is there the other four collide at link. So either all five go or
none do. All five went — they were behaviourally identical (`gc_vsnprintf` is a
thin `vsnprintf` wrapper on Linux; `StringCchCopyA` and `gc_snprintf("%s")`
truncate at the same boundary), so nothing changed except what is tested.

**`config_text_utils.cpp` already compiled for Linux untouched.** The initial
assumption was that it would need a new header, because it `#include`s
`app_shared.h`, which looks Win32. It is not: `app_shared.h` includes
`win32_compat.h` itself on non-Windows. Worth checking before designing around
a header split that is not needed.

**Two Win32 string calls did have to go**, and neither is in `win32_compat.h`:
`StringCchCopyA` → `gc_snprintf("%s")`, and `_strnicmp` → an explicit ASCII
fold. The fold is the one with a real failure mode: it must stop at a NUL, or
`config_section_header_matches_ascii("[prof", "profiles")` reads past the end of
the line buffer. Assertions 1900-1902 pin exactly that, and they run under
ASan/UBSan.

**What the move exposed:** `parse_fan_value` had *zero* regression coverage on
either platform, while the Linux daemon ran an untested second copy of it. It
now has 13 assertions. Two existing blocks (`parse_cli_point_arg_w` 21-25, the
section-header cases 523-527) were `#if defined(_WIN32)` purely because the
symbols were unreachable on Linux; both are now platform-neutral and run on both
hosts.

A source guard fails the build if `linux_port.cpp` defines any of the five
again, matching the definition signature rather than any mention so call sites
stay legal. Negative control performed. `config_strings` is now in
`FUZZ_LINUX_TARGETS` with `win32_compat.h` force-included (the harness names
`WCHAR` and must stay unmodified for Windows), so four of five fuzz targets run
on Linux; only `task_xml` is left, and unlocking it means making
`main_startup_task_definition.cpp` build without `windows.h`.

Still open: `linux_port.cpp` duplicates the fan-curve math. That is the last of
the duplication gap and the one with genuine behavioural risk.

---

## 2026-07-28: the Linux host gates were never actually running

Prompted by "we fuzzed on Windows; is that enough now that we're on bare metal
Arch?" It was not — three separate gates were passing vacuously or absent.

**Fuzzing had never run on Linux at all.** `run_fuzz_targets()` opened with an
unconditional `return 0` on non-Windows: it printed a note and reported success
without compiling anything. Every recorded fuzz result came from Windows builds.
Two of the five targets are *Linux daemon code* (`vf_snapshot`, `wire_prefix`)
and `service_request` is the gate on the daemon's Unix socket, so the host is a
real variable, not a formality — LLP64 vs LP64, different libc, different
allocator under ASan. Three targets now build on Linux with no extra translation
unit; the other two link Win32-only shards and are refused by name.

**`--test --asan` was broken on Linux, silently.** Zig ships `tsan` but no ASan
runtime, so the link died on undefined `__asan_register_elf_globals`. Plain
`--test` passed, the ASan variant failed to build, and nobody noticed because
the two are separate invocations. The daemon, transport and TUI had never seen
ASan. Sanitizer builds now resolve a host `clang++`; plain `--test` still uses
the pinned Zig, so the hermetic path is unchanged.

**The Linux binary shipped with zero `endbr64`.** `-fcf-protection=full` sat in
`WINDOWS_FLAGS` only, and `--check-cet` parses PE load-config, so no gate ever
looked at the ELF side. Now 551. This is worse than the Windows gap the page
already documented: Windows does not enforce user-mode IBT, but this box
(Zen 3) reports `user_shstk`, so the backward edge is genuinely enforceable
here. `-ftrivial-auto-var-init=pattern` was Windows-only too.

Three things worth remembering:

- **The property note cannot be forced, and should not be faked.** Our objects
  do carry `IBT, SHSTK`; `ld.lld` intersects the property across all inputs and
  the Zig CRT objects are unmarked, so the linked image has none and no loader
  will enable either edge. `zig cc` rejects `-z shstk` and `-z force-ibt`
  outright. The gate reports this state instead of asserting it — promising
  SHSTK on behalf of vendor objects is a claim we cannot back.
- **`endbr64` is not a per-function invariant.** The first version of the ELF
  gate required it on every function symbol and failed on *correct* output: the
  compiler emits it only at indirect-branch targets, so 136 of our 439 functions
  correctly have none. The per-object property bits are the exact signal.
- **The gate found a latent portability bug immediately.** An early probe built
  with host clang instead of Zig failed on `abort()` undeclared in
  `linux_crash_breadcrumb.h` — it had no `<stdlib.h>`, and compiled only because
  Zig's bundled headers pull it in transitively. Fixed at the source.

`--check-cet` grew an ELF branch (object-level attribution; ELF has no Guard CF
table to walk). Release artifacts gained an x64 `endbr64` gate alongside the
existing ARM64 BTI/PAC one, so this class of regression now fails every build
rather than waiting for someone to run the opt-in check. Negative controls
performed on both new gates. Note `--check --target windows` cannot run from a
Linux host at all: `llvm-rc.exe` is a PE binary. Pre-existing, unrelated.

Open: no deep (1M+) Linux fuzz session yet; `config_strings` stays Windows-only
until `parse_fan_value` / `parse_cli_point_arg_w` /
`config_section_header_matches_ascii` move into `config_text_utils.cpp`, which
would also delete `linux_port.cpp`'s duplicate `parse_fan_value`.

---

## 2026-07-28: Linux desktop integration, a real debug log, and a configurable boot apply

Five reported gaps, plus one found on the way. The parts worth remembering are
the ones that were not obvious going in.

**The "extra keypress to get the terminal back" was a real byte-stream defect,
not a Konsole quirk to shrug at.** Reproduced on hardware and then bisected by
capturing the exit stream through a pty and replaying it into a real Konsole:
the last write ended *exactly* at `ESC[?1049l`, and Konsole then did not paint
the next write until further output arrived. Appending any byte after the
alternate-screen exit fixes it; so does removing either the duplicated restore
or the empty synchronized-update batches. Rather than pick one, all three
wasteful behaviours were removed, because each is wrong on its own merits:

1. `tui_render()` emitted `ESC[?2026h ESC[0m ESC[?2026l` even when the row diff
   was empty, and the one-second live refresh made that the *usual* case — four
   such batches were on the wire before the last exit.
2. The event loop rendered one more frame after `q` had already set
   `running = false`.
3. The supervisor repeated the child's entire presentation restore
   unconditionally. It now compares termios against the pre-fork copy and only
   restores when the child left it modified — the child is already reaped at
   that point, so nothing races.

The restore now ends with `\r\n`, which also puts the shell prompt on a clean
line. Verified in a real Konsole: the prompt appears immediately.

**A desktop launch is "no tty *and* a display", not "no tty".** Getting that
backwards would break `greencurve --tui > log` and every ssh/cron invocation.
`linux_terminal_should_relaunch()` requires both streams to be non-tty, so a
single redirected stream is still a terminal launch. The terminal table only
lists emulators with a documented argv flag (`-e`, `-x`, `--`, bare, `start --`)
so the launcher can `execvp()` a fixed vector and never build a shell string.

**The daemon cannot read the user's config.ini.** `ProtectHome=yes` makes the
obvious design for a boot profile — "store slot N, resolve it at startup" —
impossible. The client resolves the profile and ships a *snapshot* bound to one
exact GPU identity; the daemon stores it in `/var/lib/greencurve/startup.bin`
with the same checksummed root-owned atomic write as `active.bin`. Same reason
the daemon's debug log lives in the state directory: `ProtectSystem=full` mounts
`/usr` read-only, so it physically cannot log next to its own binary.

**A corrupt boot policy must not fall back to the old behaviour.** Downgrading
"unreadable policy" to "replay the committed intent" would write to the GPU
under a policy the administrator cannot read back. It fails closed to
apply-nothing and says so in the log.

**The crash breadcrumb now reaches a file.** It was stderr-only, which a
desktop-launched TUI does not have. It appends to the already-open debug log
descriptor — `open()` is async-signal-safe but formatting a fresh crash-file
name is not, so the descriptor is established up front. That forced log rotation
to preserve the descriptor *number* via `dup2()`: a closed-and-reallocated fd
could later be the daemon's control socket, and a signal handler would then
write text into it. Green Curve still writes no dump of its own on Linux; the
core file remains the kernel's job (`coredumpctl`), which `--probe` now reports.

**Wheel scrolling took three fixes, not one, and the first two looked like
"the list wraps back to the beginning".** Fixing only the clamp was not enough,
which is exactly what the second bug report said. Driving real SGR wheel events
through a pty and reading back which VF indices were on screen showed the shape
immediately: with selected point 76 and 16 visible rows the view walked
61 -> 64 -> ... -> 76 and jumped straight back to 61, forever.

1. Both scroll paths clamped to `VF_NUM_POINTS - 1` instead of the last page of
   *populated* points (`tui_vf_max_first_visible()`).
2. `reveal_selected_point()` ran on **every** render, so any scroll that pushed
   the selection off screen was undone on the very next frame. Now gated on
   `tui_selection_needs_reveal()` — reveal on select, never on render.
3. `tui_refresh_service()` clamped `vfScroll` down to `selectedPoint` once per
   second, so even after (2) the view snapped back on every live-refresh tick.

The lesson worth keeping: "keep the selection visible" was implemented three
times in three places, and each copy silently fought the user's scrolling. There
is now one rule and one clamp. The fan list had the same class of defect.

**The permission diagnostic existed but never reached the log.** A user who is
not in the `greencurve` group got one truncated status row for one frame. Every
`linux_daemon_send()` failure path now logs its full text (deduplicated, because
the TUI polls at 1 Hz), and `linux_daemon_log_client_environment()` records
group membership and socket owner/mode once per process *before* anything can
fail — so the log answers "was this user in the group" even for runs that
worked. The remedy line is emitted only when `access()` actually fails, and is
selected by cause; printing `usermod` advice on a healthy run would send someone
chasing a non-problem. The daemon side now names a rejected caller's pid,
command and version instead of only setting `invalid protocol fields`.

**Ratchet fallout, fixed by splitting rather than raising.** Protocol v13 pushed
`service_protocol.h` to 858 lines and the policy work pushed `linux_daemon.cpp`
to 948. Extractions: `service_protocol_validation.h` (the envelope/response
validators; the guards address it and `service_protocol.h` as one surface),
`linux_startup_policy.h` (the whole boot-policy feature), `tools/linux_gates.py`
(the F-LNX-TUI group plus the new guards), and `tools/release_manifest.py` (the
archive allowlist).

**Packaging had to learn that the binary now writes next to itself.** Running
the freshly built binary out of `dist/` leaves `greencurve_debug.txt` there, and
the payload allowlist is exact in both directions, so that alone would fail the
build. Runtime artifacts are *removed* before the manifest check rather than
whitelisted — the debug log records GPU identifiers and applied settings and
must never end up inside a release archive.

Verified: `python build.py --test`, the Linux x64 + arm64 build and archives,
and every Windows TU (GUI, service, arm64, installer) cross-compiled clean with
`-Werror`. The Windows *link* still cannot run on a Linux host.

On hardware (RTX 5070, build 17): the TUI exit shows the shell prompt with no
extra keypress in a real Konsole; a no-tty launch with a live Wayland display
opens Konsole and closes it again on `q`; the client debug log appears next to
the binary and the daemon's in `/var/lib/greencurve/`. The v13 daemon starts
healthy, takes the default no-record path (`no startup policy configured;
restoring the last committed intent` -> `Applied: 5 phase(s), 0 failed`), and
commits both a `none` and a `profile 1` policy. **Still unobserved: a restart
with a non-default policy already in place** — `none` suppressing the replay and
`profile` performing the boot apply — plus the corrupt-record path and the TUI
cycle control.

---

## 2026-07-27: Custom Win32 installer replaces NSIS; Windows archives become "Green Curve"

`installer.nsi` and `generate_version_nsh()` are gone. `python build.py` now
emits `greencurve-<version>-windows-<arch>-setup.exe` for x64 and arm64 next to
the archives, built from `source/installer_*` with the project's own hardening
flags. Full details in [installer.md](../installer.md); the notes worth keeping
here are the ones that were not obvious going in.

**Compression without a third-party library.** The constraint ruled out zlib,
LZMA, and friends, and hand-writing an inflate decoder would put a novel entropy
decoder between a download and a service binary in Program Files. cabinet.dll's
Compression API (`CreateCompressor`/`Decompress`, XPRESS_HUFF) is an OS service,
reachable from Python via `ctypes` at build time and from the installer at run
time — the same implementation on both sides, so a round trip cannot disagree.
`tools/installer_build.py` decompresses every payload twice before writing a
setup file. Payload lands around 58% of raw.

**The re-apply had to not become a second restore path.** The obvious design —
have the service replay its persisted intent after the upgrade — would directly
contradict [auto-restore-policy.md](../auto-restore-policy.md) ("a persisted
settings snapshot is intent, not permission to write"). Instead the *client*
carries it: `--export-active-settings` before the upgrade,
`--apply-settings-file` after, the second one performing a perfectly ordinary
explicit CLI Apply. The service gains nothing new. Two consequences worth
remembering: the export deliberately **fails** when nothing is applied (so an
upgrade with a stock GPU does not "restore" a synthesized profile), and the
*old* binary does the export, so client and service always match and the
installer never learns the protocol version.

**Ordering, not features, is what makes an upgrade correct.** Capture while the
old build still runs; stop the GUI with `WM_CLOSE` (its real shutdown path, which
releases the tray icon, mutex, and pipe) rather than terminating it; wait on the
*service process handle* rather than polling SCM status, because process exit is
both a real event and the thing that unlocks the binary; register the service
before re-applying. Three `require_order_in_operation` gates pin this. First
attempt used plain `require_order`, which passed on definition order rather than
call order — anchoring to `gc_install_execute` was the fix.

**A moved installation is three separate pointers.** Files, the SCM ImagePath,
and shortcuts. The ImagePath is re-pointed by running the *new* binary's
`--service-install` (it owns `CreateService`/`ChangeServiceConfig`, the failure
actions, and the DACL hardening — reimplementing any of it in setup would fork
the security model) and then **reading the registration back** to confirm it
moved. The old folder's protected DACL is reverted to inherited so the user can
actually delete the leftovers, which are deliberately not removed.

**Two things the SCM knows that the registry does not.** A hand-registered
portable copy has no Add/Remove Programs entry, so `gc_read_prior_install()`
treats a registered service with no registry record as a prior install too;
otherwise a fresh install over it would leave two registrations.

**Ratchet fallout, fixed by splitting rather than raising.** The change pushed
`entry.cpp`, `main_runtime_nvml.cpp`, `main.cpp`, and `installer_ui.cpp` over
their limits. Extractions: `main_cli_admin.cpp` (the six machine-wide admin
commands, which all reported identically in six copies), `main_cli_options.cpp`
(`parse_cli_options`, a fifth of a file it had nothing to do with),
`installer_ui_pages.cpp` (layout + painting), and both new CLI shards moved from
`main.cpp` into the `main_shell.cpp` include hub. `entry.cpp` 916 -> 861 and
`main_runtime_nvml.cpp` 1105 -> 901; both ratchets lowered to match.

**Palette extracted.** `theme_palette.h` is now included by both `app_shared.h`
and the installer. Two colours that existed only as repeated literals
(`COL_DISABLED_BORDER`, `COL_BUTTON_LABEL`) are named there and the app's
literals replaced, so setup and program cannot drift.

**First live run found two defects unit tests could not.** The user installed
the result immediately, which was worth more than any amount of further static
review. (1) No shortcuts were created: `CoCreateInstance(ShellLink)` returned
`CO_E_NOTINITIALIZED`, because `CoInitializeEx` is per-thread and only the UI
thread had called it — the install runs on a worker. (2) The settings capture
blocked for its full 60 s timeout, because it ran the *installed* binary, and a
build predating `--export-active-settings` treats it as an unknown argument and
falls through to opening the GUI. Fixes: the worker initializes COM itself
(apartment-threaded, matching the shell link handler, so no marshalling), the
capture unpacks and runs the *payload's* greencurve.exe instead, and
`gc_run_and_wait()` now terminates any child that outlives its timeout so setup
can never leave a stray process holding files it is about to replace. Both are
now gated by source checks.

**Second live run: the restore itself was rejected.** The transfer ran and wrote
the right values (`gpu=475MHz lockCi=76 curvePoints=57 fanMode=2`), but
`greencurve_cli_log.txt` said `Service request contains invalid protocol fields`.
A `SERVICE_CMD_APPLY` is only valid with `expectedServiceInstanceId`,
`expectedGpuGeneration`, a valid `targetGpu`, and (for VF changes) a topology
signature — all projected onto app state by `apply_ready_service_envelope_to_app()`
after a READY envelope. `handle_cli()`'s normal apply path fetches that in its
preamble; `settings_transfer_apply()` called `apply_desired_settings()` directly
and skipped it, so all four went out zero. Behind that sat a genuine race: setup
applies straight after `--service-install`, and the SCM reports RUNNING before
the GPU phase is READY — 18 ms apart in the captured run. The fix fetches a READY
envelope with a bounded retry (a *prerequisite* wait, which the auto-restore
contract permits; the write still happens once), validates the configured GPU,
then applies. The failure was also invisible: a failed restore does not fail the
install, so no log was written and the final page said nothing — the outcome is
now reported there either way.

Verified: `--test`, `--test --asan`, and full `python build.py` pass. Two
in-place upgrades confirmed the install itself; the restore fix is **not yet
confirmed on a real upgrade**. A moving upgrade, a fresh install, silent mode,
and the uninstaller are still unexercised.

---

## 2026-07-27: Fan control never worked on Linux; the TUI hid every power target above 100%

Two independent defects, both found by dumping what the driver actually returns
rather than trusting the readback we happened to call.

**Manual fan writes were verified against the wrong NVML entry point.**
`--fan-mode fixed --fan-fixed 35` reported "Apply failed: 4 phase(s), 1 failed
(rolled back)" with `fan: fixed write/readback failed for fan 0/1`. A standalone
probe against the live driver showed the write itself was fine:

```
fan0 setFanSpeed_v2(35)    -> 0 (Success)
   getFanSpeed_v2          -> pct=0     <- measured; fan is stopped
   getTargetFanSpeed       -> pct=35    <- intent; exactly what we wrote
   getFanControlPolicy_v2  -> policy=1  <- MANUAL
```

`nvmlDeviceGetFanSpeed_v2` is *measured* telemetry. It reads 0 for as long as
the zero-RPM fan stop holds, and overshoots while the fan accelerates — a
settled 55% duty measured 59%. The Linux backend gated on
`measured == requested`, so on an idle GPU **every** fixed/curve apply was
declared a failure and rolled back. `nvmlDeviceGetTargetFanSpeed` is the actual
readback, and Linux never even resolved it. Windows already had the right idea
in `manual_fan_readback_matches_target()`; that predicate is now the shared
`fan_manual_write_confirmed()` in `fan_runtime_policy.h` and both platforms call
it. The rollback snapshot had the same bug in a nastier form: it captured the
*measured* duty as the value to restore, so a rollback would have pinned the GPU
at whatever the tachometer happened to read.

Same bug class as f3bc0d8 (memory-offset grid snap) and f7ce6e2 (lock frequency
compared exactly). That is now three exact-equality readback gates against a
driver that does not promise exact echoes — worth suspecting the next one too.

**The driver also clamps a duty into its advertised range.**
`nvmlDeviceGetMinMaxFanSpeed` reports 30..100 here; writing 10% succeeds and
reports an intent of 30. Green Curve now clamps up front
(`fan_manual_effective_percent`) and verifies the clamped value. The daemon had
been publishing a hardcoded `fanMinPct=0 / fanMaxPct=100`, so the UI could offer
duties the driver would silently ignore.

**The power limit ran through a 0..100 percent clamp.**
`normalize_desired_settings_for_ui()` used the generic `clamp_percent()`, but the
power limit is a percentage *of the board default* — 105% of 250 W = 262.5 W,
well inside the 300 W ceiling. The clamp ran on load, on save, and on every TUI
refresh, so `--save-config --power-limit 105` stored `power_limit_pct=100`, and
the TUI redisplayed the daemon's live 105% as 100%. The canonical 50..150 range
already existed in three other places (`validate_desired_settings_for_ipc`, the
apply gate, the TUI spinner) with the bounds hardcoded at each; they now share
`POWER_LIMIT_MIN_PCT`/`POWER_LIMIT_MAX_PCT`/`clamp_power_limit_pct` in
`gpu_core.h`.

The normalizer was untestable where it lived: `linux_port.cpp` is not part of
the pure regression harness, so nothing pinned its clamps. It moved to
`desired_settings_ui_policy.h` (pure, header-only) and the harness now asserts
that a 105% target survives normalization.

**Logging.** Curve mode re-asserts every poll interval, so the write outcome and
the duty-range line are transition-gated. Gating on the *effective* duty matters:
a curve wandering across 27/28/29% while the 30% floor pins all three produces no
lines. Verified at two lines per minute with a 500 ms interval.

**Verified on hardware** (RTX 5070, Arch, driver 610.43.03): fixed 35% and 55%
(`nvidia-smi` confirms), curve mode tracking temperature, driver-range clamp,
power limit 105% -> 262.50 W with the TUI showing `[ 105 ]`, reset handing the
fan back to auto, and the `systemctl stop` handback.

**Build note:** the Windows target cannot be built on this Linux host —
`llvm-mingw/bin/*.exe` are PE binaries. Every Windows TU was instead
cross-compiled to objects with the bundled Zig (`-target x86_64-windows-gnu`),
which covers all the shards since `main.cpp` `#include`s them; only the final
link and `llvm-rc` resource step are unreachable.

**Housekeeping:** `build.py` was one line over its size ratchet at HEAD (commit
2884c78 added a source-list entry without adjusting it). Rather than raising the
bar, the fan guards moved into `tools/fan_gates.py` (the pattern
`tools/ui_gates.py` and `tools/security_gates.py` already established), and
`linux_backend.cpp` — pushed to 845 lines by this fix — was split by extracting
`linux_backend_nvml_write.cpp`.

---

## 2026-07-27: Reset never worked on Linux; odd memory offsets rolled back every apply

Found while exercising the installed daemon on the RTX 5070.

**Reset was rejected 100% of the time**, in both the CLI and the TUI, with
"invalid protocol fields". `send_simple()` set `SERVICE_REQUEST_FLAG_INTERACTIVE`
for every command, but `validate_service_request_for_ipc()` requires `flags == 0`
on RESET. The Windows client already builds its reset request with no flags, and
the daemon only consumes the interactive bit on the APPLY path, so Linux was the
outlier and the validator was right. Both clients now go through
`service_request_flags_for_command()`.

**Odd memory offsets failed the whole apply.** `--mem-offset 15` reported
"Apply failed: 1 phase(s), 1 failed (rolled back)" with nothing in the journal
identifying the phase. Probing NVML directly showed the driver ACCEPTS every
value (`ret=0`) and snaps it to a grid, then reports the snapped value:

| domain | grid | measured |
|---|---|---|
| graphics | 1 MHz (exact) | 0..8 and -6..0 all round-trip |
| memory | 2 MHz toward zero | 15->14, 1->0, 3->2, -5->-4, -1->0 |

The Linux verification demanded an exact readback, so a perfectly good write was
declared a failure. Windows already tolerates this and only reports inexactness.
Verification now accepts a snap toward zero of less than one grid step, still
fails closed on overshoot/sign-flip/full-step, and logs the snap.

Same bug class as f7ce6e2 ("compare the lock frequency within one VF grid step,
not exactly") — worth checking any remaining exact-equality readback gate.

**Measurement caution:** the driver's *base* VF curve drifts a few MHz between
reads with GPU state, so comparing two separately-timed dumps invents apparent
quantization. An initial reading that GPC offsets snapped to 15 MHz was wrong;
within a single `--dump-live` snapshot `live == base + offset` exactly for +10,
+20 and +30. Always compare base and live from the same snapshot.

**Verified working on hardware:** daemon install/upgrade + socket
`root:greencurve 0660`, systemd sandboxing (the retained directives do not block
the driver — previously untested), full 127-point VF read, global GPU offset,
per-point VF edit (only the requested point moves), memory offset, power limit
(90% -> 225 W, confirmed by nvidia-smi), reset to stock, profile save/apply
across slots, text/JSON exports, `--write-assets`, and all three TUI tabs
including Reset via the button. Fan control was deliberately not exercised.

---

## 2026-07-27: RTX 5070 hardware acceptance — two ABI defects made the GPU uncontrollable

First real Linux hardware run (Arch, GB205 RTX 5070, driver 610.43.03). The GPU
was recognized by name but every control path failed closed. Two independent
root causes, both found by dumping what the driver actually returns rather than
trusting the struct declarations.

**1. `nvmlPciInfo_t` had the wrong ABI.** It declared a leading `busId[32]`;
the real NVML layout is `busIdLegacy[16]`, then domain/bus/device/pciDeviceId/
pciSubSystemId, then `busId[32]` at offset 36. Every integer was read 16 bytes
early, so `pciDeviceId`/`pciSubSystemId` came out as ASCII sliced from the
trailing bus-id string — `0x3a37303a` is `":07:"` and `0x302e3030` is `"00.0"`.
Discovery then reported `sole GPU identity conflict` and refused to bind.

The bug hid because `sscanf(pci.busId, ...)` accidentally still worked: at
offset 0 the wrong declaration lands on `busIdLegacy`, which holds a valid
`"0000:07:00.0"`. So bus/slot were right while the IDs were garbage, which is
why the conflict looked like a driver quirk rather than a layout error.

Verified byte-exact against the live driver before changing anything (a 68-byte
dump of all three entry points). NvAPI's `device=2f0410de subsystem=89e71043`
match NVML's exactly once decoded correctly. Added `offsetof`/`sizeof` asserts
so a future ABI drift fails the build. Windows was also affected: it resolved
only `nvmlDeviceGetPciInfo` (v1), which never fills `pciSubSystemId` — it now
prefers `_v3` via the shared `nvml_resolve_pci_info()`.

**2. The VF status table concatenates every clock domain.** After the binding
fix, health became `point 127 voltage topology is not ordered`. Dumping the raw
table showed why: entries 0..126 are the graphics curve (180 MHz @ 450 mV ..
3187 MHz @ 1240 mV), 127 starts a second domain at 405 MHz @ 540 mV, 128 a
third, and 129..131 are the ~14 GHz GDDR7 memory domain. `numClocks` (15) is
the domain count, not a point count. The code assumed the graphics curve
occupied exactly the first `VF_NUM_POINTS`; here it is 127.

Ruled out the two other candidate discriminators explicitly: the 128-bit
editable mask is all-ones and does not mark the boundary, and the trailing
per-entry dword is not a domain terminator (set on 126/127/128, clear on 131,
the last memory point). Voltage ascends within a domain and drops at a
boundary, so `linux_vf_graphics_domain_length()` takes the leading
non-decreasing run and zeroes the rest — unpopulated points are already skipped
by validation and by the write path, so a foreign domain can be neither
published nor written. That also removes a latent hazard: previously a "point
127" edit would have written into another clock domain.

`--self-test` now reports PASS, healthy, fresh 127-point snapshot.

**Testing infrastructure.** `python build.py --test` could not run on a Linux
host at all — it fed Win32-only TUs to a native compiler. Win32-behavior suites
are now `#ifdef`-gated and everything platform-neutral runs on both hosts; see
`testing.md`. No extra toolchain was needed (the bundled Zig is a full native
compiler and also cross-compiles Windows PE without wine). Also fixed: the
harness aliased assertion codes through the 8-bit POSIX exit status (1670 read
as 134, a real assertion number), and `build.py` re-downloaded the entire Zig
toolchain on **every** Linux invocation because the pinned executable digest
was Windows-only.

Follow-up: `linux_port.cpp` duplicates the fan-curve math and
`set_message`/`parse_int_strict`, so Windows tests validate code the Linux
daemon never runs.

## 2026-07-27: Unchecking "share slot N with all users" left the tick on screen (F-CHECKBOX-PAINT)

Reported: unchecking the box only shows as unchecked after a GUI restart;
checking it works. The machine config was never wrong — only the pixels.

The tick is derived on every paint (`themed_checkbox_checked_state()` re-reads
`is_machine_profile_slot_saved(slot) && machineLogonSlotCache == slot`), but the
repaint gate asked the control via `BM_GETCHECK`. These are `BS_OWNERDRAW`
buttons; button type bits are mutually exclusive, so Windows stores no check
state and always answers `BST_UNCHECKED`. That made the gate directional:
sharing compared "checked" vs. a permanent "unchecked", saw a difference, and
invalidated; unsharing compared "unchecked" vs. "unchecked", reported "no
change", and never invalidated. The caption (same slot), the enable state
(`assignmentPresent || reachable` is true either way), and visibility were all
unchanged too, so nothing else in `update_share_all_users_check_state()` set
`changed`. The user32 button repaints itself on `WM_LBUTTONUP` *before* sending
`BN_CLICKED` — i.e. with the still-shared state — so those stale pixels were
exactly a tick. Restart repaints from scratch, hence the reported workaround.

The INI path was ruled out by elimination rather than assumed: the painter
re-reads the machine config live, so *sharing* showing a tick already proves the
read is fresh across the elevated child process.

This is the same defect the auto-profile dialog hit on 2026-07-12; the guards
added then were scoped to `auto_profile_dialog.cpp` only, so the main window
still had it. Fixed the same way, with the loop closed one step further:
`draw_themed_button()` now records what it painted into a `UiCheckboxState`
mirror in `AppState`, and projections ask `ui_checkbox_state_needs_repaint()`.
Recording in the painter (not at the projection) makes the mirror unable to
drift — an out-of-band `RDW_ALLCHILDREN` redraw updates it too.
`gui_set_button_check_if_changed()` was deleted rather than repaired.

Swept the same class elsewhere. Every remaining `BM_SETCHECK` was a provable
no-op, since `draw_lock_checkbox()` derives from `g_app.lockedVi`/`lockMode` and
the fan dialog from `g_fanCurveDialog.working.points[].enabled`. Two were hiding
real bugs: the profile projection that clears a lock
(`config_profiles_gui_state.cpp`) changed the model with no repaint at all, and
`fan_curve_dialog_sync_controls()` never repainted after a working-curve reload.
Two more repainted *before* flipping the model and worked only by accident of
`InvalidateRect` being asynchronous; those now repaint after.

Separately, the three right-hand controls used three different rules — GPU
selector at a 12 px inset, fan-curve button hardcoded at `dp(1006)` (so its gap
to the edge grew on every window wider than the base canvas), License button at
the correct 8 px. All three now go through
`main_layout_right_anchored_x()` against `MAIN_LAYOUT_SIDE_MARGIN_LOGICAL`, the
same inset the left gutter uses, each with an overlap floor against its left
neighbour.

Both new pure suites were mutation-verified: emulating the old `BM_GETCHECK`
gate exits 1652, and reinstating the 12 px inset exits 716. Source guards moved
to `tools/ui_gates.py` (`check_owner_draw_checkbox_repaint`,
`check_right_anchored_controls`), which also absorbed the older
`auto_profile_dialog.cpp` owner-draw guards so all of them live in one place and
`build.py` stays under its size ratchet. The forbid matches the *call* form
(`BM_GETCHECK,` with the trailing comma) so the comments explaining the rule can
stay next to the code. F-MAINT-1 splits: `main_runtime_ui.cpp` 819 -> 655 via
the new `ui_theme_button.cpp`, and `ui_main_window.cpp` 1288 -> 1224 by moving
the share-toggle handler next to the shared-state projection it re-runs in
`config_profiles_ui.cpp`.

**Not confirmed on screen.** An older instance from `C:\Program Files\greencurve`
holds the single-instance mutex on this machine, so the rebuilt binary could not
be launched to watch the tick clear. `python build.py` (six binaries, `-Werror`)
and `python build.py --test` both pass.

**Files:** `ui_checkbox_state.h`, `ui_control_projection.h`, `app_shared.h`,
`ui_theme_button.cpp` (new), `main_runtime_ui.cpp`, `config_profiles_ui.cpp`,
`config_profiles_gui_state.cpp`, `ui_main_window.cpp`, `ui_main.cpp`,
`ui_lock_checkbox.cpp`, `gui_service_state.cpp`, `fan_curve_dialog.cpp`,
`main_layout_policy.h`, `ui_main_layout.cpp`, `ui_main_controls.cpp`,
`tests/regression_main.cpp`, `tools/ui_gates.py`, `build.py`.
